## References

- Food Safety Authority of Ireland. “Starting a Food Business,” “Register Your Food Business,” and *Business Start-up Information*, current guidance accessed September 2026.
- Regulation (EC) No 852/2004 on the hygiene of foodstuffs, current consolidated text.
- Regulation (EU) No 1169/2011 on food information to consumers, current consolidated text.
- Directive (EU) 2024/1438 and Council Directive 2001/110/EC relating to honey, including origin-labelling rules applying from 14 June 2026.
- National tax, company-registration, employment, planning, animal-health, food-business, and insurance authorities for the target jurisdiction.
- Standard farm/business-management literature on cash flow, gross margin, break-even, depreciation, inventory, and risk management.
- Current local beekeeping associations and agricultural-extension resources for realistic regional production data.


<a id="chapter-61"></a>

# Chapter 61 — Equipment Costs

## Introduction

Equipment costs can make a beekeeping business look either deceptively cheap or unnecessarily expensive, depending on what is counted. A beginner may total the price of a hive, suit, smoker, and extractor and believe the investment is complete. A commercial operator knows that the true equipment system also includes replacement frames, spare boxes, storage, transport, food-grade containers, cleaning tools, scales, maintenance parts, cold-chain equipment for perishable products, labels, work surfaces, and enough surplus capacity to keep operating when something breaks at the busiest time of year.

The purpose of this chapter is not to publish a list of prices that will be obsolete next year. Instead, it teaches a cost model that can be filled with current local quotations in any country and updated annually.

Every purchase should answer four questions:

1. What production problem does the item solve?
2. What capacity does it add?
3. What is its full ownership cost over its useful life?
4. Is the business already producing enough margin to justify it?

The cheapest purchase is not always the lowest-cost choice. Equipment that breaks during harvest, cannot be cleaned properly, contaminates food, requires excessive labour, or does not match the hive system can be more expensive than a higher-quality item.

## Learning Objectives

After completing this chapter, the reader should be able to:

- distinguish capital expenditure, operating expense, inventory, and working capital;
- build a complete start-up equipment list by business model;
- calculate cost per colony and cost per unit of output;
- estimate useful life and annual depreciation for management purposes;
- compare buying new, buying used, renting, sharing, and outsourcing;
- identify hygiene, disease, compatibility, and safety risks in used equipment;
- size extraction, storage, bottling, cold-chain, and transport equipment to actual capacity needs;
- calculate total cost of ownership rather than purchase price alone;
- include maintenance, repairs, electricity, cleaning, calibration, insurance, interest, and replacement parts;
- use scenario analysis before investing in mechanisation;
- recognise when redundancy is economically justified;
- maintain an equipment register and replacement plan.

## 61.1 Price Lists Become Obsolete

Equipment prices change with:

- inflation;
- currency;
- shipping;
- material costs;
- tax;
- supplier;
- season;
- scale discounts.

The book therefore teaches cost structure and quotation methods instead of pretending one 2026 catalogue price is permanent.

## 61.2 Build the List From the Business Model

A honey-only operation needs different equipment from a business producing queens, pollen, or royal jelly.

Start with the product flow:

**colony → harvest → transport → processing → storage → packing → sale**

List the equipment required at each step.

## 61.3 Core Apiary Equipment

Typical core items include:

- hive bodies;
- floors/bottom boards;
- roofs;
- brood boxes;
- honey supers;
- frames;
- foundation or starter systems;
- queen excluders where used;
- feeders;
- hive stands;
- straps;
- entrance reducers;
- spare components.

## 61.4 Spares Are Part of the System

A business should not operate with exactly one component per hive.

Spare equipment is needed for:

- swarm capture;
- splits;
- damaged boxes;
- unexpected strong flows;
- disease isolation;
- queen failure;
- emergency feeding.

Budgeting only for equipment currently occupied by bees understates working capacity.

## 61.5 Frames and Foundation

Frames are a recurring cost because:

- comb is retired;
- frames break;
- new colonies are created;
- supers expand;
- disease controls can destroy comb.

Track cost separately for:

- frame components;
- foundation;
- wire/pins;
- labour to assemble.

## 61.6 Assembly Labour

A flat-pack box may be cheaper than an assembled one but requires labour and tools.

Include:

- assembly time;
- screws/nails;
- glue;
- paint/protective coating;
- workspace;
- rejects.

## 61.7 Protective Equipment

Budget for:

- suits/jackets;
- veils;
- gloves;
- boots;
- spare PPE for staff/visitors where required;
- washing/replacement.

PPE is not a one-time lifetime purchase.

## 61.8 Hand Tools

Common tools include:

- smokers;
- hive tools;
- frame lifters;
- brushes;
- queen clips/cages;
- grafting tools if relevant;
- torches;
- scales;
- marking equipment.

Small tools are easy to under-budget because they are purchased gradually.

## 61.9 Smoker Costs

A smoker requires:

- purchase;
- replacement bellows/guards;
- fuel;
- fire-safe transport/storage;
- cleaning.

Cheap smokers that fail repeatedly can waste inspection time.

## 61.10 Monitoring Equipment

Health and production monitoring may require:

- Varroa wash cups;
- alcohol/detergent supplies;
- sample jars;
- magnification;
- hive scales;
- temperature sensors;
- records/software.

Monitoring is a production-control cost, not an optional scientific hobby.

## 61.11 Feeding Equipment

Costs can include:

- feeders;
- syrup tanks;
- pumps;
- food-grade buckets;
- measuring devices;
- protective storage from rodents/ants.

Feed handling can become mechanised as colony count grows.

## 61.12 Queen-Rearing Equipment

A queen business can require:

- mating nuclei;
- queen cups;
- cell bars;
- incubators where used;
- cages;
- marking tools;
- transport boxes;
- grafting station;
- record system.

These costs belong to the queen enterprise, not automatically to honey production.

## 61.13 Pollen Equipment

Pollen production may require:

- traps;
- collection drawers;
- sorting screens;
- freezer;
- dryer or freeze-dryer;
- water-activity/moisture testing;
- suitable packaging.

The cold/dry chain can cost more than the trap itself.

## 61.14 Royal-Jelly Equipment

Royal-jelly production may require:

- cell cups/frames;
- grafting tools;
- collection tools/pumps;
- clean chilled containers;
- refrigeration/freezing;
- cold transport;
- specialised laboratory tests.

## 61.15 Propolis Equipment

Propolis production can require:

- traps;
- freezer/chiller;
- grinder;
- screens;
- solvent-compatible extraction equipment where lawful;
- fire-safe solvent storage;
- filtration;
- light-protective containers.

## 61.16 Bee-Venom Equipment

Venom production requires specialised professional collection equipment, electrical inspection, secure collection plates, PPE, controlled recovery, and laboratory analysis.

Do not price only the collector. Safety and testing can dominate the cost.

## 61.17 Honey Harvest Equipment

Harvest may require:

- escapes;
- blowers;
- brushes;
- super covers;
- food-grade trays;
- trolleys;
- lifting aids;
- transport pallets.

## 61.18 Extraction Room

Core equipment can include:

- uncapping tank;
- knives/forks;
- extractor;
- strainers;
- settling tanks;
- pumps;
- hoses;
- valves;
- food-grade buckets;
- scales;
- refractometer;
- warming cabinet/room where justified.

## 61.19 Extractor Size

Do not size the extractor only by hive count.

Consider:

- peak supers per harvest day;
- frame size;
- time available;
- labour;
- viscosity;
- future growth;
- cleaning time.

## 61.20 Throughput Calculation

A simple capacity estimate is:

`frames/hour = frames per load × loads per hour`

Then compare with expected peak frames.

Include:

- loading;
- unloading;
- uncapping;
- turning where relevant;
- cleaning/downtime.

## 61.21 The Extractor Is Not Always the Bottleneck

A large extractor can sit idle if uncapping cannot feed it quickly enough.

Likewise, fast extraction can overwhelm:

- strainers;
- tanks;
- pumps;
- staff.

Design the line as a system.

## 61.22 Manual Versus Motorised

Manual equipment costs less upfront but requires more labour.

Motorised equipment can reduce labour but adds:

- electricity;
- maintenance;
- controls;
- electrical safety;
- replacement motors/gears.

## 61.23 Bottling Equipment

Options range from:

- manual honey gates;
- bench fillers;
- piston/gear pumps;
- semi-automatic fillers;
- complete packing lines.

The correct investment depends on jars per hour and labour cost.

## 61.24 Packaging Equipment

Do not forget:

- scales;
- cap/lid tools;
- label applicators;
- date/lot coding;
- carton sealing;
- shelving;
- pallet wrap.

## 61.25 Storage Tanks

Tank capacity should allow:

- separation of lots;
- settling;
- temporary crop peaks;
- cleaning;
- future packing.

One enormous tank can reduce traceability flexibility.

## 61.26 Container Inventory

Packaging stock ties up cash.

Track:

- jars;
- lids;
- bottles;
- labels;
- cartons;
- inserts;
- tamper seals.

Buy enough for continuity without overstocking obsolete label designs.

## 61.27 Premises Costs

Equipment cost is affected by the building.

A honey room may need:

- washable surfaces;
- sinks;
- potable water;
- drainage;
- lighting;
- ventilation;
- electrical outlets;
- pest control;
- storage.

A cheap extractor in an unsuitable room does not create a compliant process.

## 61.28 Utilities

Budget for:

- electricity;
- water;
- hot water;
- refrigeration;
- dehumidification;
- heating;
- waste disposal.

## 61.29 Vehicle Costs

Transport can be one of the largest commercial costs.

Include:

- purchase/lease;
- depreciation;
- fuel;
- tyres;
- service;
- tax/registration;
- insurance;
- breakdown;
- cleaning;
- trailer.

## 61.30 Load Capacity

Hive bodies and honey are heavy.

Check:

- legal payload;
- axle limits;
- trailer limits;
- tie-down requirements;
- safe load distribution.

Do not plan using vehicle volume only.

## 61.31 Forklifts and Pallet Systems

Larger operations may use:

- forklift;
- loader;
- pallet jack;
- hive pallets.

Add:

- training;
- maintenance;
- charging/fuel;
- inspection;
- storage.

## 61.32 Digital Equipment

Business tools can include:

- laptop/tablet/phone;
- printer;
- label printer;
- barcode scanner;
- inventory software;
- accounting software;
- website/hosting.

## 61.33 Laboratory and Quality Tools

Routine in-house tools can include:

- refractometer;
- thermometers;
- calibrated scales;
- pH/conductivity tools where justified;
- data loggers.

Advanced authenticity/residue testing is usually more economical to outsource.

## 61.34 Calibration

Measuring tools require:

- calibration/check standards;
- records;
- replacement batteries;
- periodic professional calibration where required.

The purchase price is not the full metrology cost.

## 61.35 Capital Expenditure

Capital equipment provides service for more than one season.

Track each asset by:

- purchase date;
- purchase cost;
- useful life;
- expected salvage value;
- maintenance history.

## 61.36 Straight-Line Depreciation

A simple management estimate is:

`annual depreciation = (purchase cost − estimated salvage value) ÷ useful life`

Tax rules may use different allowances; consult current local tax guidance.

## 61.37 Total Cost of Ownership

TCO includes:

- purchase;
- finance interest;
- delivery;
- installation;
- maintenance;
- energy;
- cleaning;
- consumables;
- repairs;
- downtime;
- disposal/resale.

## 61.38 Cheap Equipment Can Be Expensive

A low-cost item can create high TCO through:

- short life;
- poor cleanability;
- slow throughput;
- unavailable spare parts;
- product damage;
- safety problems.

## 61.39 Expensive Equipment Can Also Be Wasteful

A large machine can destroy return on capital if used only a few hours per year.

Calculate utilisation before buying.

## 61.40 Capacity Utilisation

A simple measure is:

`utilisation = actual throughput ÷ practical available capacity`

Very low utilisation suggests overinvestment unless redundancy or future growth justifies it.

## 61.41 Labour-Saving Investment

Estimate annual labour saving:

`hours saved × true hourly labour cost`

Compare against annual ownership cost.

## 61.42 Payback Period

A simple payback estimate is:

`purchase cost ÷ annual net cash benefit`

It ignores time value of money but is useful for quick comparisons.

## 61.43 Net Present Value

Larger investments can be assessed using discounted cash flow/NPV.

This accounts for:

- timing of cash flows;
- required return;
- equipment life.

Use accounting/financial advice for significant borrowing.

## 61.44 Financing

Equipment may be funded through:

- retained earnings;
- loan;
- lease;
- grant;
- cooperative ownership.

Compare full cost and obligations.

## 61.45 Interest

Borrowed equipment has interest cost even when accounting depreciation is separate.

A machine that appears affordable at cash price can become expensive under poor finance terms.

## 61.46 Grants

Grants can reduce capital cost but may require:

- eligible equipment;
- pre-approval;
- quotes;
- records;
- matching funds;
- retention periods.

Do not purchase first and assume reimbursement later.

## 61.47 Buying Used Equipment

Used equipment can reduce capital costs.

Inspect:

- structural condition;
- food-contact surfaces;
- electrical safety;
- rust;
- seals/bearings;
- spare-part availability;
- contamination history.

## 61.48 Used Hive Equipment and Disease

Second-hand hive bodies and comb can carry disease risk.

Old brood comb can transmit:

- AFB spores;
- other pathogens;
- pests;
- residues.

Disease risk can outweigh purchase savings.

## 61.49 Used Food Equipment

Ask what product was previously processed.

Avoid equipment with unknown history involving:

- chemicals;
- non-food oils;
- allergens;
- corrosion;
- unsuitable metals.

## 61.50 Cleaning Cost Before Use

A used tank may need:

- dismantling;
- seals;
- polishing;
- food-grade hoses;
- professional electrical work.

Include refurbishment in purchase cost.

## 61.51 Renting

Renting can be economical for rarely used equipment.

Advantages:

- low capital requirement;
- no long-term storage;
- maintenance may be included.

Disadvantages:

- availability risk;
- transport;
- cleaning verification;
- scheduling.

## 61.52 Shared Honey Houses

Shared facilities can spread capital cost across producers.

Require rules for:

- booking;
- cleaning;
- lot segregation;
- liability;
- breakage;
- traceability.

## 61.53 Outsourcing Extraction

Contract extraction can postpone equipment investment.

Compare:

- price per super/kg;
- transport;
- turnaround;
- lot control;
- quality system;
- risk of mixing.

## 61.54 Outsourcing Packing

A contract packer can provide professional throughput without owning a filling line.

Requirements include:

- written specification;
- supply-chain traceability;
- packaging ownership;
- label approval;
- minimum run size.

## 61.55 Equipment Compatibility

Standardise hive dimensions where practical.

Mixed formats increase:

- spare inventory;
- frame incompatibility;
- extractor complexity;
- transport inefficiency.

## 61.56 Standardisation

Commercial efficiency improves when common items are standardised:

- box size;
- frame type;
- lid;
- pallet;
- jar family;
- labels;
- hoses/fittings.

## 61.57 Spare Parts

Keep critical spares for harvest equipment:

- belts;
- bearings;
- gaskets;
- valves;
- fuses;
- pump seals;
- controller components where user-serviceable.

## 61.58 Downtime Cost

A breakdown during a one-week harvest window can cost far more than the spare part.

Estimate downtime cost through:

- lost honey quality;
- extra labour;
- delayed harvest;
- rental/emergency repair.

## 61.59 Preventive Maintenance

Schedule maintenance before peak season.

Tasks can include:

- extractor inspection;
- pump service;
- vehicle service;
- freezer check;
- scale calibration;
- electrical inspection.

## 61.60 Equipment Register

Record:

- asset ID;
- description;
- supplier;
- serial number;
- purchase date;
- cost;
- location;
- maintenance;
- warranty;
- expected replacement.

## 61.61 Replacement Reserve

Set aside money for future replacement.

If an extractor has a 10-year useful life, the business should earn enough over those years to replace it without crisis borrowing.

## 61.62 Safety Equipment

Budget explicitly for:

- fire extinguisher appropriate to risk;
- first-aid kits;
- eye protection;
- heat gloves;
- guards;
- lifting aids;
- electrical protection.

Safety is part of equipment cost.

## 61.63 Fire Safety

Wax, ethanol, fuel, smokers, and electrical equipment create different fire risks.

Use appropriate professional fire assessment rather than buying one generic extinguisher and assuming every hazard is covered.

## 61.64 Manual Handling

Mechanisation may be justified by injury reduction even when direct labour payback is modest.

Consider:

- trolleys;
- lifts;
- smaller supers;
- pallet systems.

## 61.65 Cold-Chain Equipment

Perishable hive products may require:

- refrigerator;
- freezer;
- temperature data logger;
- insulated transport;
- backup power/alarm.

A freezer without monitoring is not a complete cold chain.

## 61.66 Backup Power

Backup can protect:

- frozen pollen;
- royal jelly;
- refrigerated products;
- digital inventory.

Compare generator, battery, alarm, or emergency-transfer options according to scale.

## 61.67 Water and Cleaning Equipment

Budget for:

- sinks;
- hoses;
- hot-water system;
- brushes;
- squeegees;
- food-safe detergents;
- drying racks;
- wastewater handling.

## 61.68 Pest Control Equipment

Facilities may need:

- insect screens;
- sealed doors;
- rodent-proof storage;
- monitoring traps;
- shelving.

Prevention costs less than contaminated product.

## 61.69 Signage and Labelling Equipment

Operational labels include:

- batch ID;
- HOLD/RELEASED status;
- chemical labels;
- equipment ID;
- allergen/product segregation.

A label printer may be a control tool, not merely marketing equipment.

## 61.70 Cost per Colony

Divide apiary-specific equipment cost by productive colony count carefully.

Do not dilute costs using colonies that are not actually productive without explaining the calculation.

## 61.71 Cost per Kilogram

For processing equipment:

`annual equipment cost ÷ annual kg processed`

can help compare options.

Annual equipment cost can include depreciation, maintenance, energy, and finance.

## 61.72 Cost per Jar

Packing cost per jar may include:

- jar;
- lid;
- label;
- tamper seal;
- carton;
- packing labour;
- filler depreciation;
- payment/fulfilment costs.

## 61.73 Make-or-Buy Analysis

Compare in-house processing with outsourcing using:

- variable cost;
- fixed cost;
- minimum volume;
- quality control;
- capacity;
- cash risk.

## 61.74 Break-Even Volume for a Machine

If owning a filler costs €A annually and outsourcing saves €B per jar, the rough break-even volume is:

`A ÷ B`

Use current local prices.

## 61.75 Currency Risk

Imported equipment prices can change with exchange rates.

Quotes should specify:

- currency;
- validity period;
- shipping;
- duties/taxes;
- delivery terms.

## 61.76 Shipping Cost

Bulky hive equipment can have high freight cost relative to item price.

Always compare landed cost, not catalogue price.

## 61.77 Import Duties and Compliance

Imported electrical/food-contact equipment may require:

- customs;
- product conformity;
- electrical safety;
- food-contact documentation.

Cheap non-compliant equipment can be unusable commercially.

## 61.78 VAT/Sales Tax

Whether a business can reclaim input tax depends on local tax registration and rules.

Budget using the amount the business will actually pay after applicable recovery—not assumptions.

## 61.79 Quotation Sheet

For major purchases compare at least:

- purchase price;
- freight;
- tax;
- installation;
- warranty;
- spare parts;
- energy;
- throughput;
- cleaning time;
- resale value.

## 61.80 Capacity Headroom

Buying exactly today's required capacity may force replacement after small growth.

Reasonable headroom can be economical.

Excessive headroom is idle capital.

## 61.81 Modular Growth

Modular systems can grow by adding:

- tanks;
- pump;
- filling head;
- freezer capacity;
- supers.

This can reduce large premature investments.

## 61.82 Purchase Timing

Buy equipment before it becomes an emergency but after the business case is proven.

Lead times can be months for specialised machinery.

## 61.83 Seasonal Discounts

Off-season buying may reduce cost, but avoid buying because of a discount alone.

Unused equipment at 30% discount is still wasted capital.

## 61.84 Warranties

Review:

- duration;
- commercial-use exclusions;
- shipping of parts;
- service location;
- electrical modifications that void warranty.

## 61.85 Resale Value

Standard equipment from established manufacturers may retain more value.

Custom equipment can have low resale value even if expensive to build.

## 61.86 Decision Table

| Situation | Cost interpretation | Response |
|---|---|---|
| Cheap extractor, slow and hard to clean | High labour/hygiene TCO | Compare full ownership cost |
| Large extractor used 5 hours/year | Low utilisation | Rent/share/outsource may be better |
| Repeated lifting injuries | Hidden labour/safety cost | Evaluate mechanical aids |
| Old brood equipment offered cheaply | Disease/residue risk | Reject or only use under validated control |
| Cold product expands faster than freezer space | Capacity bottleneck | Add cold capacity before more production |
| Packaging stock fills warehouse | Cash tied in inventory | Reduce order size/standardise packs |
| One pump seal can stop harvest | Downtime risk | Stock critical spare |

## 61.87 Practical Costing Worksheet

For every major item record:

- item;
- required capacity;
- purchase price;
- freight/tax;
- installation;
- useful life;
- annual maintenance;
- energy/consumables;
- annual utilisation;
- labour saving;
- expected resale value;
- financing cost;
- critical-spare cost.

Then calculate annual ownership cost and cost per unit processed.

## 61.88 Start-Up Equipment Checklist

- [ ] Business model defined.
- [ ] Hive standard selected.
- [ ] Spare boxes/frames budgeted.
- [ ] PPE and tools budgeted.
- [ ] Health-monitoring equipment included.
- [ ] Feed handling included.
- [ ] Harvest covers/lifting aids included.
- [ ] Extraction line capacity matched end to end.
- [ ] Storage tanks sized by lot needs.
- [ ] Packaging inventory budgeted.
- [ ] Food-room infrastructure included.
- [ ] Vehicle payload checked.
- [ ] Cold chain included for perishable products.
- [ ] Calibration/quality tools budgeted.
- [ ] Safety/fire equipment budgeted.
- [ ] Maintenance/spares included.
- [ ] Used-equipment contamination/disease risk checked.
- [ ] Outsourcing/rental comparison completed.
- [ ] Full landed/TCO cost calculated.
- [ ] Replacement reserve included.

## Key Takeaways

- Equipment should be costed as a system, not as isolated catalogue items.
- Current local quotations are more useful than static prices in a book.
- Spare capacity and spare parts are part of commercial reliability.
- Purchase price is only one component of total cost of ownership.
- Owner labour, cleaning time, energy, maintenance, downtime, and finance can dominate equipment economics.
- Used hive equipment can carry disease and residue risks that exceed the purchase saving.
- Used food equipment requires known previous use and hygienic refurbishment.
- Extraction capacity must be balanced with uncapping, transfer, tanks, and packing.
- Mechanisation should solve a proven bottleneck or safety problem.
- A replacement reserve prevents equipment ageing from becoming a future cash crisis.

## Chapter Summary

Commercial beekeeping equipment should be purchased from a capacity plan rather than a shopping list. Begin with the business model, map the production flow, and identify the true bottleneck. A large extractor cannot solve slow uncapping, inadequate tanks, or weak sales.

The correct comparison is total cost of ownership. Purchase price, freight, tax, finance, energy, labour, cleaning, maintenance, downtime, calibration, and resale value all influence the economic result. Used or shared equipment can reduce capital, but disease, contamination, scheduling, and traceability risks must be included.

As the operation grows, safety and resilience become increasingly important. Lifting aids, fire controls, electrical protection, cold-chain monitoring, and critical spares may not directly increase honey yield, but they prevent injury and catastrophic downtime.

A disciplined equipment register, maintenance schedule, and replacement reserve turn machinery from emergency purchases into planned productive assets.

## Review Questions

1. Why does this chapter avoid a fixed equipment price list?
2. What four questions should every purchase answer?
3. Why should the equipment list start from the business model?
4. Name six core hive-equipment categories.
5. Why are spare boxes part of productive capacity?
6. Why are frames a recurring cost?
7. Which assembly costs are often overlooked?
8. Why is PPE not a lifetime purchase?
9. Which small hand tools are easily under-budgeted?
10. What are the ongoing costs of a smoker?
11. Why is monitoring equipment a production-control cost?
12. Which costs occur in feed handling?
13. Which specialised items belong to queen production?
14. Why can pollen cold-chain equipment cost more than the trap?
15. What equipment is specific to royal jelly?
16. What equipment is needed for propolis processing?
17. Why is a venom collector only part of venom-production cost?
18. Which harvest tools should be budgeted?
19. Name eight extraction-room equipment categories.
20. How should extractor size be chosen?
21. How is frames-per-hour estimated?
22. Why can uncapping be the real bottleneck?
23. What are the trade-offs of manual equipment?
24. What costs come with motorisation?
25. Which bottling options exist?
26. Which packaging tools are often forgotten?
27. Why can one large tank reduce traceability flexibility?
28. Why does packaging inventory tie up cash?
29. Which building upgrades can be part of equipment investment?
30. Which utilities should be budgeted?
31. What vehicle costs belong in TCO?
32. Why is legal payload more important than cargo volume alone?
33. Which extra costs accompany forklifts?
34. What digital tools can a business need?
35. Which quality instruments are useful in-house?
36. Why does calibration have a cost?
37. What information should be recorded for capital assets?
38. How is straight-line depreciation estimated?
39. What is total cost of ownership?
40. Why can cheap equipment have high TCO?
41. Why can expensive high-capacity equipment be wasteful?
42. What is capacity utilisation?
43. How can labour savings be valued?
44. What is a payback period?
45. What does NPV add to a major investment decision?
46. Which financing methods can be used?
47. Why is interest separate from depreciation?
48. What conditions can accompany grants?
49. What should be inspected on used equipment?
50. Why is second-hand brood comb particularly risky?
51. Why does previous use matter for a used food tank?
52. What refurbishment costs can occur?
53. When can renting be economical?
54. What risks accompany shared honey houses?
55. What should be compared when outsourcing extraction?
56. What should a contract packer agreement control?
57. Why is equipment compatibility important?
58. What can be standardised across an operation?
59. Which critical spares can be kept?
60. What is downtime cost?
61. Why should maintenance be scheduled before harvest?
62. What belongs in an equipment register?
63. What is a replacement reserve?
64. Which safety equipment should be budgeted explicitly?
65. Why can mechanisation be justified by injury reduction?
66. What does a complete cold chain include?
67. Why might backup power be necessary?
68. Which cleaning equipment has to be budgeted?
69. What physical pest-control infrastructure can be needed?
70. Why can a label printer be an operational control tool?
71. How can equipment cost per colony be misleading?
72. How can annual equipment cost per kg be calculated?
73. What belongs in cost per jar?
74. What is make-or-buy analysis?
75. How can machine break-even volume be estimated?
76. Why does currency risk matter for imports?
77. What is landed cost?
78. Which compliance risks can accompany imported equipment?
79. How can VAT/sales-tax treatment affect budget cost?
80. Which fields should be compared on a quotation sheet?
81. What is capacity headroom?
82. Why can excessive headroom be wasteful?
83. What is modular growth?
84. When should equipment be ordered?
85. Why can seasonal discounts create bad purchases?
86. What should be checked in a warranty?
87. Why can resale value matter?
88. What does a cheap hard-to-clean extractor illustrate?
89. What should be considered when an extractor is used only a few hours annually?
90. How can lifting injury alter equipment economics?
91. Why might cheap old brood boxes be a bad investment?
92. What should happen before increasing pollen production beyond freezer capacity?
93. How can overstocked jars harm cash flow?
94. Why are critical spare parts valuable?
95. Which fields belong in a major-item costing worksheet?
96. Why must food-room infrastructure appear in the equipment budget?
97. Why should full ownership cost be calculated before buying?
98. Why should equipment capacity be evaluated end-to-end?
99. What prevents equipment ageing from creating a future financial shock?
100. What combination of capacity, TCO, safety, hygiene, and utilisation should determine a commercial beekeeping equipment purchase?

## References

- Current supplier quotations and local beekeeping-equipment catalogues should be used for prices at the time of purchase rather than static book figures.
- Standard farm-business and engineering-economics references for depreciation, total cost of ownership, payback, NPV, asset utilisation, and maintenance planning.
- Food-contact-material, electrical-safety, machinery-safety, occupational-safety, vehicle-payload, fire-safety, and food-premises requirements for the target jurisdiction.
- Chapter 60 — Starting a Beekeeping Business, and Chapters 47–59 for production-specific equipment requirements.
- National tax and grant authorities for current depreciation/capital-allowance, VAT/sales-tax, and grant rules.


<a id="chapter-62"></a>

# Chapter 62 — Marketing

## Introduction

Marketing is the system used to understand customers, shape products for real demand, communicate truthful value, choose sales channels, and build repeat purchasing. Advertising is only one part of that system.

A beekeeper can produce excellent honey and still build a weak business if customers do not understand why the product is different, cannot find it easily, do not trust the claims, or never return for a second purchase. The opposite problem is equally dangerous: aggressive marketing can create demand that the operation cannot supply consistently or can encourage claims that exceed the evidence or the law.

Honey marketing has an unusual foundation: provenance matters. Customers often care about where honey was harvested, which floral source dominates, who produced it, how it tastes, and how it differs from anonymous blends. These qualities can create value, but only when they are supported by traceability and honest product descriptions.

Current EU rules make this especially important. From 14 June 2026, blends of honey marketed in the EU generally must identify the countries of origin in descending order by weight and include each country's percentage contribution, subject to specified rules. Floral or vegetable-origin descriptions are allowed only when the honey comes wholly or mainly from the stated source and has the corresponding organoleptic, physicochemical, and microscopic characteristics. In Ireland, current FSAI guidance also states that there are no authorised health claims for honey; non-authorised claims cannot be used on labels, packaging, websites, menus, or advertising, and food information must not attribute disease-preventing, treating, or curing properties to honey.

Marketing therefore begins with evidence. A credible story is more valuable than an exaggerated one.

## Learning Objectives

After completing this chapter, the reader should be able to:

- distinguish marketing, advertising, sales, branding, and promotion;
- identify useful customer segments from actual buying behaviour;
- define a clear value proposition without unsupported medical or environmental claims;
- position honey and other hive products by origin, sensory quality, convenience, format, service, and trust;
- design a product portfolio and pack-size strategy;
- compare direct retail, farmers markets, online, wholesale, food-service, gift, and B2B channels;
- price and promote products without undermining margin or customer trust;
- support floral, geographical, local, organic, raw, and sustainability claims with appropriate evidence;
- understand current EU honey-origin and health-claim constraints as jurisdiction-specific examples;
- build repeat purchasing through customer service, email, content, sampling, and reliable fulfilment;
- measure conversion, average order value, customer acquisition cost, repeat rate, channel margin, stock turn, and sell-through;
- create a practical marketing plan tied to inventory and production capacity.

## 62.1 Marketing Is More Than Promotion

Marketing includes:

- customer research;
- product design;
- positioning;
- pricing;
- packaging;
- distribution;
- communication;
- sales support;
- customer retention;
- measurement.

Promotion is only the communication layer.

## 62.2 Sales and Marketing Are Different

Marketing creates and communicates an offer that customers want.

Sales converts specific prospects into transactions.

A market stall conversation is sales. Choosing which jar sizes to bring, what prices to use, and which customer group the stall serves is marketing.

## 62.3 Branding Is Also Different

Branding creates recognisable identity and meaning around the business.

Chapter 63 covers branding in detail.

Marketing uses that brand in the wider system of product, price, place, promotion, and customer experience.

## 62.4 Start With Customer Behaviour

A useful segment is defined by meaningful differences in buying behaviour or needs.

Examples can include:

- local households buying everyday honey;
- gift buyers;
- premium varietal-honey enthusiasts;
- restaurants/cafés;
- independent food retailers;
- tourists;
- online repeat customers;
- beekeepers buying queens/nuclei;
- growers buying pollination services.

Do not define segments only by vague demographic stereotypes.

## 62.5 Customer Problem or Desire

Ask what the customer is actually seeking.

Possible needs include:

- dependable everyday sweetener;
- local provenance;
- distinctive flavour;
- premium gift;
- traceable origin;
- convenient squeeze packaging;
- reliable wholesale supply;
- documented colony strength for pollination;
- locally adapted queen genetics.

Marketing should connect the real product to the real need.

## 62.6 Customer Interviews

Useful questions include:

- Which honey do you currently buy?
- Where do you buy it?
- What makes you switch brands?
- Which jar size do you finish?
- What makes a product feel premium?
- Which information on the label matters?
- What price difference would change your choice?

Avoid leading questions such as “Would you buy my amazing local honey?”

## 62.7 Observe Purchases, Not Compliments

People may praise a product and still not buy it.

Stronger evidence includes:

- actual purchase;
- repeat purchase;
- pre-order;
- email signup followed by purchase;
- wholesale reorder.

## 62.8 Market Size

Estimate demand through:

- local population;
- retail outlets;
- market footfall;
- current category prices;
- competitor stock movement;
- online search and sales data where available;
- own test sales.

Avoid multiplying population by an unrealistic assumed annual consumption.

## 62.9 Competitor Analysis

Study comparable products for:

- size;
- price;
- origin;
- packaging;
- channel;
- claims;
- reviews;
- visual presentation;
- availability.

The goal is not to copy. It is to understand the choices customers already have.

## 62.10 Direct Competitors

Direct competitors sell a similar product to a similar customer.

Examples:

- another local beekeeper selling 340 g jars at the same market;
- a premium Irish varietal honey online.

## 62.11 Indirect Competitors

Indirect competitors solve the same customer need differently.

For a breakfast sweetener, competition can include:

- jam;
- maple syrup;
- sugar spreads;
- other sweeteners.

For a gift, competition can include chocolates, wine, preserves, or candles.

## 62.12 Value Proposition

A value proposition explains why a customer should choose the product.

Strong examples are specific:

- single-apiary seasonal honey with documented origin;
- small-batch comb honey from fresh broodless comb;
- dependable wholesale case supply with full traceability;
- locally adapted overwintered nuclei with health records.

Weak propositions use empty words such as “premium quality” without evidence.

## 62.13 Differentiation

Differentiation can come from:

- verified local origin;
- floral origin;
- sensory profile;
- packaging format;
- customer service;
- availability;
- story/provenance;
- certification;
- traceability;
- specialist product expertise.

## 62.14 Differentiation Must Be Defensible

Do not claim:

- “chemical-free” when no evidence supports it;
- “organic” without certification where certification is required;
- “raw” as a medical benefit;
- “local” when product is blended with imported honey;
- a monofloral origin without supporting evidence.

## 62.15 Product Positioning

Positioning is the place the product aims to occupy in the customer's mind.

Possible positions include:

- everyday local honey;
- premium gift honey;
- rare varietal honey;
- sustainable refill product;
- specialist comb honey;
- dependable B2B supply.

Trying to occupy every position at once creates confusion.

## 62.16 Product Portfolio

A portfolio can include:

- standard liquid honey;
- creamed honey;
- comb honey;
- limited seasonal varietal honey;
- wax products;
- gift sets.

Keep the range small enough to manage inventory and labels efficiently.

## 62.17 Too Many SKUs

Every SKU can require:

- separate stock;
- label;
- packaging;
- price;
- photograph;
- website listing;
- sales history.

A large product range can hide low-selling inventory.

## 62.18 Pack Size Strategy

Different pack sizes serve different needs.

Small packs can support:

- gifts;
- tasting;
- premium trial;
- travel.

Large packs can support:

- households;
- value buyers;
- food-service.

## 62.19 Price Architecture

A range can use price steps that make differences clear.

For example:

- standard local honey;
- limited varietal honey;
- comb honey/gift product.

Do not create artificial tiers with identical product and unsupported claims.

## 62.20 Cost Sets a Floor, Value Influences the Ceiling

Chapter 60 covers unit economics.

Marketing adds the customer's willingness to pay.

A price below sustainable cost is not a marketing strategy; it is a loss.

## 62.21 Premium Pricing

Premium pricing requires reasons customers can understand and trust.

Possible supporting factors include:

- scarce floral crop;
- exceptional sensory profile;
- verified origin;
- difficult comb-honey production;
- certified product;
- high-quality gift packaging.

## 62.22 Discounting

Discounts can increase trial or clear stock, but frequent discounts can teach customers to wait.

Track whether the discount increases:

- new customers;
- repeat purchase;
- total margin;

not only unit sales.

## 62.23 Bundles

Bundles can combine:

- several flavours/origins;
- honey and wax candle;
- gift packaging;
- small tasting jars.

Bundle pricing should preserve margin and truthful product identity.

## 62.24 Direct Retail

Advantages:

- highest control over presentation;
- direct feedback;
- full customer relationship;
- potentially higher gross margin.

Costs include:

- selling time;
- retail systems;
- payment fees;
- customer service.

## 62.25 Wholesale

Wholesale customers may include:

- independent grocers;
- farm shops;
- speciality food stores;
- hotels;
- cafés;
- gift shops.

They need reliable margins, case sizes, lead times, and supply.

## 62.26 Retailer Margin

A retailer must earn a margin too.

If the producer's retail price leaves no room for a retailer, wholesale expansion becomes difficult.

Design channel pricing before approaching stores.

## 62.27 Wholesale Sell Sheet

A simple sell sheet can include:

- product image;
- pack size;
- case quantity;
- wholesale price;
- suggested retail price where appropriate;
- origin;
- shelf life/best-before approach;
- lead time;
- ordering contact;
- legal product information.

## 62.28 Samples for Buyers

Samples should represent the product actually supplied.

Do not send a premium seasonal sample and later substitute a different blend without agreement.

Maintain sample lot identity.

## 62.29 Food-Service Customers

Restaurants and cafés may value:

- larger packs;
- predictable supply;
- invoices;
- consistent flavour;
- delivery reliability.

Retail-style gift packaging may be irrelevant to them.

## 62.30 Farmers Markets

Markets combine sales and research.

They allow observation of:

- questions customers ask;
- pack sizes picked up;
- tasting response;
- price resistance;
- repeat customers.

Chapter 65 covers market operations in detail.

## 62.31 Online Channels

Online can include:

- own website;
- marketplace;
- social-commerce tool;
- subscription/repeat-order system.

Chapter 64 covers e-commerce operations and legal requirements.

## 62.32 Distance-Selling Food Information

Under current EU food-information rules, mandatory food information for prepacked food offered by distance selling—except the date of minimum durability/use-by particular—must generally be available before the purchase is concluded, and all mandatory particulars must be available at delivery.

This affects product-page design, not only the physical jar label.

## 62.33 Local Delivery

Local delivery can strengthen direct sales but has real cost.

Track:

- kilometres;
- driver time;
- failed delivery;
- packaging;
- minimum order.

Free delivery can destroy margin on small orders.

## 62.34 Subscription or Repeat Orders

Honey is naturally suited to repeat purchasing when consumption is regular.

A repeat-order programme can improve:

- forecast demand;
- customer lifetime value;
- retention.

Do not overcomplicate the subscription before demand exists.

## 62.35 Seasonal Demand

Sales often increase around:

- gifting periods;
- tourism season;
- winter food shopping;
- local events.

Build inventory and packaging plans before peak demand.

## 62.36 Gift Products

Gift buyers value:

- attractive presentation;
- clear origin;
- story;
- easy choice;
- reliable shipping.

Gift packaging should not create excessive cost or misleading luxury claims.

## 62.37 Sampling and Tastings

Tasting can convert uncertain buyers.

Use food-safe sampling practices:

- clean utensils;
- single-use portions where appropriate;
- allergen/ingredient information;
- protected food;
- hand hygiene.

## 62.38 Sensory Vocabulary

Useful descriptions are specific and non-medical:

- floral;
- malty;
- caramel-like;
- herbal;
- citrus-like;
- mild;
- robust;
- creamy;
- fine-crystallised.

Avoid inventing medicinal effects from flavour.

## 62.39 Storytelling

A strong product story can include:

- apiary location;
- flowering season;
- beekeeper process;
- harvest conditions;
- traceability;
- family/business history.

Keep every factual statement true and documentable.

## 62.40 Origin Is a Marketing Asset

Country, region, apiary, and floral source can create value.

But the stronger the origin claim, the stronger the evidence required.

Traceability converts provenance from a story into a verifiable claim.

## 62.41 Current EU Honey-Origin Rules

For honey blends placed on the EU market under the rules applying from 14 June 2026, countries of origin generally must appear in the principal field of vision, in descending order by weight, together with their percentage share, subject to the Directive's tolerances and limited Member State options.

A marketing team cannot invent origin percentages after packing; processing records must support them.

## 62.42 Floral-Origin Claims

Under the EU honey framework, a floral or vegetable-origin description can be used where the honey comes wholly or mainly from the named source and has the corresponding organoleptic, physicochemical, and microscopic characteristics.

Visual observation of flowers around the apiary alone is insufficient evidence.

## 62.43 Regional-Origin Claims

EU rules permit regional, territorial, or topographical origin information where the honey comes entirely from the indicated origin.

Blended origins require truthful labelling.

## 62.44 “Local” Claims

“Local” can mean different things to customers.

Define what it means operationally:

- harvested within a county;
- harvested within a stated radius;
- packed locally from imported honey;
- produced and packed locally.

Do not use ambiguity to create a false impression.

## 62.45 “Raw” Claims

“Raw honey” is not defined identically in every jurisdiction.

If used, the business should define its processing practice accurately and avoid implying medically superior effects that are not authorised.

## 62.46 Organic Claims

Organic beekeeping has specific legal/certification rules in regulated markets.

Do not market honey as organic merely because no synthetic treatment was used that season.

Chapter 70 covers organic beekeeping.

## 62.47 Sustainability Claims

Claims such as:

- sustainable;
- bee-friendly;
- regenerative;
- plastic-free;
- carbon-neutral

need clear definitions and evidence.

Avoid vague environmental marketing that cannot be substantiated.

## 62.48 Health Claims

Food health claims are regulated.

In Ireland/EU, current FSAI guidance states that there are no authorised health claims for honey.

Non-authorised claims cannot be used in commercial communications including labels, websites, menus, or advertising.

## 62.49 Disease Claims

Food information must not claim or imply that honey prevents, treats, or cures human disease.

Do not market honey as a treatment for:

- infection;
- cancer;
- allergy;
- diabetes;
- wounds

unless the product has an entirely different authorised regulatory status that permits the claim.

## 62.50 Nutrition Claims

Nutrition claims such as “source of” or “high in” nutrients have defined legal conditions in regulated markets.

Natural presence of a nutrient does not automatically permit the claim.

## 62.51 Research References in Advertising

Citing a study does not automatically make an unauthorised claim lawful.

A marketing sentence can imply treatment even if every word is technically linked to a paper.

Review the overall impression to the consumer.

## 62.52 Testimonials

Customer testimonials can create compliance risk if they make unauthorised medical claims.

A business should not republish “this honey cured my condition” as advertising simply because a customer wrote it.

## 62.53 Influencers and Affiliates

Paid or incentivised promotion should be transparently disclosed under applicable advertising law.

Provide promoters with approved factual product information and prohibited-claim guidance.

## 62.54 Photography

Good product photography should show the real product accurately.

Useful images include:

- clear jar front;
- texture;
- comb detail;
- apiary/context;
- scale/pack size.

Do not digitally alter colour so far that the delivered honey looks materially different.

## 62.55 Video

Video can demonstrate:

- extraction;
- creamed-honey texture;
- comb cutting;
- apiary environment;
- packaging.

Protect food hygiene and worker safety while filming.

## 62.56 Content Marketing

Useful educational topics include:

- why honey crystallises;
- how floral sources differ;
- how to store honey;
- comb honey use;
- what traceability means;
- seasonal apiary work.

Education builds trust when it remains accurate.

## 62.57 Search Visibility

Website content should use the words customers actually search for, such as:

- local honey;
- comb honey;
- Irish honey;
- honey gift set;
- varietal honey.

Do not stuff pages with repetitive keywords that reduce readability.

## 62.58 Local Search

Keep business information consistent across relevant listings:

- name;
- address/service area;
- opening hours;
- contact details.

Incorrect information damages trust and wastes visits.

## 62.59 Reviews

Reviews can improve trust.

Ask satisfied customers for genuine reviews but do not:

- fabricate reviews;
- buy fake ratings;
- hide legitimate complaints dishonestly.

## 62.60 Responding to Negative Reviews

Respond with:

- facts;
- courtesy;
- solution where appropriate.

Do not disclose private customer data in a public response.

## 62.61 Email Marketing

Email can support repeat sales through:

- harvest announcements;
- restock alerts;
- market dates;
- educational content;
- gift launches.

Collect and use personal data according to applicable privacy and electronic-marketing rules.

## 62.62 Permission and Privacy

Do not add customers to marketing lists merely because you have their email from an order unless local law permits that use and required conditions are met.

Provide appropriate privacy information and unsubscribe mechanisms.

## 62.63 Customer Relationship Management

Even a simple CRM can track:

- wholesale contacts;
- order history;
- follow-up dates;
- preferences;
- consent status.

Do not collect more personal data than the business needs.

## 62.64 Repeat Rate

Repeat purchase rate indicates whether customers return.

A simple version is:

`repeat customers ÷ total customers in the cohort`

Define the time period consistently.

## 62.65 Average Order Value

`AOV = total order revenue ÷ number of orders`

AOV can improve through:

- bundles;
- larger jars;
- gifts;
- complementary products.

Do not increase AOV by pushing unsuitable products.

## 62.66 Conversion Rate

Conversion rate can mean:

- website purchasers ÷ visitors;
- buyers ÷ market-stall interactions;
- wholesale accounts opened ÷ qualified leads.

Always define the denominator.

## 62.67 Customer Acquisition Cost

A simplified CAC is:

`marketing and sales acquisition cost ÷ new customers acquired`

Include advertising and relevant labour when possible.

## 62.68 Customer Lifetime Value

CLV estimates the long-term gross profit contribution of a customer relationship.

Do not use revenue alone as lifetime value.

## 62.69 Channel Margin

Compare channels after channel-specific costs.

For example, online sales can have high retail price but also:

- advertising;
- payment fees;
- shipping subsidy;
- packaging;
- returns.

## 62.70 Sell-Through Rate

Sell-through compares units sold with units made available during a period.

It helps identify:

- slow SKUs;
- wrong pack sizes;
- overproduction.

## 62.71 Stock Turn

Stock turn measures how quickly inventory sells and is replaced.

Slow inventory ties up cash and can age on the shelf.

## 62.72 Marketing Return

Do not judge marketing by likes alone.

Measure:

- sales;
- gross profit;
- new customers;
- repeat customers;
- qualified wholesale leads.

## 62.73 Attribution Is Imperfect

A customer may see a market stall, social post, review, and email before buying.

Do not expect perfect attribution.

Use practical indicators and controlled tests.

## 62.74 A/B Testing

Where volume permits, test one variable at a time:

- product image;
- headline;
- bundle;
- call to action.

Do not run complex tests on tiny samples and treat random variation as certainty.

## 62.75 Promotion Calendar

Plan promotions around:

- actual stock;
- harvest availability;
- gifting periods;
- market events;
- production capacity.

Do not advertise a product that cannot be fulfilled reliably.

## 62.76 Limited Editions

A genuine limited floral harvest can create urgency.

Use scarcity claims only when true.

Fake “only 5 left” messages damage trust and can breach consumer law.

## 62.77 Pre-Orders

Pre-orders can test demand and improve cash planning.

State clearly:

- expected fulfilment date;
- refund/cancellation terms;
- quantity limits;
- what happens if crop fails.

Biological crops are uncertain; promises should reflect that.

## 62.78 Out-of-Stock Management

Use out-of-stock periods to:

- collect restock interest;
- offer a truthful alternative;
- explain seasonal supply.

Do not quietly substitute a different origin or blend.

## 62.79 Complaint Handling

Complaints provide quality data.

Record:

- lot;
- issue;
- purchase channel;
- customer evidence;
- resolution.

Repeated complaints can reveal a process failure.

## 62.80 Refunds and Replacement

A clear policy can preserve trust.

Investigate food-safety complaints separately from ordinary preference issues.

Do not resell returned food automatically.

## 62.81 Crisis Communication

If there is a recall or labelling problem:

- communicate quickly;
- identify affected lots;
- state what customers should do;
- avoid speculation;
- update when verified facts change.

Trust is protected by accuracy, not defensiveness.

## 62.82 Wholesale Lead Generation

Potential wholesale outreach can include:

- targeted email;
- sample pack;
- trade fair;
- local food network;
- direct visit by appointment.

Research the buyer first.

## 62.83 Qualifying a Wholesale Lead

Ask:

- Does the shop fit the product?
- What volume can it sell?
- Who decides?
- What margin does it require?
- What delivery schedule is needed?
- Are there listing fees?

## 62.84 Retailer Reorders

A first wholesale order is not success if it never reorders.

Track:

- reorder interval;
- units/store/week;
- returned/damaged units;
- payment timing.

## 62.85 B2B Pollination Marketing

Pollination marketing should emphasise:

- colony strength;
- delivery reliability;
- health records;
- communication;
- contract terms.

Do not market pollination based only on hive count.

## 62.86 B2B Queen/Nucleus Marketing

Useful differentiators include:

- queen lineage;
- mating information;
- health status;
- overwintering status;
- pickup/shipping conditions;
- replacement policy.

Avoid unsupported claims of “mite-proof” or guaranteed performance.

## 62.87 Marketing Budget

A budget can include:

- packaging/design;
- photography;
- website;
- advertising;
- market fees;
- samples;
- printed material;
- promotional labour.

## 62.88 Budget as a Percentage of Sales Is Not Universal

A new brand may require more acquisition spending than an established direct-sales business.

Use expected customer economics rather than copying one industry percentage blindly.

## 62.89 90-Day Marketing Test

A practical test can include:

### Month 1 — Foundation

- define target customer;
- refine product/pack sizes;
- create compliant product information;
- establish baseline metrics.

### Month 2 — Channel Tests

- test one direct channel;
- test one repeat-customer channel;
- contact selected wholesale leads.

### Month 3 — Measure

- compare channel margins;
- repeat rate;
- AOV;
- conversion;
- customer feedback;
- unsold stock.

Then keep, change, or stop each activity.

## 62.90 Marketing Decision Table

| Observation | Likely issue | Response |
|---|---|---|
| Many compliments, few purchases | Weak value/price/channel fit | Test offer, price, pack size and target customer |
| High sales, low margin | Pricing/channel-cost problem | Recalculate channel margin before scaling |
| First orders, no repeat | Product/experience mismatch | Investigate quality, use case and follow-up |
| Premium claim cannot be documented | Trust/legal risk | Remove or gather evidence |
| Website visitors abandon product page | Information/trust/price problem | Improve mandatory info, images, proof and checkout clarity |
| One SKU rarely sells | Portfolio complexity | Reduce, reposition or discontinue |
| Wholesale account orders once | Poor store sell-through | Review retailer fit and shelf support |
| Medical claim drives engagement | Compliance/reputation risk | Remove unauthorised claim even if it attracts clicks |

## 62.91 Marketing Plan Template

For each target segment record:

- customer need;
- product;
- evidence-backed value proposition;
- price;
- channel;
- core message;
- proof points;
- promotion activity;
- inventory available;
- target metric;
- review date.

## 62.92 Scientific and Management Notes

### Origin reduces information uncertainty

Traceable origin can function as a trust signal because customers cannot personally verify every invisible characteristic of food. Strong documentation therefore supports marketing as well as compliance.

### Choice architecture matters

Too many similar products can make decisions harder. A small clear range can outperform a confusing large one.

### Repeat behaviour is stronger evidence than stated preference

Customers may say they value local or premium products, but repeated paid purchases reveal actual willingness to pay.

### Marketing and operations are coupled

Successful promotion can become an operational problem when stock, packing, or customer service cannot keep up. Marketing capacity should therefore match fulfilment capacity.

## 62.93 Marketing Checklist

- [ ] Target customers defined from behaviour/need.
- [ ] Competitors and alternatives researched.
- [ ] Value proposition specific and defensible.
- [ ] Product range manageable.
- [ ] Pack sizes tied to use cases.
- [ ] Prices cover sustainable economics.
- [ ] Channel-specific margin calculated.
- [ ] Origin claims traceable.
- [ ] Floral claims supported.
- [ ] “Local”, “raw”, “organic”, and sustainability wording reviewed.
- [ ] No unauthorised health/disease claims.
- [ ] Online mandatory food information available where required.
- [ ] Product photography accurate.
- [ ] Sampling hygienic.
- [ ] Wholesale materials show practical ordering information.
- [ ] Email/privacy permissions controlled.
- [ ] Conversion/AOV/CAC/repeat rate tracked where useful.
- [ ] Complaint data linked to lots.
- [ ] Promotions matched to real stock.
- [ ] Marketing activities reviewed by profit and retention, not likes alone.

## Key Takeaways

- Marketing is customer research, offer design, communication, channel selection, and retention—not only advertising.
- Paid repeat purchasing is stronger evidence than compliments.
- A good value proposition is specific and supported by real product evidence.
- Origin and floral-source claims can create value only when traceability supports them.
- EU honey-origin rules applying from 14 June 2026 make accurate blend provenance visible to consumers.
- Current Irish/EU guidance does not authorise health claims for honey, and disease-treatment claims are prohibited for ordinary food marketing.
- Direct, wholesale, market, and online channels have different hidden costs and margins.
- Customer acquisition cost, average order value, repeat rate, channel margin, and sell-through are more useful than social-media likes alone.
- Marketing should not create demand beyond safe production and fulfilment capacity.
- Trust compounds when claims, product quality, origin, service, and records all agree.

## Chapter Summary

Beekeeping marketing works best when it begins with real customer behaviour and real product evidence. The business identifies who buys, what problem or desire the product serves, why its origin or sensory qualities matter, and which channel delivers the product at a sustainable margin.

Honey is especially suited to provenance-based marketing, but provenance must be documented. Current EU rules make country percentages in blended honey visible to consumers and impose conditions on floral and regional origin descriptions. Health marketing is even more constrained: ordinary honey cannot be advertised as a disease treatment, and current Irish guidance reports no authorised health claims for honey.

A professional marketing system therefore relies on truthful differentiation—origin, flavour, format, service, traceability, seasonal scarcity, or certified characteristics—rather than exaggerated wellness promises. It also measures performance by commercial outcomes such as conversion, repeat purchase, average order value, customer acquisition cost, channel margin, and retailer reorders.

Marketing succeeds when it strengthens the complete business rather than merely creating attention. The best campaign is one the operation can fulfil reliably, profitably, and honestly.

- Council Directive 2001/110/EC relating to honey, consolidated version applicable from 14 June 2026.
- Directive (EU) 2024/1438, especially revised honey-origin labelling and conditions for floral/regional origin descriptions.
- Food Safety Authority of Ireland. “Honey,” “Origin Labelling of Honey,” and current guidance on nutrition and health claims, accessed September 2026.
- Regulation (EC) No 1924/2006 on nutrition and health claims made on foods, and the current EU Register of nutrition and health claims.
- Regulation (EU) No 1169/2011 on food information to consumers, including Article 14 on distance selling.
