## References

- Regulation (EU) No 1169/2011 on food information to consumers, particularly Article 14 on distance selling.
- Food Safety Authority of Ireland. “Distance Selling” and “Selling or Advertising Food Online,” current guidance accessed September 2026.
- Food Safety Authority of Ireland. Current food-business registration, traceability, recall, health-claims, honey-labelling, and online-selling guidance.
- Directive 2011/83/EU on consumer rights, as amended, and current European Commission guidance for distance contracts.
- Directive (EU) 2024/1438 and Council Directive 2001/110/EC for honey origin/product information applicable from 14 June 2026.
- Regulation (EC) No 1924/2006 on nutrition and health claims.
- Current GDPR/ePrivacy, consumer-protection, VAT/sales-tax, customs, e-commerce, payments, cyber-security, courier, animal-health, and cross-border food rules for the target market.
- Current platform/payment-provider security and seller documentation rather than static software instructions.


<a id="chapter-65"></a>

# Chapter 65 — Farmers Markets

## Introduction

Farmers markets, food fairs, agricultural shows, seasonal markets, and community events can be excellent sales channels for beekeepers because they combine direct retail margin with face-to-face customer education. They also expose the business to a demanding environment: weather, transport, crowd movement, temporary food premises, cash handling, product display, food information, hygiene, insurance, traceability, and local trading rules must all work at the same time.

A successful market stall is therefore not simply a table covered with jars. It is a temporary retail operation with four simultaneous objectives:

1. protect food safety and product quality;
2. make accurate product information easy to understand;
3. convert passing visitors into profitable customers;
4. preserve traceability, stock control, and brand reputation.

The same basic food law that applies to a permanent food business can also apply to a temporary or movable food stall. For example, Irish FSAI guidance states that food businesses operating from stalls must comply with the food law relevant to their activities, including notification/registration, hygiene, traceability, labelling, and food-safety-management requirements. Exact rules, licences, market permits, and inspection systems differ by jurisdiction, so the examples in this chapter must always be checked locally.

## Learning Objectives

After completing this chapter, the reader should be able to:

- evaluate whether a farmers market is commercially suitable;
- calculate market-day profitability rather than judging only gross sales;
- design a safe, weather-resistant and customer-friendly stall;
- manage food hygiene in a temporary retail environment;
- maintain traceability from stock box to sold lot;
- display legally required product information correctly;
- handle samples safely when sampling is permitted;
- manage cash, card payments and inventory controls;
- reduce breakage, heat, rain, contamination, theft and crowd risks;
- measure conversion rate, average transaction value, repeat sales and event profitability;
- use direct customer feedback to improve product and marketing decisions;
- distinguish organiser rules, local trading permissions and food-business obligations.

## 65.1 Why Markets Can Be Valuable

Direct markets offer advantages that wholesale channels do not.

They can provide:

- higher selling price per unit;
- immediate cash flow;
- direct customer feedback;
- product sampling;
- brand recognition;
- repeat-customer relationships;
- opportunities to test new pack sizes;
- local-food positioning;
- seasonal sales spikes.

They also consume substantial labour and time.

## 65.2 Gross Sales Are Not Profit

A market that takes €800 can be less profitable than a market taking €500 if the first requires:

- higher pitch fee;
- longer travel;
- two staff;
- parking charges;
- food sampling costs;
- greater breakage;
- full-day attendance;
- poor repeat-customer potential.

Measure contribution after event-specific costs.

## 65.3 Market-Day Contribution

A useful simplified calculation is:

`Market contribution = sales revenue − product variable cost − event-specific costs`

Event-specific costs can include:

- stall/pitch fee;
- transport;
- parking/tolls;
- card-processing fees;
- hired staff;
- sampling consumables;
- packaging used at the event;
- breakage;
- event-specific advertising.

Owner labour should also be measured, even if not paid immediately.

## 65.4 Profit per Labour Hour

Calculate:

`Contribution per labour hour = market contribution ÷ total labour hours`

Total labour includes:

- loading;
- travel;
- setup;
- selling;
- packing down;
- return travel;
- stock reconciliation;
- cleaning.

This metric helps compare markets with online or wholesale activity.

## 65.5 Choosing a Market

Evaluate:

- footfall quality, not just volume;
- customer profile;
- competitor mix;
- event frequency;
- pitch fee;
- weather exposure;
- access and parking;
- organiser reputation;
- power/water availability;
- local food focus;
- repeat-customer potential;
- rules on sampling;
- rules on signage;
- insurance requirements.

## 65.6 Visitor Quality

Ten thousand visitors do not guarantee sales.

Ask:

- Are visitors there to buy food?
- Are they local or tourists?
- Do they carry shopping bags?
- Is the market a destination or a pass-through event?
- Are prices compatible with the audience?
- Is there excessive duplication of honey stalls?

## 65.7 Trial Before Commitment

Where possible, test a market for several dates before committing to a long season.

Record:

- weather;
- footfall estimate;
- transactions;
- revenue;
- product mix;
- customer questions;
- repeat visitors;
- labour hours.

One unusually good festival day should not define the expected weekly average.

## 65.8 Organiser Requirements

Market organisers may require:

- application form;
- product description;
- business registration evidence;
- insurance certificate;
- food-business registration details;
- stall photographs;
- risk assessment;
- electrical certificates where relevant;
- payment of pitch fee;
- compliance with opening hours.

Organiser acceptance does not replace legal obligations.

## 65.9 Local Trading Permission

Some locations require separate street-trading, casual-trading or local-authority permission.

The market organiser may hold one permission while individual traders still require another, depending on local law.

Verify who is legally responsible before attending.

## 65.10 Food-Business Registration

Where the beekeeper is operating a food business, temporary retail does not automatically remove food-law obligations.

Irish FSAI guidance, for example, treats regular food-stall activity as a food business subject to notification/registration and hygiene controls, with limited exceptions for genuinely one-off activities lacking continuity or organisation.

Check current local requirements before trading.

## 65.11 Food Safety Management

A market stall needs simple controls that fit the actual risks.

For sealed jars of honey, priorities include:

- clean products and packaging;
- protection from contamination;
- traceability;
- intact seals;
- safe sampling if used;
- allergen/ingredient controls for composite products;
- clean hands and utensils;
- separation from chemicals.

The control system should be proportionate but documented where required.

## 65.12 Stall Layout

A good stall layout separates:

- customer display;
- stock reserve;
- sampling area;
- payment area;
- bags/packaging;
- waste;
- personal belongings.

Avoid storing coats, food, phones and cash directly beside open samples.

## 65.13 The Table Is a Food Environment

A market table can be exposed to:

- dust;
- rain;
- insects;
- dog contact;
- customer hands;
- muddy bags;
- wind-blown debris.

Use cleanable surfaces and protect food accordingly.

## 65.14 Canopies and Weather Protection

A canopy can protect against sun and rain but creates wind-loading hazards.

Use:

- approved weights;
- secure legs;
- suitable anchors where permitted;
- no improvised dangerous tie-downs;
- an emergency plan for strong wind.

A flying canopy can seriously injure people.

## 65.15 Rain

Rain threatens:

- labels;
- cardboard cartons;
- electrical devices;
- food-sample hygiene;
- floor safety.

Keep reserve stock in closed waterproof containers rather than under dripping tablecloths.

## 65.16 Heat and Direct Sunlight

Direct sunlight can:

- heat honey;
- soften comb honey;
- damage wax products;
- fade labels;
- make chocolate/flavoured products unstable.

Use shade and monitor vulnerable products.

## 65.17 Cold Weather

Cold conditions can:

- thicken liquid honey;
- make creamed honey firmer;
- increase glass breakage risk during thermal shocks;
- reduce battery performance;
- affect staff comfort and selling ability.

Plan storage and display accordingly.

## 65.18 Product Display

Display should make it easy to answer:

- What is the product?
- What size is it?
- What does it cost?
- Where is it from?
- How is it different from the next jar?

Do not require the customer to ask for every price.

## 65.19 Price Visibility

Clear prices:

- reduce hesitation;
- speed transactions;
- improve fairness;
- reduce repeated questions;
- help customers compare pack sizes.

Follow local price-display rules.

## 65.20 Product Information

Mandatory food information must remain accurate at markets.

Depending on product and jurisdiction, this may include:

- name of food;
- net quantity;
- origin;
- durability/date information where required;
- lot identification;
- business identity/address;
- ingredients for composite products;
- allergens;
- storage instructions.

Do not assume direct sales exempt the product from labelling law.

## 65.21 Honey-Origin Claims

Country, region, locality or floral claims should match records and legal definitions.

A market stall encourages conversation, but verbal statements can also be misleading if unsupported.

Train sellers to use the same verified claims as the label and website.

## 65.22 Health Claims at the Stall

A salesperson can create a prohibited medical impression even when the printed label is compliant.

Avoid statements such as:

- “This cures allergies.”
- “This prevents infections.”
- “This treats arthritis.”
- “This honey is medicine.”

Only use permitted nutrition/health claims under the applicable law.

## 65.23 Samples

Sampling can increase conversion, but it changes the hygiene risk because food becomes open and repeatedly handled.

Before offering samples, verify:

- organiser permission;
- local food-safety requirements;
- allergen information;
- utensil hygiene;
- waste disposal;
- weather suitability.

## 65.24 Single-Use Sampling

Useful controls include:

- single-use tasting sticks/spoons;
- small disposable cups where appropriate;
- no double dipping;
- covered sample containers;
- controlled portions;
- prompt disposal of used utensils.

Do not allow communal dipping into the retail jar.

## 65.25 Hand Hygiene

Sampling requires reliable hand hygiene.

Possible temporary arrangements depend on law and product risk, but must provide the facilities required for the activity.

Hand sanitiser can supplement hygiene but does not always replace handwashing where actual food handling requires washing.

## 65.26 Money and Food

Cash and payment devices are handled by many people.

Where open food is sampled:

- separate payment and sample handling where practical;
- use utensils rather than fingers;
- clean hands when contamination occurs;
- avoid touching food-contact surfaces immediately after handling money.

## 65.27 Sample Temperature

Serve products at a temperature that represents normal consumer use.

Very cold honey can seem excessively firm; overheated honey loses normal texture.

For formal product comparison, keep sample conditions consistent.

## 65.28 Allergens in Composite Products

Plain honey is different from honey products containing:

- nuts;
- milk;
- sesame;
- spices;
- flavour ingredients.

Required allergen information must be available and accurate.

Do not allow a flavoured sample spoon to contaminate plain honey.

## 65.29 Sampling and Children

Honey should not be given to infants under 12 months because of infant botulism risk.

If sampling at family events, sellers should be trained not to offer honey indiscriminately to babies.

## 65.30 Glass Jar Safety

Glass jars are attractive but heavy and breakable.

Controls include:

- stable shelving;
- low display height for heavy stock;
- barriers at table edges;
- safe transport crates;
- breakage procedure.

## 65.31 Broken Glass Procedure

If a jar breaks:

1. stop access to the area;
2. remove exposed food/samples that may contain fragments;
3. collect large glass safely;
4. clean the area thoroughly;
5. inspect adjacent containers;
6. dispose of contaminated food where safety is uncertain.

Do not rely on visual picking from open honey.

## 65.32 Bees and Wasps at the Stall

Sweet samples can attract bees and wasps.

Reduce attraction by:

- covering samples;
- cleaning drips;
- closing waste;
- avoiding open honey jars for long periods;
- keeping empty sticky containers sealed.

Do not spray insecticide around exposed food.

## 65.33 Dogs and Other Animals

Open-air markets often allow pets.

Protect food from:

- nose contact;
- hair;
- leashes pulling displays;
- urination near stock;
- contamination of low-level boxes.

Keep food above the ground and within controlled display space.

## 65.34 Stock Storage Under the Stall

Reserve stock should be:

- off wet/dirty ground;
- in clean closed crates;
- protected from heat;
- organised by lot;
- easy to count.

Do not use open cardboard cartons directly on muddy surfaces.

## 65.35 Lot Separation

If multiple lots are visually identical, keep stock organised so the seller knows which lot is being sold.

Methods include:

- crate labels;
- shelf labels;
- lot-specific box positions;
- POS mapping.

Traceability should survive the temporary stall environment.

## 65.36 Recall Readiness

If a product must be withdrawn during a market, the trader should be able to identify:

- affected lot;
- remaining stock;
- which events received it;
- known wholesale customers;
- online orders where relevant.

Markets should not become a traceability blind spot.

## 65.37 Stock Sheet

A simple market stock sheet can record:

- product;
- lot;
- opening quantity;
- additional quantity;
- closing quantity;
- damaged units;
- samples used.

This helps reconcile sales and traceability.

## 65.38 POS Systems

A point-of-sale system can improve:

- product-level sales data;
- payment speed;
- stock control;
- tax records;
- staff accountability.

Offline capability is valuable where mobile signal is unreliable.

## 65.39 Card Payments

Card acceptance can increase convenience but adds:

- transaction fees;
- terminal cost;
- connectivity dependence;
- chargeback exposure.

Measure the effect rather than assuming it is free revenue.

## 65.40 Cash Handling

Use a defined system for:

- opening float;
- cash storage;
- change;
- shift handover;
- end-of-day count;
- deposit.

Do not leave large cash amounts visibly accessible.

## 65.41 Theft Prevention

Small premium products can disappear easily.

Reduce theft through:

- controlled display quantity;
- staff presence;
- stock behind the table;
- clear lines of sight;
- frequent counts;
- secure cash handling.

## 65.42 Personal Security

Market traders can handle cash early in the morning or late at night.

Consider:

- parking location;
- lighting;
- two-person closing procedures;
- cash concealment;
- emergency contacts;
- avoiding isolated loading areas.

## 65.43 Public Liability

Markets commonly require public-liability insurance.

Potential claims can arise from:

- trip hazards;
- falling displays;
- canopy accidents;
- broken glass;
- allergic reactions;
- contaminated samples.

Insurance requirements differ by event and jurisdiction.

## 65.44 Product Liability

Product-liability cover relates to harm caused by products sold.

Review whether insurance covers:

- honey;
- comb honey;
- pollen;
- propolis products;
- cosmetics;
- candles;
- flavoured products.

New product categories can change risk.

## 65.45 Stall Risk Assessment

A practical risk assessment considers:

- wind;
- rain;
- electrical equipment;
- trip hazards;
- glass;
- lifting;
- cash security;
- samples;
- allergens;
- children;
- vehicles during setup.

The assessment should result in actual controls.

## 65.46 Electrical Equipment

If lights, refrigerators, card terminals or other mains devices are used:

- follow organiser requirements;
- use suitable outdoor-rated equipment where exposed;
- protect cables from water and trip hazards;
- use approved connections;
- do not improvise electrical distribution.

## 65.47 Battery Equipment

Battery devices reduce cable hazards but introduce:

- charging requirements;
- battery failure;
- heat risk;
- theft risk.

Carry appropriate backup where payment depends on them.

## 65.48 Signage

Good signage should answer customer questions quickly.

Useful signs can show:

- product name;
- price;
- origin;
- pack size;
- payment methods;
- short evidence-based product difference.

Avoid cluttered walls of text.

## 65.49 Visual Hierarchy

From several metres away, customers should see:

1. brand/category;
2. main product;
3. price or offer;
4. reason to stop.

Detailed educational material can be closer to the table.

## 65.50 Height and Accessibility

Place products within easy viewing and reach.

Consider customers who are:

- children;
- wheelchair users;
- shorter/taller adults;
- carrying bags.

Avoid creating unstable high towers of glass jars.

## 65.51 Storytelling

A concise stall story can include:

- where the apiary is;
- what the current honey tastes like;
- how comb honey differs;
- how crystallisation works;
- why batches vary.

Keep claims factual and short.

## 65.52 Customer Questions as Research

Record repeated questions.

Examples:

- “Why has it crystallised?”
- “Is it local?”
- “Why is this darker?”
- “Can I give it to a baby?”
- “Do you sell online?”
- “Can I buy a larger jar?”

Questions reveal gaps in labels, website and product range.

## 65.53 Explaining Crystallisation

Staff should know that natural crystallisation is normal and depends on floral composition and storage.

Do not imply crystallisation proves purity or adulteration.

## 65.54 Explaining Colour

Honey colour varies naturally with floral source and storage history.

Do not claim darker honey is automatically healthier or stronger unless a permitted claim is specifically supported.

## 65.55 Repeat Customers

Markets become more valuable when customers return.

Support repeat purchase through:

- consistent attendance;
- email list with consent;
- website/QR code;
- loyalty systems where profitable;
- recognisable packaging;
- reliable stock.

## 65.56 QR Codes

QR codes can link to:

- product information;
- batch story;
- online store;
- email signup;
- social media.

Test every code before the event.

## 65.57 Email Collection

Collect email only with appropriate consent and privacy information.

Do not add card-payment emails or competition entrants automatically to marketing lists without lawful basis.

## 65.58 Market-Only Offers

A limited market offer can:

- increase conversion;
- move seasonal stock;
- create urgency.

But frequent discounting can train customers to wait for markets rather than buy online at full price.

## 65.59 Bundles

Bundles can increase average transaction value.

Examples:

- three honey varieties;
- honey + beeswax candle;
- gift pack;
- comb honey + liquid honey.

Calculate bundle margin before setting the offer.

## 65.60 Samples and Conversion

Measure whether sampling actually improves sales.

Record, for selected periods:

- people sampled;
- transactions following sample;
- product chosen;
- cost of sample.

A popular free sample is not automatically profitable marketing.

## 65.61 Conversion Rate

A practical market estimate can be:

`Conversion rate = purchasing customers ÷ engaged visitors`

Counting every passer-by may be difficult, so use a consistent definition.

## 65.62 Average Transaction Value

`ATV = total sales revenue ÷ number of transactions`

Increase ATV through useful bundles, larger packs and complementary products rather than pressure selling.

## 65.63 Units per Transaction

Track average number of units per sale.

This can identify whether customers are:

- trying one jar;
- stocking up;
- buying gifts;
- responding to bundles.

## 65.64 Sell-Through

`Sell-through = units sold ÷ units brought`

Low sell-through can indicate:

- excessive stock;
- wrong product mix;
- weak demand;
- poor display;
- bad weather.

Do not bring the entire warehouse simply to make the stall look full.

## 65.65 Stockouts

Selling out can be positive, but repeated early stockouts may mean lost high-margin sales.

Track time of stockout by SKU.

Use demand history to improve loading.

## 65.66 Product Mix

Markets are excellent for observing relative demand among:

- liquid honey;
- creamed honey;
- comb honey;
- small/large jars;
- pollen;
- candles;
- gift packs.

Use transaction data rather than memory.

## 65.67 Slow Products

A slow product may still be strategically valuable if it:

- attracts attention;
- raises ATV;
- differentiates the stall;
- sells strongly seasonally.

Judge portfolio role, not only raw unit count.

## 65.68 Seasonal Events

Christmas, harvest festivals and tourism events can shift demand toward:

- gifts;
- premium packs;
- candles;
- comb honey;
- decorative packaging.

Plan production months earlier.

## 65.69 Weather and Demand

Weather can affect both footfall and product preference.

Record weather with sales.

Over time, data can reveal whether rain, heat or cold materially changes event economics.

## 65.70 Market Location Within the Event

Stall position matters.

Potential advantages include:

- near entrance;
- near food zone;
- near seating;
- corner visibility.

Potential disadvantages include:

- wind tunnel;
- hidden aisle;
- competitor cluster;
- loud stage;
- poor lighting.

Track stall number/location in event records.

## 65.71 Neighbouring Traders

Neighbours can be:

- competitors;
- referral partners;
- information sources;
- shared customers.

Professional relationships improve the trading environment.

Do not disparage competitors with unsupported claims.

## 65.72 Cross-Promotion

Cross-promotion may work with:

- bread bakers;
- cheese makers;
- tea/coffee sellers;
- gift producers;
- local tourism businesses.

Ensure food pairing claims and bundles remain correctly labelled.

## 65.73 Wholesale Leads from Markets

Retail markets can attract:

- shop owners;
- chefs;
- café buyers;
- corporate gift buyers.

Keep a simple wholesale information sheet or digital link ready.

Do not negotiate unprofitable wholesale prices impulsively during a busy market.

## 65.74 Media Opportunities

Local journalists and content creators visit markets.

Prepare concise facts:

- who you are;
- where the bees are;
- what is distinctive;
- what is seasonal;
- how customers can buy.

Avoid making speculative scientific or health claims on camera.

## 65.75 Complaints at the Stall

Handle complaints calmly.

Record:

- product;
- lot if known;
- purchase date/place;
- problem;
- customer contact if offered;
- action.

Do not argue about crystallisation, leakage or flavour; investigate objectively.

## 65.76 Refunds and Replacements

Set a consistent policy compatible with consumer law.

A generous replacement may be commercially sensible, but safety complaints still require investigation rather than only compensation.

## 65.77 Product Returns

Do not automatically resell returned food.

Storage and handling after purchase may be unknown.

Separate returns from saleable stock.

## 65.78 Daily Setup Checklist

Before opening:

- verify permits/organiser documents;
- secure canopy;
- inspect table stability;
- check stock and lots;
- display prices;
- test card terminal;
- set cash float;
- prepare sampling controls;
- inspect glass and seals;
- clear trip hazards.

## 65.79 During-Trading Checks

Periodically check:

- canopy security;
- sample cleanliness;
- insects;
- spills;
- cash level;
- stock level;
- temperature-sensitive products;
- waste.

A stall changes throughout the day.

## 65.80 Close-Down Procedure

At closing:

1. stop sampling;
2. secure cash;
3. count stock;
4. record damage/samples;
5. pack glass securely;
6. collect waste;
7. inspect site;
8. dismantle canopy safely;
9. reconcile sales later in a controlled environment.

## 65.81 Cleaning After the Event

Clean:

- table coverings;
- sampling tools;
- crates;
- reusable signs;
- payment devices where hygienically necessary.

Do not return sticky equipment directly into clean storage.

## 65.82 Market Data Sheet

Record per event:

- date;
- event;
- location;
- pitch fee;
- weather;
- staff hours;
- travel cost/time;
- revenue;
- transactions;
- units by product;
- samples;
- breakage;
- complaints;
- leads;
- closing stock.

## 65.83 Event Ranking

After several events, compare:

- contribution;
- contribution/hour;
- ATV;
- transactions;
- repeat customers;
- wholesale leads;
- strategic value.

Drop chronically poor events unless they serve another clear purpose.

## 65.84 Opportunity Cost

A Saturday market uses time that could be spent on:

- hive work;
- extraction;
- online fulfilment;
- family/rest;
- another event;
- wholesale delivery.

The best market must beat the value of the alternative use of that time.

## 65.85 Multiple Markets

Scaling to several simultaneous markets requires:

- staff training;
- duplicate stall equipment;
- inventory control;
- consistent claims;
- cash controls;
- transport planning.

Owner presence can no longer fix every problem personally.

## 65.86 Staff Script

Train staff on:

- product differences;
- origin;
- crystallisation;
- infant warning;
- approved claims;
- price/bundles;
- complaints;
- allergens;
- sampling hygiene.

Do not allow staff to improvise medical claims.

## 65.87 Staff Incentives

Sales incentives can increase pressure selling or risky claims if designed badly.

Use measures such as:

- customer service;
- accurate cash/stock;
- compliance;
- reasonable sales targets.

Quality and safety should not lose to commission.

## 65.88 Sustainability at Markets

Possible improvements include:

- reusable crates;
- minimal transport trips;
- recyclable packaging where practical;
- right-sized bags;
- reduced sample waste.

Avoid vague “zero waste” claims without evidence.

## 65.89 Waste

Plan for:

- tasting sticks;
- cups;
- broken packaging;
- cardboard;
- damaged food;
- general waste.

Follow organiser and local waste rules.

## 65.90 Unsold Stock

After the event:

- inspect seals;
- remove wet/damaged labels;
- separate heat-exposed product if quality may be affected;
- return stock to controlled storage;
- preserve lot identity.

Do not automatically mix event stock into another lot.

## 65.91 Decision Table

| Situation | Main question | Response principle |
|---|---|---|
| High sales, very high fee/labour | Is contribution worthwhile? | Calculate contribution and labour return |
| Large footfall, low sales | Is audience/product/display fit poor? | Observe engagement and test offer/signage |
| Samples popular, sales unchanged | Is sampling profitable? | Measure conversion and sample cost |
| Wind warning | Can stall remain safely anchored? | Follow organiser/weather safety plan; stop trading if unsafe |
| Broken jar near open samples | Could glass contaminate food? | Stop area, discard exposed food and clean fully |
| Unknown lot in reserve box | Can traceability be proven? | Hold from sale until identified |
| Baby offered honey sample | Infant botulism risk | Do not provide honey to infants under 12 months |
| Customer asks if honey cures illness | Unsupported medical claim risk | Explain product factually without treatment claim |

## 65.92 Practical Market-Day Procedure

### Before departure

- load stock by SKU and lot;
- verify labels and seals;
- pack clean display equipment;
- check canopy/weights;
- test payment systems;
- prepare float;
- pack sampling equipment if authorised;
- take organiser/insurance documents;
- check weather.

### Setup

1. secure stall structure;
2. create clean product display;
3. store reserve stock off ground;
4. display prices/information;
5. prepare controlled payment area;
6. set up sampling only when hygiene controls are ready.

### Trading

7. keep samples covered and single-use;
8. clean spills immediately;
9. maintain accurate claims;
10. monitor stock and cash;
11. record useful customer questions/leads.

### Close

12. stop open-food activity;
13. reconcile stock;
14. secure money;
15. pack product safely;
16. clean site/equipment;
17. record event economics.

## 65.93 Farmers-Market Checklist

- [ ] Market commercially evaluated.
- [ ] Pitch/organiser approval confirmed.
- [ ] Local trading permission checked.
- [ ] Food-business registration/notification status checked.
- [ ] Insurance requirements met.
- [ ] Stall risk assessment current.
- [ ] Canopy/weights safe.
- [ ] Product protected from rain, heat and dust.
- [ ] Mandatory information accurate.
- [ ] Prices visible.
- [ ] Lots traceable.
- [ ] Reserve stock stored off ground.
- [ ] Sampling authorised and hygienic.
- [ ] Allergen information available for composite products.
- [ ] No honey offered to infants under 12 months.
- [ ] Cash/card systems controlled.
- [ ] Glass-breakage procedure known.
- [ ] Staff trained on claims and complaints.
- [ ] Opening/closing stock recorded.
- [ ] Market profitability reviewed after event.

## Key Takeaways

- A farmers market is a temporary retail food operation, not merely a sales table.
- Gross sales must be adjusted for product cost, fees, travel, labour, sampling and breakage.
- Food-law obligations can apply fully to temporary or mobile stalls.
- Organiser approval does not replace registration, trading permission, insurance or food-law duties.
- Good stall layout separates display, reserve stock, sampling, payment and waste.
- Samples require stronger hygiene controls than sealed jars.
- Traceability must continue through crates, stock sheets and market-day sales.
- Weather, wind and glass are genuine safety risks.
- Claims made verbally by staff must be as accurate as claims on the label.
- Measure contribution, labour return, conversion, ATV, sell-through and repeat-customer value to decide which markets deserve continued attendance.

## Chapter Summary

Farmers markets can generate excellent direct margins and valuable customer relationships, but only when the entire market day is treated as a controlled business process. A profitable event must cover product cost, stall fee, transport, payment fees, samples, labour and opportunity cost.

The stall itself is part of the food system. Product must be protected from weather, dust, animals, insects, broken glass and customer handling. Open sampling introduces additional hygiene, allergen and hand-contact risks. Regular food-stall businesses can also require notification or registration, food-safety controls, traceability and compliance with the same core food law that applies elsewhere.

Direct customer interaction is the channel's greatest advantage. Questions reveal confusion about crystallisation, origin, colour, infant safety and product differences. Recorded systematically, these conversations can improve labels, online content, pack sizes and new-product decisions.

The final test is evidence. Revenue alone does not identify the best market. A professional beekeeper compares event contribution, contribution per labour hour, average transaction value, sell-through, repeat business and strategic leads. Markets that consistently underperform can then be replaced by channels that use scarce time and stock more effectively.

<a id="chapter-66"></a>

# Chapter 66 — Food Safety

## Introduction

Food safety begins before honey enters a jar. It begins with the materials placed in the hive, the medicines used on colonies, the water used to clean equipment, the fuel burned in the smoker, the cleanliness of harvest boxes, the condition of the extraction room, the design of storage containers, and the accuracy of the records that connect a finished product back to its source.

Honey is naturally hostile to the growth of many microorganisms because of its high sugar concentration, low water activity, acidity, and other antimicrobial properties. This natural stability is valuable, but it must not be confused with sterility or with exemption from food law. Honey can still be contaminated by chemicals, physical foreign bodies, excessive moisture, dirty equipment, unsafe ingredients, packaging failures, or naturally occurring spores. Other hive products such as fresh pollen and royal jelly have very different moisture and preservation requirements and can be substantially more perishable.

A professional food-safety system therefore asks four questions continuously:

1. What can harm the consumer?
2. Where can that hazard enter, survive, increase, or remain uncontrolled?
3. What preventive control stops or reduces it?
4. What evidence proves the control is working?

This chapter uses HACCP-style reasoning without pretending that every small beekeeper needs an industrial food factory. The scale of controls should match the operation, but the responsibility for safe food remains.

## Learning Objectives

After completing this chapter, the reader should be able to:

- distinguish food safety from quality, authenticity, and marketing;
- identify biological, chemical, physical, and allergen hazards in beekeeping products;
- explain prerequisite programmes and HACCP-based control;
- map hazards from hive to harvest, extraction, processing, storage, packing, transport, online fulfilment, and market sales;
- design hygiene zones and food-contact controls;
- prevent contamination from veterinary medicines, pesticides, fuel, smoke, cleaning chemicals, water, glass, metal, pests, and dirty equipment;
- understand product-specific risks in honey, pollen, royal jelly, propolis, creamed products, comb honey, and flavoured products;
- distinguish cleaning from sanitation and verify both;
- create corrective-action, hold, withdrawal, and recall procedures;
- understand current Irish/EU food-business registration and HACCP expectations as jurisdiction-specific examples.

## 66.1 Food Safety Is Not the Same as Quality

A product can be safe but low quality. It can also look premium while being unsafe.

Examples:

- coarse crystallisation is mainly a quality issue;
- fermented honey can be a quality and legal-compliance issue;
- glass in a jar is a physical food-safety hazard;
- unauthorised veterinary-medicine residue is a chemical hazard;
- incorrect country-of-origin wording is primarily a legal/traceability issue;
- a false therapeutic claim is a legal/marketing issue;
- undeclared allergenic ingredients in flavoured honey can become a food-safety issue.

Separating these categories prevents weak controls and confused corrective action.

## 66.2 The Four Main Hazard Categories

### Biological hazards

Relevant examples include:

- osmotolerant yeasts in high-moisture honey;
- mould or yeast growth in poorly dried pollen;
- microbial growth in water-based propolis products;
- microbial deterioration of inadequately chilled royal jelly;
- naturally occurring bacterial spores;
- pests contaminating exposed food.

### Chemical hazards

Examples include:

- veterinary-medicine residues;
- pesticide residues;
- cleaning chemicals;
- fuel and lubricants;
- smoke contaminants;
- heavy metals;
- unauthorised additives;
- migration from unsuitable food-contact materials.

### Physical hazards

Examples include:

- glass;
- metal fragments;
- broken plastic;
- wood splinters;
- stones;
- insects;
- brush bristles;
- packaging fragments.

### Allergen hazards

Plain honey is not normally formulated with the regulated allergenic ingredients used in composite foods, but allergen hazards become important when the same facility handles:

- nuts;
- milk powders;
- sesame;
- cereal ingredients;
- flavoured spreads;
- other composite foods.

Bee-product sensitivity also exists, particularly for pollen and propolis, although legal allergen-labelling categories vary by jurisdiction.

## 66.3 Natural Stability Does Not Mean Sterility

Honey can contain microorganisms and spores while still resisting active growth.

A key public-health example is *Clostridium botulinum* spores. Public-health authorities advise against feeding honey to infants under 12 months because infant intestinal conditions can allow spores to germinate.

Routine honey extraction, straining, warming, or creaming does not reliably sterilise honey of these spores.

This is a useful reminder: low water activity suppresses growth; it does not prove absence.

## 66.4 Product Risk Varies Across the Hive-Product Range

| Product | Typical stability challenge | Important control |
|---|---|---|
| Mature honey | Moisture, chemical/physical contamination | Dry hygienic handling, moisture control, traceability |
| Creamed honey | Moisture redistribution, fermentation, ingredients | Base-honey quality, stable crystallisation, formulation control |
| Comb honey | Heat damage, pests, contamination of edible wax | Fresh broodless comb, food-safe storage, pest control |
| Pollen | High moisture, microbes, pesticides, allergens | Rapid chilling/freezing or validated drying, clean traps |
| Royal jelly | Highly perishable, temperature-sensitive | Cold chain, hygiene, short controlled exposure |
| Propolis | Chemical variability, contaminants, solvent/formulation hazards | Clean collection, defined extraction, contaminant control |
| Beeswax for food/cosmetic chain | Residues/adulteration | Source segregation, end-use specification, testing |

The food-safety plan must therefore be product-specific.

## 66.5 The Food-Safety Management System

A food-safety management system should be a working system rather than a folder kept for inspections.

Its components commonly include:

- prerequisite programmes;
- hazard analysis;
- control measures;
- monitoring;
- corrective action;
- verification;
- records;
- traceability;
- withdrawal/recall procedure;
- staff training.

In Ireland, FSAI describes HACCP-based procedures as a legal requirement for food businesses and requires registration before a food business begins operating. The exact competent authority and obligations depend on the activity. These are current Irish examples, not universal rules for every country.

## 66.6 Prerequisite Programmes

Prerequisite programmes create the hygienic foundation on which HACCP depends.

Common prerequisites include:

- approved suppliers;
- suitable premises;
- potable water;
- equipment maintenance;
- cleaning and sanitation;
- pest control;
- personal hygiene;
- staff training;
- waste management;
- chemical control;
- glass/brittle-plastic control;
- temperature control where required;
- allergen control;
- traceability;
- calibration of measuring equipment.

If these foundations are weak, a complicated HACCP chart will not rescue the operation.

## 66.7 HACCP Logic

HACCP stands for Hazard Analysis and Critical Control Point.

The practical logic is:

1. describe the product and process;
2. map the process steps;
3. identify reasonably foreseeable hazards;
4. evaluate their likelihood and severity;
5. identify effective controls;
6. determine whether any step is a critical control point or whether the hazard is controlled through prerequisites/operational controls;
7. define limits or acceptance criteria;
8. monitor;
9. take corrective action when control fails;
10. verify the system;
11. keep records.

Small operations can use simple forms, but the reasoning must still be real.

## 66.8 Not Every Control Is a Critical Control Point

Over-labelling every step as a CCP makes the system unusable.

Examples of controls that may be managed as prerequisite programmes include:

- handwashing;
- pest-proof doors;
- cleaning schedules;
- supplier approval;
- glass control.

A true CCP requires a specific point where loss of control can create unacceptable food-safety risk and where control can be monitored effectively.

The exact classification depends on the process.

## 66.9 Process Mapping: Hive to Consumer

A useful flow diagram may include:

**apiary inputs → harvest → covered transport → receiving → extraction → straining/settling → processing → bulk storage → packing → finished storage → dispatch → retail/online/market sale**

For other products add product-specific steps such as:

- pollen trapping → immediate chilling/freezing/drying;
- royal-jelly grafting/collection → immediate cold chain;
- propolis trap harvest → cleaning → extraction/formulation.

Walk the actual process rather than copying a generic flow chart.

## 66.10 Hive Inputs Are Food-Safety Inputs

Anything deliberately placed into a food-producing hive can affect later products.

Control:

- veterinary medicines;
- feed;
- foundation/wax;
- hive paint/coatings;
- cleaning materials;
- pest-control products;
- smoker fuels.

The cheapest hive input can become the most expensive contamination event.

## 66.11 Veterinary Medicines

Before using any veterinary or mite-control product, verify:

- legal authorisation;
- target condition;
- label dose;
- placement;
- temperature restrictions;
- honey-super restrictions;
- withdrawal periods;
- required records;
- disposal instructions.

Do not use agricultural formulations simply because they contain a similar active ingredient.

## 66.12 Treatment Records

Treatment records should identify:

- apiary and colony/group;
- product;
- active ingredient;
- batch/lot where relevant;
- application and removal dates;
- person applying;
- supers present/absent;
- withdrawal/release date;
- deviations.

A harvest should not proceed if treatment status cannot be established.

## 66.13 Feed and Honey Contamination

Feeding sugar syrup while marketable honey supers are on can create product-identity and quality problems if feed reaches harvest comb.

Feeding unknown honey can also move bee diseases such as AFB spores.

Use feed deliberately, document timing, and protect saleable crop identity.

## 66.14 Smoker Fuel

Use clean suitable smoker fuel.

Avoid:

- painted timber;
- treated wood;
- plastic;
- oil-soaked material;
- synthetic fabrics;
- chemical packaging.

Excess smoke can taint honey and comb even when no acute toxic hazard is created.

## 66.15 Agricultural Pesticide Exposure

Suspected pesticide contamination requires more than a bee-mortality response.

If honey or pollen may be affected:

- identify and segregate relevant lots;
- record application/exposure history if known;
- preserve samples;
- contact appropriate authority/laboratory when required;
- do not blend suspect product into unaffected stock.

## 66.16 Harvest Hygiene

At harvest:

- cover supers immediately;
- keep them off soil and dirty surfaces;
- prevent rain entry;
- control robbing;
- exclude brood comb from ordinary honey extraction;
- use clean food-contact brushes/covers;
- use a clean vehicle.

The apiary is the first food-handling site.

## 66.17 Transport Hygiene

Transport vehicles should be:

- clean;
- dry;
- free from fuel leakage;
- free from chemical cargo contamination;
- protected from rain and dust;
- secured against load shift.

Never transport open food supers beside pesticides, waste, fuel cans, or dirty livestock equipment.

## 66.18 Receiving Controls

At the extraction room, check:

- lot identity;
- covers intact;
- no brood frames;
- no rainwater;
- no chemical odour;
- no gross contamination;
- no pest infestation;
- no leaking/broken comb that could contaminate surrounding material.

Questionable supers should be held separately.

## 66.19 Hygiene Zoning

A simple honey room benefits from logical separation between:

- incoming field-exposed equipment;
- clean food-contact processing;
- packaging materials;
- chemical storage;
- waste.

One-way product movement reduces cross-contamination.

## 66.20 Food-Contact Surfaces

Food-contact surfaces should be:

- suitable for food;
- smooth;
- durable;
- cleanable;
- corrosion-resistant;
- maintained in good repair.

Common suitable materials include appropriate stainless steel and certified food-grade plastics.

Unknown metals, damaged coatings, or reused chemical containers are poor choices.

## 66.21 Water

Water used where it can contact food or food-contact surfaces should be potable or otherwise meet the legal suitability standard for the operation.

Water can introduce:

- microbes;
- chemicals;
- excess moisture.

Honey equipment must be fully dry before use after wet cleaning.

## 66.22 Honey Is Hygroscopic

Honey absorbs moisture from humid air.

Food-safety implications include:

- increased fermentation risk;
- local wet zones under condensation;
- dilution from wet equipment;
- moisture gain during long open-tank exposure.

A dry honey room is as important as a clean one.

## 66.23 Moisture and Fermentation

As honey moisture increases, osmotolerant yeasts can grow more readily.

Control includes:

- harvesting mature honey;
- representative refractometer measurement;
- keeping rooms dry;
- protecting open tanks;
- avoiding water ingress;
- segregating wet frames/lots;
- suitable storage temperature.

Legal moisture limits should not be confused with the optimum safety margin for every lot.

## 66.24 Cleaning Versus Sanitation

**Cleaning** removes food residues, wax, propolis, dirt, and organic matter.

**Sanitation/disinfection** reduces microorganisms to an acceptable level where required.

A dirty surface cannot be made hygienic simply by spraying sanitiser over honey and wax residues.

Remove soil first.

## 66.25 Cleaning Sequence

A controlled sequence can include:

1. remove bulk honey/wax dry;
2. dismantle equipment as needed;
3. wash with suitable detergent;
4. rinse where required;
5. sanitise where required by the process;
6. drain and dry fully;
7. inspect before use.

The exact method must suit the equipment and product.

## 66.26 Cleaning Chemicals

Cleaning chemicals should be:

- labelled;
- stored away from food;
- used at correct concentration;
- compatible with equipment;
- rinsed when required;
- handled with PPE;
- never decanted into food containers without unmistakable control.

Chemical residues can destroy an entire batch.

## 66.27 Cleaning Verification

Verification can include:

- visual inspection;
- no sticky residue;
- no chemical odour;
- complete dryness;
- ATP or microbiological testing where appropriate;
- documented pre-operational checks.

A signed checklist should represent an actual inspection.

## 66.28 Pest Control

Food rooms should exclude and monitor:

- rodents;
- flies;
- ants;
- cockroaches;
- wasps/bees;
- stored-product pests.

Use integrated pest management that prevents pesticide contamination of exposed products.

## 66.29 Doors and Openings

Control entry through:

- self-closing or managed doors;
- screens;
- sealed gaps;
- clean waste practices;
- prompt removal of spills.

Open honey is a powerful insect attractant.

## 66.30 Glass Control

Broken glass in viscous honey is difficult to remove confidently.

A glass-control programme can:

- minimise unnecessary glass in processing areas;
- identify essential glass items;
- inspect lights or use protected fittings;
- define a breakage response;
- quarantine exposed product.

Do not strain and release honey after a glass break simply because visible pieces were removed.

## 66.31 Brittle Plastic

Hard plastic can fragment like glass.

Inspect:

- lids;
- guards;
- scrapers;
- measuring tools;
- buckets;
- light covers.

Replace cracked items before they shed fragments.

## 66.32 Metal Control

Metal fragments can come from:

- damaged strainers;
- extractor baskets;
- knives;
- screws;
- wire;
- machinery wear.

Preventive maintenance and visual inspection are more valuable than trying to find metal after it enters honey.

## 66.33 Lubricants

Use food-grade lubricants where incidental food contact is possible.

Control:

- quantity;
- application point;
- drip shields;
- maintenance records;
- damaged seals.

Automotive grease has no place in a food-contact zone.

## 66.34 Personnel Hygiene

Food handlers should understand:

- handwashing;
- wound covering;
- clean clothing;
- illness reporting;
- no eating/smoking over food;
- safe jewellery policy;
- hair control where required;
- cross-contamination prevention.

Small family businesses need the same discipline even when nobody is called an “employee”.

## 66.35 Handwashing

Handwashing requires:

- suitable water;
- soap;
- hygienic drying;
- accessible sink/location;
- correct timing.

Gloves do not replace handwashing and can spread contamination when worn carelessly.

## 66.36 Illness

Food handlers with vomiting, diarrhoea, infectious skin lesions, or other relevant illness should follow current food-handler exclusion/reporting rules.

Honey may be low risk for microbial growth, but dirty hands can still contaminate packaging, ingredients, and high-moisture products.

## 66.37 Training

Training should match the task.

Examples:

- apiary workers: treatment/harvest separation;
- honey-room workers: hygiene and foreign-body control;
- market staff: sampling and allergen communication;
- online fulfilment staff: lot selection and damaged-package control;
- managers: HACCP, recall, and traceability.

Training is effective only when behaviour is observed and corrected.

## 66.38 Packing Controls

Before packing:

- product is released;
- lot identity is known;
- jars/containers are clean and suitable;
- closures are correct;
- labels match the product;
- net quantity controls are working;
- foreign-body and allergen risks are controlled.

A label error can turn safe food into a legally non-compliant product.

## 66.39 Packaging Materials

Packaging should be:

- food-contact compliant;
- appropriate for honey acidity and shelf life;
- clean;
- protected in storage;
- traceable where necessary.

Reuse is not automatically safe simply because a container “looks clean”.

## 66.40 Tamper Evidence

Tamper-evident features can improve product integrity and customer confidence.

They do not replace hygiene or secure packaging.

A damaged seal should trigger investigation of the individual unit or lot as appropriate.

## 66.41 Honey Sampling at Markets

Sampling creates new hazards because sealed food becomes exposed repeatedly.

Control:

- clean serving utensils;
- covered sample food;
- single-use sticks/spoons where appropriate;
- no double-dipping;
- hand hygiene;
- allergen information for added ingredients;
- waste disposal.

Do not leave an open communal honey jar with unrestricted consumer contact.

## 66.42 Flavoured Honey Products

Adding ingredients changes the hazard profile.

Potential new hazards include:

- allergens;
- extra moisture;
- microbial spores;
- undeclared ingredients;
- cross-contact;
- product-name changes.

A plain-honey HACCP plan is not sufficient for a honey-and-nut spread.

## 66.43 Fresh Fruit in Honey

Fresh fruit can add enough water to support microbial growth.

Do not assume honey automatically preserves high-moisture additions safely.

A formulated product requires validated water-activity, pH, process, preservation, and shelf-life controls as appropriate.

## 66.44 Dried Ingredients

Dry ingredients can introduce:

- allergens;
- spores;
- foreign bodies;
- pesticide residues;
- moisture.

Use approved suppliers and specifications.

## 66.45 Pollen Food Safety

Fresh pollen is much more perishable than honey.

Control priorities include:

- clean traps;
- frequent harvest;
- rapid cooling/freezing or validated drying;
- water-activity/moisture control;
- pest exclusion;
- pesticide/residue awareness;
- allergy warnings as required.

Do not leave fresh pollen warm in collection drawers for extended periods.

## 66.46 Royal Jelly Food Safety

Royal jelly is high-moisture and biologically active.

Control priorities include:

- hygienic collection tools;
- rapid cooling;
- validated chilled/frozen storage;
- minimal room-temperature exposure;
- clean packaging;
- cold-chain monitoring.

Temperature abuse can reduce quality even before obvious spoilage appears.

## 66.47 Propolis Products

Raw propolis is relatively dry, but extracts can have very different hazards depending on solvent.

Water-based propolis products can require microbiological preservation.

Alcohol-based products introduce:

- flammable-liquid safety;
- residual-solvent/strength specifications;
- packaging compatibility;
- labelling restrictions.

Food safety and workplace safety overlap.

## 66.48 Comb Honey

Comb honey requires protection of both honey and edible wax.

Important controls include:

- fresh broodless comb;
- no treatment contamination;
- moisture control;
- heat protection;
- food-safe wax-moth control;
- clean packaging;
- no pesticide exposure during storage.

## 66.49 Beeswax for Food-Related Uses

Rendered apiary wax is not automatically food-additive or cosmetic grade.

Potential hazards include:

- acaricide residues;
- pesticide residues;
- adulterants;
- disease history;
- processing contaminants.

End-use specifications determine required testing.

## 66.50 Allergen Management

If regulated allergens enter the facility:

1. identify every allergenic ingredient;
2. verify supplier information;
3. store separately as needed;
4. schedule production to reduce cross-contact;
5. clean and verify;
6. label correctly;
7. manage rework carefully.

Do not rely on memory when multiple product recipes exist.

## 66.51 Allergen Labelling and Precautionary Statements

Mandatory allergen declaration and precautionary “may contain” practices depend on jurisdiction.

Precautionary wording should not be used casually as a substitute for proper cross-contact control.

Consult current applicable food-information rules.

## 66.52 Infant Honey Risk

Honey should not be given to infants under 12 months because of infant botulism risk associated with spores.

This warning should be communicated accurately without implying that honey is unsafe for the general adult population.

Local label requirements differ.

## 66.53 Food Fraud as a Safety-System Concern

Adulteration can create food-safety as well as authenticity risks when unknown syrups, colours, medicines, or chemicals enter the food chain.

Supplier approval and traceability help prevent economically motivated adulteration.

A suspicious cheap bulk lot deserves more, not less, due diligence.

## 66.54 Approved Suppliers

For purchased inputs, maintain:

- supplier identity;
- product specification;
- food-contact status where relevant;
- lot numbers;
- certificates of analysis where appropriate;
- complaint history;
- audit/approval status for higher-risk supplies.

Supplier approval is a preventive control.

## 66.55 Incoming Inspection

Check incoming:

- jars/lids;
- labels;
- ingredients;
- bulk honey;
- seed honey;
- pollen/propolis;
- chemicals;
- food-contact equipment.

Inspect identity, lot, condition, documentation, and contamination risk.

## 66.56 Hold and Release

Use clear product status:

- HOLD;
- RELEASED;
- REJECTED;
- REWORK-ELIGIBLE.

A product awaiting a laboratory result should not be indistinguishable from saleable stock.

## 66.57 Corrective Action

A corrective action should answer:

1. What failed?
2. What product was affected?
3. Was food safety compromised?
4. What immediate containment was taken?
5. What is the disposition of affected product?
6. What caused the failure?
7. What prevents recurrence?
8. How will effectiveness be verified?

Do not stop at “operator retrained” when equipment design caused the failure.

## 66.58 Non-Conforming Product

Non-conforming product can result from:

- high moisture;
- wrong label;
- broken seal;
- residue concern;
- fermentation;
- foreign body;
- traceability failure;
- temperature abuse.

Segregate first; investigate second; release only with evidence.

## 66.59 Withdrawal Versus Recall

Terminology varies, but commonly:

- **withdrawal** removes product from the supply chain before it reaches consumers or when consumer action is not required;
- **recall** involves product that has reached consumers and may require public/customer action.

Follow the definitions and procedures of the competent authority in the relevant market.

## 66.60 Recall Readiness

A recall system should identify quickly:

- affected lot;
- quantity produced;
- stock on site;
- business customers supplied;
- consumer sales channels;
- source inputs;
- related lots;
- public communication needs.

Traceability determines the precision of the recall.

## 66.61 Mock Recalls

A mock recall tests the system without a real hazard.

Set a scenario such as:

> “Lot H260915A has a suspected chemical contamination.”

Then measure:

- time to identify source;
- time to identify customers;
- quantity reconciliation;
- completeness of records;
- communication readiness.

Fix weaknesses before a real incident.

## 66.62 Complaint Management

Consumer complaints can reveal hazards.

Prioritise complaints involving:

- glass/metal;
- chemical taste/odour;
- swelling/fermentation;
- allergic reaction;
- wrong label;
- suspected tampering.

Retain product and evidence where possible and link the complaint to the lot.

## 66.63 Food Incident Records

Record:

- date/time;
- product and lot;
- reporter;
- nature of hazard;
- quantity;
- distribution;
- immediate action;
- authority/customer contacts;
- sample/lab information;
- final disposition;
- corrective action.

Objective records are more useful than emotional descriptions.

## 66.64 Emergency: Glass Breakage

If glass breaks near exposed food:

1. stop activity;
2. isolate the area;
3. identify exposed product;
4. remove people safely;
5. clean using the defined glass procedure;
6. discard or hold any product whose freedom from glass cannot be assured;
7. inspect before restarting.

## 66.65 Emergency: Chemical Spill

Protect people first.

Then:

- stop food processing;
- isolate affected product/equipment;
- identify chemical;
- follow spill instructions;
- assess packaging/contact;
- clean and verify;
- discard food if safety cannot be demonstrated.

## 66.66 Emergency: Water Ingress

If water enters honey:

- stop transfer;
- isolate affected lot;
- identify water source and cleanliness;
- measure moisture;
- assess chemical/microbial contamination;
- do not blend the problem away.

## 66.67 Emergency: Power Failure

For honey, short power loss may have limited safety impact, but cold-chain products such as royal jelly can be affected rapidly.

Record:

- duration;
- product temperature;
- storage limits;
- corrective action.

Prioritise the most temperature-sensitive products.

## 66.68 Emergency: Refrigeration Failure

For royal jelly or other chilled products:

- keep doors closed;
- record time/temperature;
- move product to validated backup storage if safe;
- hold affected lots;
- assess against validated stability criteria.

Do not reset the thermometer and pretend the excursion did not happen.

## 66.69 Calibration

Critical measuring equipment may include:

- refractometers;
- thermometers;
- scales;
- pH meters;
- water-activity meters;
- data loggers.

Calibration/verification frequency should reflect risk and use.

## 66.70 Refractometer Control

A honey refractometer should be:

- clean;
- checked/calibrated according to manufacturer method;
- protected from water dilution;
- used with representative samples;
- recorded by instrument ID where the system requires it.

Instrument precision cannot fix poor sampling.

## 66.71 Thermometer Control

Verify thermometers against a suitable reference or calibration service according to risk.

The display setting of a warming cabinet is not always the same as the product temperature.

## 66.72 Scale Control

Scales used for legal net quantity may require specific verification under weights-and-measures law.

Process scales also need routine checks so formulation and mass-balance records remain reliable.

## 66.73 Documentation Control

Food-safety documents should have:

- title;
- version;
- date;
- owner;
- approved procedure;
- obsolete-copy control where necessary.

Staff should use the current procedure, not a three-year-old printed sheet from a drawer.

## 66.74 Record Integrity

Good records are:

- legible;
- contemporaneous;
- attributable;
- accurate;
- protected from unauthorised alteration;
- retrievable.

Backfilling records from memory weakens evidence.

## 66.75 Digital Systems

Digital records can improve:

- traceability;
- alerts;
- calibration reminders;
- document control;
- recall speed.

But they require:

- backups;
- access control;
- cybersecurity;
- data export/recovery plans.

A cloud outage should not erase the only copy of the food-safety system.

## 66.76 Internal Audits

An internal audit checks whether actual practice matches the system.

Audit examples:

- follow one honey lot backwards;
- inspect chemical storage;
- observe handwashing;
- test glass-break response;
- review expired treatment products;
- inspect pest-control records;
- verify cleaning results.

Audit evidence should drive corrective action.

## 66.77 Management Review

At least periodically, review:

- complaints;
- non-conformances;
- recalls/mock recalls;
- supplier issues;
- laboratory trends;
- pest activity;
- equipment failures;
- training needs;
- legal changes.

Food safety is a living management system.

## 66.78 Irish Example: Registration

Current FSAI guidance states that a food business must be registered before it starts operating, including a business operating from home. The registering authority depends on the type of activity.

The lesson for an international reader is broader: identify the competent authority before starting commercial food activity.

## 66.79 Irish Example: HACCP-Based Procedures

FSAI states that HACCP-based procedures are a legal requirement and should be supported by prerequisite programmes.

The system should suit the business; a microproducer may use a simpler documented system than an industrial packer, but simplicity does not mean no hazard analysis.

## 66.80 Irish/EU Example: Honey Rules

Honey composition, designation, and labelling are governed by specific honey legislation plus general food-information rules.

From 14 June 2026, EU-origin rules for blended honey became more specific, requiring countries and percentage shares under the applicable conditions.

Food safety, legal identity, and traceability therefore intersect.

## 66.81 Food-Safety Culture

Food-safety culture means people act safely even when nobody is watching.

It is strengthened by:

- clear expectations;
- practical training;
- managers following the same rules;
- easy reporting of problems;
- no punishment for stopping unsafe production;
- learning from near misses.

A culture of hiding mistakes creates larger incidents later.

## 66.82 Near Misses

A near miss is an event that could have caused contamination but was caught in time.

Examples:

- unlabelled chemical found beside jars before use;
- cracked plastic scoop replaced before fragments broke off;
- wrong label detected before dispatch;
- wet tank discovered before filling.

Record significant near misses because they reveal system weakness.

## 66.83 Food Defence

Food defence addresses deliberate contamination or tampering.

Small businesses should still consider:

- locked chemical storage;
- controlled access to bulk tanks;
- secure online admin accounts;
- tamper-evident closures;
- visitor control where appropriate.

## 66.84 Fraud Prevention

Food fraud prevention addresses economically motivated deception.

Controls include:

- approved suppliers;
- origin records;
- mass balance;
- authenticity testing based on risk;
- secure labels;
- segregation of non-compliant stock.

Food defence and food fraud overlap but are not identical.

## 66.85 Product Release

Before releasing a lot, verify applicable items such as:

- lot identity;
- moisture/specification;
- treatment release;
- label version;
- package integrity;
- required laboratory results;
- origin records;
- hold status cleared.

Release should be an intentional decision.

## 66.86 Food-Safety Decision Table

| Finding | Main risk | Immediate action |
|---|---|---|
| Unknown treatment status | Chemical residue | HOLD; reconstruct records before harvest/release |
| Rainwater entered supers | Moisture/contamination | Segregate; measure/investigate |
| Broken glass near open tank | Physical hazard | Stop, isolate, apply glass-break procedure |
| Wet cleaned tank | Moisture/chemical carryover | Dry/verify before filling |
| Fermentation odour | Yeast spoilage | Hold lot; investigate moisture/history |
| Unlabelled ingredient in flavoured honey | Allergen/identity | Stop production; identify material |
| Royal jelly warm-chain excursion | Quality/microbial stability | Hold; evaluate validated time-temperature limits |
| Consumer reports metal fragment | Physical hazard | Hold related lot, investigate scope and recall need |

## 66.87 Practical Food-Safety Setup Procedure

1. List every product.
2. Draw the actual process flow for each product family.
3. List biological, chemical, physical, and allergen hazards at each step.
4. Identify prerequisites and product-specific controls.
5. Define monitoring and acceptance criteria.
6. Define hold/corrective-action procedures.
7. Build traceability and recall links.
8. Train everyone who performs the work.
9. Verify through observation, sampling, audits, and mock recall.
10. Review whenever product, equipment, supplier, law, or process changes.

## 66.88 Food-Safety Checklist

- [ ] Food-business registration/competent authority identified.
- [ ] Product list current.
- [ ] Process flows match actual work.
- [ ] Hazard analysis complete.
- [ ] Prerequisite programmes documented.
- [ ] HACCP-based controls appropriate to scale.
- [ ] Veterinary-treatment records linked to harvest release.
- [ ] Smoker fuel and hive inputs controlled.
- [ ] Harvest equipment clean and covered.
- [ ] Transport vehicle suitable for food.
- [ ] Incoming supers inspected.
- [ ] Food-contact equipment suitable and maintained.
- [ ] Potable/suitable water controlled.
- [ ] Cleaned equipment fully dry.
- [ ] Chemical storage segregated.
- [ ] Pest-control programme active.
- [ ] Glass/brittle-plastic control established.
- [ ] Personal hygiene and illness rules understood.
- [ ] Allergen controls match recipes.
- [ ] Product-specific cold/dry storage validated.
- [ ] Measuring equipment checked/calibrated.
- [ ] Hold/release system visible.
- [ ] Traceability and recall tested.
- [ ] Complaints linked to lots.
- [ ] Corrective actions verified.
- [ ] Legal and process changes reviewed.

## Scientific Notes

### Water activity is more directly linked to microbial growth than moisture percentage

Honey moisture is easy to measure and useful in routine control, but water activity better represents water available to microorganisms. Composition influences the relationship between the two.

### Osmotolerant yeasts exploit a niche most microbes cannot

Honey suppresses many microorganisms but selected yeasts tolerate high osmotic pressure. High moisture and warm storage therefore create a specific fermentation risk.

### Spores can remain without growth

Low-water foods can carry dormant bacterial spores. Safety advice for infant honey illustrates the difference between presence and growth.

### Hazard control should be risk-based

The same control intensity is not necessary for every product. Fresh pollen and royal jelly require more aggressive preservation controls than mature honey because their water activity and composition differ fundamentally.

### Verification tests the system, not only the product

A final laboratory result cannot replace hygienic design. Food-safety systems rely on preventing contamination rather than testing every jar after the event.

## Key Takeaways

- Honey's natural stability does not make it sterile or exempt from food-safety control.
- Food safety, quality, authenticity, and marketing legality are related but distinct.
- Biological, chemical, physical, and allergen hazards must be assessed across the real process.
- Prerequisite programmes create the foundation for HACCP-based control.
- Hive treatments, smoker fuel, harvest transport, water, chemicals, equipment, packaging, and ingredients can all affect food safety.
- Product risk varies greatly: royal jelly and fresh pollen are far more perishable than mature honey.
- Cleaning removes soil; sanitation is a separate microbial-control step where required.
- Hold and release status prevents questionable product from entering sale accidentally.
- Corrective action must address cause, not merely the immediate symptom.
- Traceability and mock recall determine how precisely a real food incident can be contained.

## Chapter Summary

Food safety in beekeeping is a chain of preventive decisions from hive to consumer. Honey's low water activity provides natural protection, but chemical residues, glass, fuel, cleaning agents, excess water, allergens, dirty equipment, packaging failures, and naturally occurring spores remain real hazards. Other hive products can be significantly more perishable and require dedicated preservation controls.

The strongest system begins with prerequisite programmes: suitable premises, food-contact equipment, water, cleaning, pest control, chemical control, staff hygiene, supplier approval, maintenance, and traceability. HACCP-based reasoning then identifies product-specific hazards, monitoring, corrective action, and verification.

Commercial food handling must also respond to legal requirements. In Ireland, current FSAI guidance requires food-business registration before operation and HACCP-based food-safety procedures. Other jurisdictions use different competent authorities and legal structures, so the method to teach is not memorisation of one country's form but maintenance of a current compliance map.

A food-safety system is successful when unsafe or uncertain product is stopped before release, incidents are traceable quickly, corrective action prevents recurrence, and everyday behaviour matches the written procedure.
