<a id="chapter-67"></a>

# Chapter 67 — Traceability

## Introduction

Traceability is the ability to reconstruct the history and movement of a product. In beekeeping, that means being able to answer practical questions quickly:

- Which apiary produced this jar?
- Which harvest and extraction batch did it come from?
- Which tank was used?
- Which veterinary treatments affected the source colonies?
- Which supplier provided the jars, lids, ingredients, or purchased honey?
- Which business customers received the affected lot?
- How many units remain in stock?
- If honey was blended, what proportion came from each source or country?

A traceability system does not need to be complicated, but it must be consistent. The goal is not to collect every possible fact. The goal is to preserve the links needed to investigate quality, food-safety, origin, disease, or commercial problems without relying on memory.

Under EU General Food Law, food businesses are required to identify the businesses that supplied them and the businesses they supplied: the familiar “one step back, one step forward” principle. Final consumers are generally not treated like business customers for that external-traceability requirement. Strong businesses go further by maintaining **internal traceability** linking incoming materials, production batches, tanks, pack lots, and dispatch records.

For honey, internal traceability became even more commercially important with the EU blend-origin rules applying from 14 June 2026. Where honey from multiple countries is blended, country shares must be supported by the operator's traceability documentation. In other words, origin percentages must be produced by records at blending time, not reconstructed later from memory.

## Learning Objectives

After completing this chapter, the reader should be able to:

- distinguish external and internal traceability;
- explain the one-step-back/one-step-forward principle;
- design practical apiary, colony, harvest, batch, tank, and pack lot codes;
- connect treatments and colony movements to food lots;
- define lot boundaries deliberately;
- preserve traceability through extraction, blending, storage, packing, wholesale, online sales, and farmers markets;
- perform mass-balance reconciliation;
- support country-origin percentage declarations for honey blends;
- design paper or digital records that remain usable during a recall;
- conduct and evaluate a mock recall;
- manage retained samples and chain of custody;
- understand how traceability reduces the scope and cost of incidents.

## 67.1 What Traceability Is

Traceability is the documented connection among:

- source;
- process;
- product;
- destination.

For a beekeeper this often begins with an apiary and ends with a customer-facing lot code.

A strong system allows both directions:

**backward:** jar → packing lot → bulk tank → extraction/harvest → apiary/source;

**forward:** apiary/source → harvest → tank → packed lot → business customers/channels.

## 67.2 External Traceability

External traceability identifies business relationships outside the operation.

Typical records include:

- supplier name and address;
- product supplied;
- supplier lot;
- quantity;
- delivery date;
- business customer name and address;
- product/lot supplied;
- quantity;
- dispatch date.

EU General Food Law is built around this one-step-back/one-step-forward approach.

## 67.3 Internal Traceability

Internal traceability connects steps within the business.

Examples:

- Apiary A → Harvest H1 → Extractor Batch E1 → Tank T3 → Pack Lot P7;
- Supplier Honey Lot S15 → Blend B4 → Tank T5 → Retail Lots P10 and P11;
- Pollen Trap Lot PT8 → Drying Batch D3 → Pack Lot POL6.

Internal traceability is what makes a narrow recall possible.

## 67.4 Why Legal Minimum Traceability May Not Be Enough

A legal system may only require direct suppliers and business customers, but a commercial beekeeper also needs to know:

- which colonies contributed;
- which treatment records applied;
- which extraction day was involved;
- which tank held the honey;
- whether a blend included purchased honey;
- which packaging batch was used;
- which retail channels still hold stock.

The stronger the internal chain, the smaller the uncertainty.

## 67.5 Traceability Does Not Require Tracking Every Final Consumer

For ordinary retail sales, recording the identity of every individual consumer is usually unnecessary and may create privacy problems.

Instead, retain:

- pack lot;
- sales channel;
- date range;
- quantity sold.

Online orders may naturally contain customer details because fulfilment requires them, but they should be handled under applicable data-protection rules.

## 67.6 Apiary Identification

Every apiary should have a stable identifier.

Examples:

- `AP01`;
- `NORTH-FARM`;
- `BRL-03`.

The code should remain stable even if colony numbers change.

Record:

- location;
- landowner/site agreement where relevant;
- installation/movement dates;
- local notes;
- regulatory registration number where required.

## 67.7 Colony Identification

A colony identifier supports health and production traceability.

Examples:

- `AP01-C01`;
- `AP01-C02`.

Do not reuse a deceased colony's identity for a completely different colony without a clear historical rule.

If equipment is reused, the colony record and equipment history should remain distinguishable.

## 67.8 Queen Identification

Where queen genetics or sales matter, record:

- queen ID;
- breeder/source;
- introduction date;
- year;
- mating status/source;
- replacement date;
- daughter-colony links where relevant.

This can support investigation of production, disease resistance, swarming, and breeding performance.

## 67.9 Treatment Traceability

A food lot should be traceable to treatment status.

If colonies AP01-C01 through C10 received a treatment on specified dates, later harvest records should show whether those colonies contributed to saleable honey and whether label restrictions or withdrawal requirements were satisfied.

This link protects food safety.

## 67.10 Colony Movement

Record movement of colonies between apiaries when relevant to:

- disease tracing;
- pollination contracts;
- varietal honey origin;
- pesticide exposure;
- regulatory movement requirements.

A harvest origin claim becomes weak if colony location during the relevant flow is unknown.

## 67.11 Harvest Lot

A harvest lot can be defined by a consistent rule, such as:

- one apiary on one harvest date;
- one floral crop from one apiary;
- one group of supers removed together.

Example:

`H-AP01-20260915`

The code itself matters less than consistency and uniqueness.

## 67.12 Extraction Batch

An extraction batch groups material processed together.

Record:

- harvest lots included;
- start/end time;
- extractor line;
- operator;
- moisture checks;
- destination tank;
- deviations.

If several harvest lots are mixed during extraction, the new batch becomes the traceability junction.

## 67.13 Tank Identification

Every bulk vessel should have a unique ID.

Examples:

- `T01`;
- `SS500-02`.

A tank record should show:

- product lot(s) added;
- mass added;
- date/time;
- removals;
- current balance;
- cleaning status.

Do not identify tanks only by physical position such as “left tank”.

## 67.14 Packing Lot

A packing lot links bulk product to consumer units.

Example:

`P-260916-A`

It may represent:

- one packing run;
- one tank and label version;
- one day or shift;
- another documented logical batch.

The lot boundary should be narrow enough to support practical recall but not so fragmented that records become unusable.

## 67.15 Lot Coding Principles

A good lot code is:

- unique;
- readable;
- durable;
- recorded in the batch sheet;
- not dependent on secret memory;
- short enough to print reliably;
- structured enough to search.

Avoid codes that become ambiguous after year changes.

## 67.16 Date Codes Are Useful but Not Sufficient Alone

A date can be part of a lot code, but two products packed on the same date may come from different tanks.

Add a batch suffix, tank code, product code, or sequential number as needed.

## 67.17 Lot Boundaries Are a Risk Decision

A larger lot simplifies paperwork but increases recall size.

A smaller lot narrows incidents but increases record complexity.

Choose boundaries based on:

- production scale;
- tank size;
- source diversity;
- product value;
- origin claims;
- recall capability.

## 67.18 Purchased Honey

For purchased bulk honey record:

- supplier;
- supplier lot;
- country/countries of origin;
- quantity;
- delivery date;
- certificate of analysis where used;
- seal/container condition;
- internal incoming lot;
- storage tank.

Never allow purchased honey to become anonymous once it enters your premises.

## 67.19 Packaging Traceability

High-risk packaging issues can justify recording:

- jar supplier lot;
- lid/closure lot;
- label version;
- tamper-seal batch.

This is especially useful if a supplier later reports a packaging defect.

## 67.20 Ingredient Traceability

For flavoured or composite products record every ingredient lot.

Example:

`Creamed honey lot CH20 + cinnamon lot CIN44 → product lot HC21`

This enables allergen and ingredient recalls.

## 67.21 Country-of-Origin Traceability

Origin claims must be supported by real source records.

For own honey:

- apiary country;
- harvest period;
- movement history.

For purchased honey:

- supplier country documentation;
- lot certificates/contracts;
- receipt quantity;
- blend use.

## 67.22 EU 2026 Blend Percentages

Under EU rules applying from 14 June 2026, countries in a honey blend are generally declared in descending order by weight together with their percentage contribution, with specified legal tolerances and limited options.

FSAI notes that the percentage basis is supported by the operator's traceability documentation.

This makes weighing and mass balance a label-control function.

## 67.23 Weigh at the Time of Blending

Do not calculate origin percentages after the tank is empty.

At blending, record:

- source lot;
- source country;
- mass added;
- total final mass;
- losses/rework as applicable.

Then calculate:

`country share (%) = country-origin mass ÷ final defined blend mass × 100`

Follow applicable tolerance and labelling rules.

## 67.24 Multiple Lots From the Same Country

If two source lots come from the same country, their masses can be aggregated for country-percentage calculations while retaining individual lot identity internally.

Do not discard the finer source detail.

## 67.25 Mass Balance

Mass balance compares inputs, outputs, stock, and expected process losses.

A simple batch equation is:

`Opening stock + inputs = packed output + closing stock + documented loss/rework`

Large unexplained differences can indicate:

- record error;
- spill;
- wrong scale;
- unrecorded addition;
- mislabelling;
- theft or diversion.

## 67.26 Why Mass Balance Matters for Authenticity

A business that claims to sell 2,000 kg of a specific origin after receiving only 800 kg of that origin has a traceability problem even before laboratory testing.

Mass balance is a powerful authenticity control.

## 67.27 Yield Reconciliation

At harvest and extraction, compare:

- number of supers;
- gross weight;
- extracted honey;
- cappings honey;
- residual honey;
- waste/spill.

Yield anomalies can reveal mistakes and equipment loss.

## 67.28 Chain of Custody

Chain of custody documents who controlled a sample or product through transfers.

It becomes especially important for:

- regulatory samples;
- authenticity disputes;
- pesticide incidents;
- legal complaints;
- insurance claims.

Record identity, time, person, seal, and transfer.

## 67.29 Laboratory Sample Identity

A laboratory result is only useful if it is tied to the right lot.

The submission should include:

- unique sample ID;
- lot;
- sample date;
- sampler;
- sampling point;
- requested tests;
- storage/transport conditions.

## 67.30 Retained Samples

Retained samples can support later comparison.

A retained-sample system defines:

- which batches are retained;
- sample size;
- container;
- storage;
- duration;
- disposal.

The sample should be labelled independently from the production container.

## 67.31 Paper Systems

Paper records can be reliable when they are:

- standardised;
- legible;
- dated;
- protected;
- filed logically;
- easy to retrieve.

Do not rely on loose notebook pages with undefined abbreviations.

## 67.32 Digital Systems

Digital traceability can improve:

- search;
- lot linking;
- inventory;
- origin calculations;
- recall speed;
- document backup.

It introduces risks such as:

- data loss;
- access problems;
- cyberattack;
- accidental overwriting;
- vendor lock-in.

Export and backup matter.

## 67.33 Hybrid Systems

A practical small business may use:

- paper apiary sheets;
- spreadsheet batch records;
- accounting software for customers;
- printed lot labels;
- cloud backups.

The system can be hybrid as long as the links are reliable.

## 67.34 Master Data

Define standard codes for:

- apiaries;
- products;
- tanks;
- suppliers;
- customers;
- ingredients;
- packaging;
- treatments.

Stable master data prevents spelling variations from breaking searches.

## 67.35 Record Corrections

Corrections should preserve history.

Paper:

- single-line strikeout;
- corrected entry;
- initials/date where required.

Digital:

- audit trail/version history where appropriate.

Do not erase inconvenient data silently.

## 67.36 Backdating

Records should be made when work occurs.

Backdating from memory creates uncertainty and can undermine legal evidence.

If a late entry is unavoidable, mark it honestly as a late correction with source evidence.

## 67.37 Business Customers

Maintain records for wholesale/business deliveries:

- customer;
- address/contact;
- product;
- lot;
- quantity;
- date;
- invoice/delivery reference.

This is central to one-step-forward traceability.

## 67.38 Final Consumers

At farmers markets or direct retail, individual final-consumer identities generally need not be recorded for basic one-step-forward traceability.

Instead record:

- event/channel;
- date;
- lots sold;
- quantities.

This tells you where affected product was exposed to consumers.

## 67.39 Online Orders

Online systems often contain detailed delivery data.

Link fulfilment to lot by:

- pick list;
- warehouse scan;
- packing log;
- order metadata.

Protect personal data and do not retain more than legitimately needed.

## 67.40 Farmers Market Traceability

Before the market, record:

- lot quantities loaded.

After the market, record:

- unsold quantity;
- damaged samples;
- returns;
- approximate units sold by lot.

This allows the business to estimate consumer exposure during a recall.

## 67.41 Wholesale Mixed-Lot Orders

If one wholesale shipment contains multiple lots, delivery documents should identify each lot and quantity.

Do not write only “100 jars honey” if traceability requires distinguishing 60 jars P1 and 40 jars P2.

## 67.42 Stock Location

Inventory should identify where product is stored:

- warehouse bay;
- pallet;
- shelf;
- tank;
- cold room;
- vehicle in transit.

A recall is slowed dramatically when stock location is unknown.

## 67.43 Quarantine Stock

Held or rejected stock should remain physically or digitally blocked.

Traceability must preserve:

- original lot;
- reason for hold;
- quantity;
- location;
- disposition.

Never create a new lot simply to erase a quality failure.

## 67.44 Rework

When permitted, rework must remain traceable.

Record:

- original lot;
- quantity reworked;
- receiving lot;
- reason;
- authorisation.

The receiving lot inherits relevant risk history.

## 67.45 Product Downgrade

If table honey becomes baker's/industrial product under applicable law, preserve original lot identity and create a documented status change.

A new commercial category does not erase source history.

## 67.46 Returns

Returned stock may have unknown storage or tampering history.

Record:

- customer/channel;
- original lot;
- return date;
- quantity;
- reason;
- seal condition;
- disposition.

Do not automatically return consumer-held food to saleable stock.

## 67.47 Complaint Traceability

Every complaint should link to:

- lot;
- purchase date/channel;
- product size;
- photos/sample where available;
- related complaints.

Multiple complaints from one lot can reveal a pattern.

## 67.48 Traceability and Food Incidents

In an incident, traceability answers two urgent questions:

1. Where did the problem come from?
2. Where did the affected product go?

Internal records then determine whether the incident affects:

- one jar;
- one pack lot;
- one tank;
- one harvest;
- multiple countries/suppliers.

## 67.49 Withdrawal Scope

A weak system forces a wider withdrawal because uncertainty cannot be separated from affected product.

Better traceability can reduce:

- quantity destroyed;
- customer disruption;
- reputational damage;
- investigation time.

Traceability is therefore an economic asset.

## 67.50 Recall Team

Even a small business should define who will:

- stop dispatch;
- identify stock;
- contact authority;
- contact business customers;
- update website/social channels if required;
- reconcile quantity;
- preserve evidence.

One person may hold several roles, but responsibilities should be clear.

## 67.51 Recall Contact List

Keep current contacts for:

- competent food authority;
- laboratory;
- insurer;
- major customers;
- logistics provider;
- packaging supplier;
- legal/professional adviser where used.

Do not build the contact list during the emergency.

## 67.52 Mock Recall Design

Choose a realistic scenario, for example:

- pesticide residue in purchased honey;
- glass found in a packing lot;
- incorrect allergen label;
- country-origin documentation error.

Start with one lot code and trace both directions.

## 67.53 Mock Recall Metrics

Measure:

- time to identify source;
- time to identify business customers;
- percentage of quantity reconciled;
- time to obtain contact records;
- ability to stop online/warehouse dispatch;
- communication accuracy.

Set improvement actions.

## 67.54 Quantity Reconciliation

For affected lot L:

`produced quantity = stock on hand + business-customer quantity + direct-consumer quantity estimate + samples/waste + documented loss`

Aim to explain the full quantity.

Small unexplained variance can be normal depending on system; large variance requires investigation.

## 67.55 Recall Versus Withdrawal Records

Record why the chosen action was taken and who authorised it.

Include:

- hazard assessment;
- consumer exposure;
- regulatory advice;
- affected lot;
- communications;
- recovery/destruction quantities.

## 67.56 Destruction Records

If product is destroyed, record:

- lot;
- quantity;
- method;
- date;
- witness/contractor where relevant;
- waste documentation.

Destroyed product should not remain counted as available inventory.

## 67.57 Supplier Recall

If a supplier recalls jars, ingredients, or purchased honey:

- identify incoming supplier lot;
- identify all internal batches that used it;
- stop affected stock;
- identify customers;
- follow supplier/regulator instructions.

This tests internal traceability especially strongly.

## 67.58 Packaging Recall

A lid or jar defect can affect product across several honey lots.

Packaging-lot records allow you to identify all food lots using that component.

## 67.59 Treatment-Record Incident

If an authorised treatment was accidentally used while supers were present:

trace:

- affected colonies;
- supers/harvests;
- extraction batches;
- tanks;
- packed lots;
- customers.

Do not assume the whole year's production is affected if records can narrow the scope.

## 67.60 Pesticide Incident Traceability

Record:

- affected apiary;
- exposure date;
- flowering crop/area;
- colonies;
- supers harvested before/after event;
- samples;
- later lot disposition.

This separates exposed from unrelated product.

## 67.61 Disease Traceability

Animal-health traceability can intersect food traceability.

For AFB or other serious disease, records of:

- colony movements;
- brood-frame transfers;
- equipment transfers;
- honey feeding;
- sales of colonies

can help official tracing.

## 67.62 Equipment Traceability

For critical equipment, record:

- equipment ID;
- maintenance;
- repairs;
- lubricant;
- breakdowns;
- calibration.

If a metal fragment is found, equipment records help identify the likely machine and affected time window.

## 67.63 Cleaning Traceability

For high-risk transitions, record:

- equipment;
- lot ended;
- cleaning performed;
- verification;
- next lot.

This matters especially when allergens or flavoured products are processed.

## 67.64 Label Version Control

Each label design should have a version or approval reference.

Packing records should indicate which version was used.

This prevents a legal change or allergen correction from leaving old labels in uncontrolled use.

## 67.65 Best-Before and Lot Codes

A best-before date and lot code may be related, but they serve different functions.

The lot identifies production history; the date communicates shelf-life information under applicable law.

Do not rely on best-before alone if multiple batches share the same date.

## 67.66 Barcode and QR Systems

Barcodes and QR codes can improve scanning and consumer information.

They do not create traceability by themselves.

The database behind the code must remain accurate and available.

## 67.67 GS1-Type Systems

Larger businesses may use standardised trade-item and logistic-unit identifiers.

Benefits include interoperability with retailers and distribution centres.

Small businesses can still use simpler internal codes until commercial requirements justify standardisation.

## 67.68 Privacy

Traceability and privacy should be designed together.

Keep food-business records necessary for safety and legal obligations, but avoid using customer personal data beyond legitimate purposes.

Online customer data requires appropriate security and retention controls.

## 67.69 Cybersecurity

Protect digital traceability with:

- unique accounts;
- strong authentication;
- role-based access where practical;
- backups;
- tested recovery;
- protected exports;
- secure devices.

A ransomware event during a recall can become a food-safety problem.

## 67.70 Backup Strategy

Use at least one recoverable backup independent of the primary working system.

Periodically test:

- restore;
- export;
- readability;
- completeness.

A backup that has never been restored is only an assumption.

## 67.71 Data Retention

Retention periods depend on:

- food law;
- tax law;
- shelf life;
- customer contracts;
- insurance;
- certification.

Set a documented schedule rather than deleting records randomly.

## 67.72 Record Availability

Records should be retrievable promptly during inspection or incident.

A theoretically complete archive that requires several days to decipher is weak operational traceability.

## 67.73 Internal Audit

Audit one randomly chosen finished lot:

- trace backward to apiary/supplier;
- identify treatment and quality records;
- identify packaging/label version;
- reconcile quantity;
- trace forward to customers/channels.

Then audit a raw material forward.

## 67.74 Traceability Decision Table

| Situation | Traceability question | Required record |
|---|---|---|
| Consumer reports glass | Which packing lots/equipment window? | Pack lot, line/equipment, time, customers |
| Supplier recalls lids | Which food lots used that lid batch? | Packaging receipt + pack batch records |
| AFB found in apiary | Which colonies/equipment moved? | Colony and movement records |
| Country-origin challenge | What source masses formed blend? | Supplier lots + blend weights |
| High HMF in one tank | What source/process history? | Harvest/extraction/warming/tank records |
| Online recall | Which orders received lot? | Order-to-lot fulfilment link |
| Market recall | Which event sold affected lot? | Load/return/sales-by-lot record |
| Lost label on drum | Can identity be proven from controlled records? | Container/location/transfer records |

## 67.75 Example Lot Coding System

A small operation might use:

- apiary: `A03`;
- harvest: `H-A03-260815`;
- extraction: `E-260816-01`;
- tank: `T02`;
- packing: `P-260901-A`.

The packing sheet then links `P-260901-A` to `T02`, which links to the source extraction and harvest.

The exact syntax is optional; the links are essential.

## 67.76 Example Blend Record

| Source lot | Country | Mass added |
|---|---|---:|
| S1 | Ireland | 400 kg |
| S2 | Romania | 300 kg |
| S3 | Spain | 300 kg |
| **Total** |  | **1,000 kg** |

Before losses/adjustments permitted by the applicable calculation basis, the nominal shares are 40%, 30%, and 30%.

Record actual legally relevant final calculations under current rules.

## 67.77 Example Market Reconciliation

Before market:

- P1: 40 jars;
- P2: 30 jars.

After market:

- P1 returned: 12;
- P2 returned: 8;
- samples/damage: 2 jars equivalent documented.

The remainder can be assigned to direct-consumer sales with reasonable lot-level precision.

## 67.78 Example Online Fulfilment Link

Order `O-12345`:

- 2 × 340 g honey;
- lot P7;
- packed by operator X;
- dispatch date;
- carrier reference.

If lot P7 is recalled, the customer can be contacted where lawful and necessary.

## 67.79 Traceability Checklist

- [ ] Apiaries have stable IDs.
- [ ] Colonies/groups are identifiable where needed.
- [ ] Treatments link to harvest release.
- [ ] Colony movements recorded where relevant.
- [ ] Harvest lots uniquely coded.
- [ ] Extraction batches link to harvest lots.
- [ ] Tanks have unique IDs.
- [ ] Tank additions/removals recorded by mass.
- [ ] Packing lots link to bulk lots.
- [ ] Packaging/ingredient lots recorded where risk justifies it.
- [ ] Supplier identity and supplier lots retained.
- [ ] Business-customer deliveries include lot/quantity.
- [ ] Direct-sale channels record lots/quantities.
- [ ] Online fulfilment can link orders to lots.
- [ ] Country-origin source documentation retained.
- [ ] Blend masses support percentage declarations.
- [ ] Rework remains linked to original lot.
- [ ] Returns remain traceable.
- [ ] Quarantined stock retains identity.
- [ ] Retained samples clearly labelled.
- [ ] Record corrections preserve history.
- [ ] Digital records backed up.
- [ ] Recall contacts current.
- [ ] Mock recall performed and measured.
- [ ] Mass balance reconciled.
- [ ] Retention schedule documented.

## Scientific and Management Notes

### Traceability reduces uncertainty rather than eliminating hazards

A perfect lot code does not prevent contamination. It reduces the time and scope required to identify and control affected product.

### Granularity has economic value

Finer internal traceability can reduce recall cost by separating affected from unaffected product. However, extremely fine lot fragmentation can create administrative error. The optimal system balances precision and usability.

### Mass balance is an authenticity tool

Origin or premium-product claims are constrained by physical quantity. A business cannot legitimately sell more material of a defined origin than the traceable material received or produced, adjusted for documented processing flows.

### Data integrity matters as much as data volume

Ten accurate, linked records are more useful than hundreds of inconsistent fields. Traceability design should prioritise reliable links.

### Traceability should be tested under time pressure

Mock recalls reveal problems that ordinary record review misses: missing phone numbers, inaccessible cloud accounts, unknown warehouse locations, and unrecorded rework.

## Key Takeaways

- Traceability connects source, process, product, and destination.
- One-step-back/one-step-forward external traceability is the legal foundation in EU General Food Law.
- Internal traceability determines how narrowly a business can isolate an incident.
- Apiary, harvest, extraction, tank, and packing codes should be stable and linked.
- Treatment status and colony movements can be relevant to food and animal-health tracing.
- Blend-country percentage labels require real weight and origin documentation at blending time.
- Mass balance supports authenticity, inventory, and recall.
- Final consumers normally do not need individual recording for basic external traceability, but sales channel and lot exposure should be known.
- Digital systems require backup, access control, and recovery testing.
- A mock recall is the best practical test of whether traceability works.

## Chapter Summary

Traceability is the memory of a beekeeping business. A finished jar should be able to lead back through its pack lot, bulk tank, processing batch, harvest, apiary, treatment status, and purchased inputs. In the other direction, a problematic supplier lot or apiary event should lead forward to every affected tank, pack lot, wholesale customer, online order, and direct-sale channel.

EU food law establishes one-step-back/one-step-forward traceability, while internal traceability is designed by the operator. Strong internal links reduce recall scope and make origin claims defensible. The 2026 EU honey-origin rules make this especially important for blends because country percentage shares depend on traceability documentation.

Mass balance strengthens the system by ensuring that physical quantities reconcile. Retained samples, chain-of-custody records, label version control, packaging traceability, and customer delivery records further improve investigation quality.

The ultimate test is a mock recall. If a business can identify source, affected stock, business customers, direct-sale channels, and reconciled quantities within a reasonable time without relying on one person's memory, the traceability system is becoming operationally reliable.

4. What is forward traceability?
5. What is external traceability?

8. What is meant by one step forward?
9. Why is strong internal traceability useful even when not explicitly required in the same detail by law?
10. Why are final consumers usually treated differently from business customers in external traceability?
11. What should an apiary ID represent?
12. Why should apiary IDs remain stable?
13. Why can colony IDs be useful?
14. Why should a dead colony's code not be casually reused?
15. What queen information can support breeding traceability?
16. How should treatment records connect to food lots?
17. Why are colony-movement records relevant to origin claims?
18. What is a harvest lot?
19. Give one practical rule for defining a harvest lot.
20. What does an extraction batch record contain?
21. Why should tanks have unique IDs?
22. Why is “left tank” an inadequate identifier?
23. What is a packing lot?
24. What qualities make a good lot code?
25. Why can a date alone be insufficient as a lot code?
26. What trade-off exists when choosing lot size?
27. Which records should accompany purchased bulk honey?
28. Why should purchased honey never become anonymous after receipt?
29. When is packaging traceability useful?
30. Why should ingredient lots be tracked in flavoured products?
31. What records support a country-of-origin claim for own honey?
32. What records support origin for purchased honey?
33. What changed in EU blend-origin labelling from 14 June 2026?
34. Why do origin percentages require traceability documents?
35. Why should blend weights be recorded during the actual blending operation?
36. How is country share calculated conceptually?
37. Can lots from the same country be aggregated for label percentage while remaining separate internally?
38. What is mass balance?
39. What can unexplained mass gain suggest?
40. What can unexplained mass loss suggest?
41. Why is mass balance useful against origin fraud?
42. What is yield reconciliation?
43. What is chain of custody?
44. When is chain of custody especially important?
45. What should accompany a laboratory sample?
46. Why are retained samples useful?
47. What makes paper records reliable?
48. What are advantages of digital traceability?
49. What risks come with digital traceability?
50. What is a hybrid traceability system?
51. What is master data?
52. Why should master-data names/codes be standardised?
53. How should paper record corrections be made?
54. Why should digital systems preserve an audit trail where appropriate?
55. Why is backdating records weak practice?
56. Which details should be recorded for business-customer deliveries?
57. What should direct farmers-market sales record?
58. How can online orders be connected to food lots?
59. Why must personal data in online orders be protected?
60. What is market load/return reconciliation?
61. Why should mixed-lot wholesale orders identify each lot?
62. Why is warehouse location part of traceability?
63. What should happen to held stock identity?
64. How is rework recorded?
65. Why does downgrade not erase original lot history?
66. What should be recorded for returned product?
67. How should complaints link to traceability?
68. Which two urgent questions does traceability answer during an incident?
69. Why can weak traceability make a recall larger?
70. What roles should a recall team cover?
71. Which contacts should be prepared before a recall?
72. What is a mock recall?
73. Give one realistic mock-recall scenario.
74. Which metrics should be measured during a mock recall?
75. What is quantity reconciliation?
76. Why should recall/withdrawal decisions be documented?
77. What belongs in destruction records?
78. How does a supplier recall test internal traceability?
79. Why can packaging recalls affect several food lots?
80. How can treatment-record incidents be narrowed by traceability?
81. What should pesticide-incident tracing record?
82. How can animal-disease tracing intersect food records?
83. Why can equipment IDs help investigate metal fragments?
84. Why is cleaning traceability important with allergens?
85. What is label version control?
86. Why are best-before date and lot code different?
87. Can QR codes create traceability without a reliable database?
88. What are potential benefits of standardised trade identifiers?
89. How should privacy be balanced with traceability?
90. Why is cybersecurity a food-safety concern?
91. What makes a backup useful?
92. Why should backups be restoration-tested?
93. What determines record-retention periods?
94. Why should records be quickly retrievable?
95. What should an internal traceability audit test backward?
96. What should it test forward?
97. How can a business prove country-origin percentages after a blend is packed?
98. What is the value of reconciling a market's loaded and returned stock by lot?
99. Why is record integrity more important than collecting excessive data?
100. What does a successful mock recall demonstrate about the traceability system?

## References

- Regulation (EC) No 178/2002, especially Article 18 on food traceability and Article 19 on responsibilities for food withdrawal/recall.
- European Commission guidance on implementation of General Food Law traceability requirements.
- Food Safety Authority of Ireland. Guidance on one-step-back/one-step-forward traceability and recall systems. Current resources accessed 16 September 2026.
- Food Safety Authority of Ireland. *Guide to Food Safety Skills*, current edition, including traceability examples.
- Food Safety Authority of Ireland. *Guidance Note 10* and current traceability guidance, as applicable.
- Council Directive 2001/110/EC relating to honey, as amended by Directive (EU) 2024/1438.
- Food Safety Authority of Ireland. “Origin Labelling of Honey.” Current guidance on rules applying from 14 June 2026, including country shares supported by traceability documentation. Accessed 16 September 2026.
- Codex Alimentarius Commission. *General Principles of Food Hygiene*, current edition, for traceability/recall within food-safety management.
- Current national data-protection, tax-record, animal-health, medicine-record, food-labelling, and recall requirements should be checked for the reader’s jurisdiction.


<a id="chapter-68"></a>

# Chapter 68 — Legal Requirements

## Introduction

Beekeeping law is not one law. A commercial beekeeper can be regulated simultaneously as:

- a keeper of livestock;
- a primary food producer;
- a food processor or packer;
- a seller to consumers;
- an online trader;
- an employer;
- a vehicle operator;
- a user of veterinary medicines and chemicals;
- a data controller;
- a land user or tenant;
- a brand owner;
- a manufacturer of candles, cosmetics, supplements, or other hive products.

The exact obligations depend on country, region, business scale, product type, sales channel, employee status, premises, and whether bees or products cross borders.

For that reason, the most useful legal skill is not memorising a long list of rules. It is learning to maintain a **compliance matrix**: a living document that identifies each activity, the competent authority, the applicable rule, the evidence required, the responsible person, the renewal/review date, and the consequences of non-compliance.

This chapter uses Ireland and the European Union as current examples where useful. These examples are time-dated and must not be copied blindly into another jurisdiction.

## Learning Objectives

After completing this chapter, the reader should be able to:

- identify the main legal domains affecting a beekeeping business;
- build and maintain a compliance matrix;
- distinguish registration, approval, licence, permit, notification, certification, and voluntary scheme requirements;
- understand the relationship between primary honey production and later food processing/packing;
- identify current Irish examples for primary honey-producer registration, food-business registration, tax registration, honey-origin labelling, notifiable disease, and veterinary medicines;
- map honey-labelling and distance-selling obligations;
- identify when consumer law, privacy, employment, planning, environmental, intellectual-property, and product-liability rules may apply;
- avoid treating voluntary buyer requirements as law or legal minimums as best practice;
- know when competent professional or regulatory advice is needed;
- build evidence that compliance is actually being maintained.

## 68.1 Compliance Starts With Activities

Do not begin with a list of laws. Begin with a list of activities.

Examples:

- keeping bees;
- moving colonies;
- selling honey from the farm;
- extracting honey;
- packing honey;
- blending imported honey;
- selling online;
- attending markets;
- selling wholesale;
- collecting pollen;
- selling propolis extract;
- producing cosmetics;
- employing seasonal workers;
- exporting queens.

Each activity can trigger different legal requirements.

## 68.2 The Compliance Matrix

A useful matrix contains:

| Activity | Requirement | Authority/source | Evidence | Responsible person | Review/renewal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Primary honey production | Registration | Current competent authority | Registration confirmation | Owner | Annual review |
| Honey packing | Food hygiene/HACCP | Food authority | FSMS records | Food-safety lead | Ongoing |
| Veterinary treatment | Authorised product/label use | Veterinary-medicine authority | Treatment record | Apiary manager | Each treatment |
| Online retail | Mandatory food info/consumer law | Food/consumer authority | Product page, T&Cs | Sales lead | On legal change |

The matrix should contain links or document references to current official sources.

## 68.3 Registration, Approval, Licence, Permit, Notification

These words are not interchangeable.

- **registration** records a business/person/activity with an authority;
- **approval** may require inspection and authorisation before defined higher-risk activities;
- **licence** grants legal permission for a specific activity;
- **permit** often authorises an event/site/use;
- **notification** informs an authority without necessarily seeking prior approval;
- **certification** can be regulatory or voluntary depending on the scheme.

Know which type applies.

## 68.4 Primary Honey Production

Primary production covers production at the farm/apiary stage under food-hygiene law.

In Ireland, DAFM currently requires people producing primary honey to register as Food Business Operators. DAFM states that honey producers cannot legally sell their produce without this registration and lists records that primary producers must keep, including feed, veterinary treatments, relevant disease, analyses, official checks, and sales to anyone other than the final consumer.

This is an Irish example; other countries may use different registration systems.

## 68.5 Food Business Registration Beyond Primary Production

Extraction, processing, packing, blending, direct food service, or operating from home can fall under food-business registration requirements depending on activity and jurisdiction.

Current FSAI guidance states that food businesses in Ireland must register before operating, including home-based food businesses, with the appropriate competent authority.

Do not assume primary-producer registration automatically covers every later processing activity.

## 68.6 Competent Authority

Different authorities can oversee different aspects of one business.

Examples in Ireland can include:

- Department of Agriculture, Food and the Marine;
- Health Service Executive environmental health services;
- Food Safety Authority of Ireland oversight/guidance;
- Health Products Regulatory Authority for veterinary medicines;
- Revenue;
- local authorities;
- Data Protection Commission;
- Competition and Consumer Protection Commission;
- Companies Registration Office.

The correct authority depends on the subject.

## 68.7 Food Hygiene

Food businesses must operate hygienically.

Typical legal requirements cover:

- suitable premises;
- clean equipment;
- personal hygiene;
- safe water;
- pest control;
- waste management;
- temperature control where relevant;
- food-safety management procedures;
- traceability.

EU Regulation 852/2004 is a central food-hygiene instrument in the EU.

## 68.8 HACCP-Based Procedures

Current FSAI guidance states that food businesses should implement food-safety management procedures based on HACCP principles.

A small honey packer may use a simpler documented system than a large industrial plant, but “small” does not mean “no control”.

The system must reflect actual processes.

## 68.9 Honey Composition Law

Honey sold as honey must meet the legal definition and compositional criteria in the target market.

In the EU, the Honey Directive controls matters such as:

- definitions;
- product categories;
- moisture;
- sugars;
- free acidity;
- electrical conductivity;
- diastase;
- HMF;
- origin labelling.

Adding ingredients can mean the product may no longer be legally sold simply as “honey”.

## 68.10 Product Names

Terms such as:

- honey;
- blossom honey;
- honeydew honey;
- comb honey;
- chunk honey;
- filtered honey;
- baker's honey

can have legal definitions.

Marketing language should not conflict with the regulated product category.

## 68.11 Floral-Origin Claims

A floral-origin claim should be supported by the honey's characteristics and applicable legal criteria.

Evidence can include:

- forage records;
- harvest timing;
- pollen analysis;
- sensory characteristics;
- conductivity;
- other physicochemical markers.

“Lots of lavender near the apiary” is not automatically enough to label a honey “lavender honey”.

## 68.12 Geographical-Origin Claims

Country and regional origin claims require traceability.

Internal records should support:

- where colonies were during the relevant flow;
- source country for purchased honey;
- blend weights;
- final label.

Origin is a factual claim with legal consequences.

## 68.13 EU Honey Origin Rules From 14 June 2026

Under the amended EU rules applying from 14 June 2026, honey blends from more than one country generally require the countries of origin to be listed in descending order by weight with percentage shares, subject to the applicable legal details and tolerances.

FSAI states that these percentages are supported by operator traceability documentation.

This makes accurate blending records essential.

## 68.14 Transition Stock

Rules often include transition provisions.

FSAI notes that honey produced, packaged, labelled, and placed on the market on or before the 14 June 2026 change may continue to be sold until stocks are exhausted under the relevant transitional provision.

Always check exact dates before re-labelling old inventory.

## 68.15 General Food Information

In the EU, Regulation 1169/2011 governs mandatory food information.

Depending on product and exemption, mandatory particulars can include:

- name of food;
- ingredients where applicable;
- allergens;
- net quantity;
- durability date;
- storage/use conditions;
- responsible food-business operator;
- origin where required;
- nutrition information where applicable/exemptions permit.

Honey-specific rules interact with these general rules.

## 68.16 Lot Marking

Food products may require lot identification under applicable law.

A lot code supports recall and should correspond to internal traceability records.

Do not print arbitrary codes that nobody can decode later.

## 68.17 Net Quantity and Metrology

Packaged honey must meet applicable quantity-declaration and weights-and-measures rules.

This can involve:

- verified/calibrated scales;
- correct units;
- nominal quantity rules;
- tolerances;
- packer obligations.

Commercial scales may be subject to specific legal-metrology controls.

## 68.18 Date Marking

Honey is generally a shelf-stable food, but applicable food-information law may require a best-before date.

The business is responsible for assigning a defensible shelf life under stated storage conditions.

Do not copy a competitor's date without evidence.

## 68.19 Online Distance Selling

Selling food online does not remove labelling duties.

EU food-information rules require mandatory food information for prepacked food to be available before the purchase is concluded, except the durability date where the regulation provides different timing.

The physical product must also carry the required information at delivery.

## 68.20 Consumer Rights Online

Online traders may be subject to consumer-law requirements covering:

- trader identity;
- price;
- delivery;
- contract information;
- cancellation/returns rules;
- complaint handling;
- unfair commercial practices.

Food/perishable-product exceptions can modify standard cancellation rights.

Use current consumer-authority guidance for the target market.

## 68.21 Market and Event Permits

Farmers markets and fairs can impose:

- stall application;
- local-authority permission;
- organiser rules;
- food-business evidence;
- public liability insurance;
- canopy/fire/electrical rules;
- sampling requirements.

A market organiser's contract can be stricter than the statutory minimum.

## 68.22 Voluntary Requirements Versus Law

Retailers or markets may require:

- higher insurance limits;
- third-party certification;
- barcodes;
- tamper evidence;
- laboratory certificates;
- sustainability documentation.

These can be commercially mandatory without being statutory law.

Label them correctly in the compliance matrix.

## 68.23 Tax Registration

Tax obligations depend on business structure and jurisdiction.

In Ireland, Revenue states that people becoming sole traders, partnerships/trusts, or companies must register for relevant tax. Sole traders can register through myAccount/ROS according to current eligibility and must maintain business records.

Tax registration is separate from food-business registration.

## 68.24 Sole Trader, Partnership, Company

Business structure affects:

- tax;
- liability;
- accounts;
- governance;
- access to investment;
- succession;
- administration.

Do not choose a legal form only because another beekeeper uses it.

## 68.25 Business Name

Trading under a business name different from the owner's legal name can trigger business-name registration requirements.

In Ireland, Revenue points traders to the Companies Registration Office for business-name registration where applicable.

Trade-mark protection is separate from business-name registration.

## 68.26 VAT

VAT treatment depends on:

- turnover;
- goods/services;
- jurisdiction;
- registration thresholds;
- customer location;
- cross-border sales.

Never hard-code a VAT threshold in a long-lived handbook without a date. Check current Revenue/tax-authority guidance.

## 68.27 Accounting Records

Tax law can require business records for defined retention periods.

Keep:

- sales;
- purchases;
- invoices;
- bank records;
- payroll where relevant;
- capital assets;
- stock records.

Food traceability and tax records can support one another but have different purposes.

## 68.28 Employment Law

Hiring workers can trigger obligations for:

- contracts/terms;
- pay;
- working time;
- leave;
- payroll tax;
- safety;
- anti-discrimination;
- records.

Seasonal harvest workers are still workers under the applicable legal relationship.

## 68.29 Employee Versus Contractor

A written label does not always determine legal employment status.

Authorities can look at the real relationship:

- control;
- independence;
- financial risk;
- integration;
- substitution;
- equipment provision.

Misclassification can create tax and employment liabilities.

## 68.30 Workplace Safety

Beekeeping creates occupational risks including:

- stings/anaphylaxis;
- manual handling;
- vehicle use;
- hot wax;
- acids/miticides;
- blades;
- centrifuges;
- electricity;
- working alone;
- remote apiaries.

Workplace-safety law applies according to jurisdiction and employment structure.

## 68.31 Risk Assessment

Businesses may be required to identify workplace hazards and implement controls.

A practical risk assessment includes:

- task;
- hazard;
- exposed people;
- likelihood/severity;
- controls;
- residual risk;
- responsible person;
- review date.

## 68.32 Anaphylaxis Planning

Bee-sting allergy can be life-threatening.

A workplace plan should address:

- emergency communication;
- worker-declared medical needs;
- remote access;
- first-aid arrangements;
- ambulance directions;
- lone-working policy.

Medical prescriptions and personal emergency medication remain matters for qualified healthcare professionals.

## 68.33 Vehicle Law

Commercial beekeeping may use vans, trailers, forklifts, or trucks.

Relevant rules can cover:

- driver licensing;
- vehicle tax;
- insurance;
- roadworthiness;
- load security;
- trailer limits;
- working-time/tachograph regimes at larger scales.

Do not overload a vehicle because hive boxes “fit”.

## 68.34 Load Security

Unsecured hives or honey drums can injure road users and employees.

Use appropriate:

- straps;
- pallets;
- load distribution;
- container closure;
- vehicle capacity.

Road-safety obligations exist independently of bee welfare.

## 68.35 Planning and Home Businesses

Operating extraction/packing from home may trigger planning, building, wastewater, or nuisance requirements depending on scale and local authority.

Current Irish public guidance notes that changes in use of a property can require planning permission.

Check before investing in a permanent honey room.

## 68.36 Landowner Agreements

Apiaries on third-party land should have a clear agreement covering:

- location;
- access;
- payment/barter;
- liability;
- pesticide communication;
- duration;
- removal;
- gate/security rules.

Written agreement reduces conflict.

## 68.37 Nuisance and Neighbours

Local law can address nuisance, trespass, public safety, or planning even where there is no bee-specific ordinance.

Manage:

- flight paths;
- water;
- defensive colonies;
- swarming;
- public access.

Good neighbour management reduces legal risk.

## 68.38 Animal-Health Law

Bee diseases and pests can be notifiable or regulated.

Legal consequences can include:

- mandatory reporting;
- movement restrictions;
- inspection;
- sampling;
- destruction or treatment orders;
- import/export restrictions.

Check current disease lists.

## 68.39 Irish Example: American Foulbrood

Ireland's animal-health surveillance system lists infection with *Paenibacillus larvae* (American foulbrood) as notifiable.

A suspected case therefore has legal implications beyond ordinary colony management.

Do not move or conceal suspect material.

## 68.40 Exotic Pests

Pests such as *Tropilaelaps* or small hive beetle can be subject to different legal rules depending on regional status.

An organism that is managed routinely in one country may trigger official containment in another.

Never copy pest-control advice across borders without checking status.

## 68.41 Bee Imports and Exports

Moving queens, packages, colonies, or bumble bees across borders can require:

- health certification;
- advance notification;
- approved origin;
- TRACES or equivalent systems;
- border inspection;
- pest restrictions.

DAFM maintains current import/export guidance for Ireland.

## 68.42 Veterinary Medicines

Veterinary medicines must be legally authorised/supplied and used according to applicable rules.

Current HPRA databases list medicines authorised for animals in Ireland, with changes to indications, withdrawal periods, and availability updated over time.

Always check current product information rather than relying on an old treatment calendar.

## 68.43 Off-Label and Unauthorised Products

Do not improvise treatment with agricultural chemicals or unregistered substances because they are cheaper or available online.

Risks include:

- illegal use;
- residues;
- bee toxicity;
- operator injury;
- resistance;
- rejected honey.

## 68.44 Medicine Supply Channels

Veterinary-medicine supply classifications can determine where products may be purchased.

Internet availability does not prove legal supply.

Verify authorised retailers and the medicine's current supply category.

## 68.45 Medicine Records

Food-producing-animal medicine records can be legally required.

Record at least what the competent authority requires, potentially including:

- product;
- quantity;
- date;
- animal/colony group;
- supplier;
- administrator;
- withdrawal period.

DAFM explicitly lists treatment records among primary honey-producer responsibilities in Ireland.

## 68.46 Pesticide Law

Agricultural pesticide use is regulated separately from veterinary hive treatment.

Beekeepers may need to understand:

- approved uses;
- notification/communication systems;
- incident reporting;
- residue limits;
- sampling.

Do not confront suspected misuse at the expense of preserving evidence and safety.

## 68.47 Biocides

Disinfectants, rodenticides, insecticides, and wood treatments can fall under biocide regulation.

A product approved for a building is not automatically suitable inside a food-processing area or hive.

Use according to authorised conditions.

## 68.48 Organic Certification

Organic beekeeping is a regulated certification system in many jurisdictions, not a self-declared marketing style.

Requirements can cover:

- apiary location;
- wax;
- feeding;
- veterinary treatment;
- hive materials;
- conversion period;
- inspection;
- records.

Chapter 70 covers organic beekeeping in detail.

## 68.49 “Raw” Claims

“Raw honey” is not defined identically in every jurisdiction.

If used, the claim should be:

- truthful;
- non-misleading;
- supported by actual processing history;
- compatible with local legal interpretation.

Avoid implying that legally processed honey is unhealthy.

## 68.50 “Local” Claims

“Local” can mean different distances to different consumers.

Define and substantiate it.

Examples:

- harvested within county X;
- harvested within 30 km;
- produced and packed on one farm.

Do not import honey and describe it as local merely because it was packed locally.

## 68.51 Health Claims

Food health claims are regulated.

In the EU, Regulation 1924/2006 controls nutrition and health claims on foods.

Current FSAI guidance notes that there are no authorised health claims for honey. Therefore, statements that honey treats, prevents, cures, or meaningfully reduces disease risk should not be invented for marketing.

## 68.52 Medicinal Claims

A product promoted as treating disease may become subject to medicine law.

This is especially relevant for:

- propolis;
- bee venom;
- royal jelly;
- pollen supplements.

Research evidence does not itself grant marketing authorisation.

## 68.53 Food Supplements

Pollen, propolis, royal jelly, or combinations may be sold as supplements in some markets.

Requirements can include:

- food-supplement notification;
- permitted ingredients;
- maximum levels;
- labelling;
- health claims;
- contaminant limits.

The finished product category matters more than the fact that the ingredient came from a hive.

## 68.54 Cosmetics

Beeswax, honey, royal jelly, and propolis can be used in cosmetics.

Cosmetic law can require:

- responsible person;
- safety assessment;
- product information file;
- ingredient nomenclature;
- notification;
- GMP;
- claim substantiation.

A balm made in a kitchen is not exempt merely because ingredients are natural.

## 68.55 Candles

Beeswax candles are not foods, but other product-safety, fire-safety, consumer, labelling, and chemical rules can apply.

Fragrances and colourants can add classification or hazard-information requirements.

Commercial candles should be safety-tested appropriately.

## 68.56 Bee Venom

Bee venom is a high-risk specialised product.

Raw collected venom should not be represented as an injectable medicine.

Medical venom immunotherapy uses standardised medicinal products under healthcare supervision, not beekeeper-collected raw material.

Electrical collection equipment also creates workplace-safety obligations.

## 68.57 Data Protection

Customer, employee, supplier, and newsletter data can be personal data.

Data-protection law can require:

- lawful basis;
- transparent notice;
- data minimisation;
- access/security controls;
- retention policy;
- rights handling;
- breach response.

Collecting an email for delivery does not automatically permit unlimited marketing.

## 68.58 Cookies and Digital Marketing

Websites and advertising can trigger privacy/e-communications requirements for:

- cookies;
- analytics;
- remarketing;
- newsletters;
- consent.

Use current privacy authority guidance rather than copying another site's cookie banner.

## 68.59 Trade Marks

A business name, logo, or product name can be protected by trade-mark law when requirements are met.

Before investment:

- search conflicts;
- choose relevant classes;
- decide territories;
- register where justified.

Business-name registration is not the same as trade-mark protection.

## 68.60 Copyright

Copyright can protect original:

- photography;
- label artwork;
- website copy;
- illustrations;
- manuals.

Do not copy competitor photos or online graphics merely because they are publicly visible.

## 68.61 Image and Music Licensing

Social media and advertising require rights to images, music, fonts, and other creative assets.

Platform access to a track does not necessarily grant every commercial advertising use.

Maintain asset licences/provenance.

## 68.62 Product Liability

Food producers can be liable for harm caused by defective products.

Ireland's Liability for Defective Products Act implements strict/no-fault product-liability principles in defined circumstances and includes primary agricultural products following amendment.

Insurance can help transfer financial risk but does not remove legal responsibility.

## 68.63 Negligence and Duty of Care

Separate civil liability can arise from negligence, for example:

- unsafe public apiary access;
- market canopy injury;
- contaminated product;
- unsafe equipment demonstration.

Reasonable prevention and records matter.

## 68.64 Contracts

Contracts with:

- retailers;
- wholesalers;
- landowners;
- pollination clients;
- co-packers;
- couriers;
- event organisers

can create obligations beyond legislation.

Review:

- product specification;
- delivery;
- rejection;
- insurance;
- indemnities;
- recalls;
- payment;
- jurisdiction.

## 68.65 Retailer Specifications

A retailer may require:

- lower moisture than law;
- higher insurance limits;
- third-party certification;
- barcode standards;
- lab testing;
- pallet configuration.

These are contract requirements, not necessarily law.

## 68.66 Consumer Guarantees

Consumer law can require goods to match description, be of acceptable quality, and comply with contract.

Claims such as “Irish honey”, “organic”, “500 g”, or “raw” become part of consumer expectations and legal exposure.

## 68.67 Pricing Law

Retail price displays and promotional price claims can be regulated.

Avoid:

- hidden mandatory charges;
- fake discounts;
- misleading “was/now” pricing;
- inconsistent online checkout totals.

## 68.68 Advertising Law

Advertising must be truthful and not misleading.

Evidence should support objective claims about:

- origin;
- health;
- sustainability;
- awards;
- ingredients;
- production method.

Marketing creativity does not create an exception to law.

## 68.69 Environmental Law

Beekeeping businesses can generate:

- wastewater;
- wax debris;
- chemical containers;
- batteries;
- packaging waste;
- veterinary-medicine waste.

Disposal rules differ by material and jurisdiction.

Do not dump honey-rich wastewater where it attracts bees or pollutes water.

## 68.70 Waste Packaging

Larger food businesses can be subject to packaging producer-responsibility schemes, recycling obligations, or reporting.

Even small producers should design packaging with disposal and compliance in mind.

## 68.71 Waste Veterinary Medicines

Expired or unused veterinary products may require controlled disposal.

Do not pour medicines into drains or dispose of contaminated strips where bees or wildlife can access them.

Follow product and authority instructions.

## 68.72 Wastewater

Honey, detergent, and wax-rich wastewater can have high organic load.

Local sewer/septic/environmental rules may limit disposal method.

Plan wash-water management before scaling extraction.

## 68.73 Planning and Building Safety

A larger processing site can require:

- planning approval;
- fire safety;
- building standards;
- accessibility;
- drainage;
- workplace facilities.

Food approval does not automatically satisfy building law.

## 68.74 Fire Safety

Hot wax, ethanol propolis extraction, electrical extractors, and packaging stock create fire risks.

Fire-law obligations can include:

- emergency routes;
- extinguishers;
- flammable-liquid storage;
- staff training;
- electrical inspection.

Do not scale solvent extraction in an ordinary unassessed room.

## 68.75 Insurance Is Not a Legal Checklist

Some insurance is legally compulsory in certain contexts; other cover is prudent or contractually required.

Chapter 69 explains the distinction.

Do not assume “insured” means “legally compliant”.

## 68.76 International Trade

Import/export can trigger:

- customs;
- VAT;
- sanitary certification;
- animal-health controls;
- origin documentation;
- tariffs;
- labelling for destination market.

Rules for live bees are often stricter than rules for packaged honey.

## 68.77 Exporting Honey

Before export, check:

- destination food standard;
- label language;
- importer registration;
- certificates;
- residue requirements;
- customs classification;
- origin rules.

An EU-compliant label is not automatically compliant in a non-EU market.

## 68.78 Importing Honey

Importers carry responsibility for ensuring imported food meets applicable law.

Supplier claims should be supported by:

- origin records;
- laboratory analysis;
- certificates;
- customs/import documents;
- authenticity controls.

## 68.79 Importing Bees

Live bee imports carry animal-health risk and can require advance notification, certification, and approved routes.

DAFM maintains current Irish import information including TRACES-related requirements.

## 68.80 Selling Queens and Nuclei

Bee sales can trigger:

- animal-health movement rules;
- disease certification;
- business/tax obligations;
- consumer/contract law;
- transport requirements.

Keep origin and health records.

## 68.81 Pollination Services

Pollination contracts should define:

- colony strength;
- delivery/removal;
- pesticide communication;
- access;
- liability;
- payment;
- colony loss;
- disease obligations.

A handshake can leave expensive ambiguity.

## 68.82 Inspections

Competent authorities can inspect food businesses, records, premises, products, or animal-health status according to their powers.

Prepare by maintaining compliance continuously, not by cleaning paperwork the night before.

## 68.83 Enforcement

Potential actions can include:

- advice/warnings;
- corrective orders;
- product detention;
- withdrawal/recall;
- fines;
- prosecution;
- licence/approval consequences.

Exact enforcement powers vary.

## 68.84 Record Requests

Authorities may require records quickly.

Keep:

- registration documents;
- treatment records;
- food-safety records;
- traceability;
- laboratory results;
- supplier/customer records;
- employee/training records where applicable.

## 68.85 Legal Change Management

Assign responsibility for monitoring:

- authority newsletters;
- official websites;
- industry alerts;
- changes in product authorisations;
- label rules;
- tax thresholds.

Record the date the compliance matrix was reviewed.

## 68.86 Do Not Rely on Old Labels

A product label that was legal three years ago may be obsolete.

Trigger label review when:

- law changes;
- recipe changes;
- supplier changes;
- origin changes;
- business address changes;
- health claims change.

## 68.87 Compliance Decision Table

| Planned activity | Main legal questions |
|---|---|
| Sell own honey direct | Producer/FBO registration, hygiene, label, lot, tax, insurance |
| Pack purchased honey | Food-business scope, supplier traceability, origin, composition, authenticity |
| Blend multi-country honey | Traceability, country percentages, label, mass balance |
| Sell online | Food info before purchase, consumer law, privacy, delivery |
| Sell pollen supplement | Food/supplement status, contaminants, claims, notification where required |
| Sell propolis “for infection” | Potential medicinal claim; regulatory classification |
| Operate from home | Food registration, premises hygiene, planning/use, waste |
| Import queens | Animal-health certification/notification/import rules |
| Employ harvest worker | Payroll, employment law, safety, insurance |
| Produce cosmetic balm | Cosmetics regulation, safety assessment, GMP, claims |

## 68.88 Practical Compliance-Matrix Procedure

1. List every product and service.
2. List every location and sales channel.
3. List every worker/contractor role.
4. For each activity, identify regulatory domains.
5. Find current official authority sources.
6. Record registration/licence/notification requirements.
7. Record evidence and renewal/review dates.
8. Assign responsible person.
9. Build legal-change monitoring.
10. Audit the matrix at least annually and whenever the business changes.

## 68.89 Compliance Checklist

- [ ] Primary-producer/FBO status checked.
- [ ] Food-business registration/approval scope confirmed.
- [ ] HACCP/hygiene system current.
- [ ] Honey composition standard current.
- [ ] Label template reviewed against current law.
- [ ] 2026 EU origin requirements applied where relevant.
- [ ] Lot coding/traceability compliant.
- [ ] Online food information present before purchase where required.
- [ ] Consumer terms and returns current.
- [ ] Tax registration/business structure current.
- [ ] VAT position reviewed using current thresholds.
- [ ] Employment/payroll duties checked.
- [ ] Workplace risk assessment current.
- [ ] Vehicle/load rules checked.
- [ ] Planning/premises requirements checked.
- [ ] Disease notification list current.
- [ ] Bee import/export rules current.
- [ ] Veterinary medicines verified in current authority database.
- [ ] Treatment records complete.
- [ ] Health/medical marketing claims checked.
- [ ] Food supplement/cosmetic status checked where relevant.
- [ ] Privacy/cookie/email rules addressed.
- [ ] Trade-mark/IP assets reviewed.
- [ ] Wastewater/chemical disposal controlled.
- [ ] Insurance and contract requirements distinguished from law.
- [ ] Compliance matrix has owner and review date.

## Management Notes

### Legal compliance is evidence-based

It is not enough to believe a requirement was met. Registration confirmations, records, approved labels, invoices, training records, and treatment logs are evidence.

### Regulators classify activities, not self-descriptions

Calling a business a “hobby” does not necessarily exempt commercial sales. Calling a contractor self-employed does not decide employment status. Calling a balm “natural” does not exempt cosmetics law.

### One business can have several regulatory identities

The beekeeper may simultaneously be a farmer, food business operator, online trader, employer, data controller, and cosmetics responsible person. The compliance matrix makes these identities visible.

### Legal minimum and best practice differ

The law defines a floor. Retailer specifications, insurance requirements, or quality systems can legitimately require more.

### Laws and authorisations change

The 2026 EU honey-origin change and ongoing veterinary-medicine database updates show why a publication must teach source checking and dated compliance review.

## Key Takeaways

- Beekeeping law is a network of legal domains rather than one beekeeper statute.
- Start compliance work by listing activities, products, locations, workers, and channels.
- Registration, approval, licence, notification, and certification are different legal concepts.
- Primary honey production and later processing/packing can have different registration requirements.
- Honey has specific compositional and labelling law in addition to general food law.
- EU blend-origin requirements changed from 14 June 2026 and depend on traceable percentage shares.
- Tax, employment, planning, vehicle, privacy, trade-mark, environmental, and product-liability rules can all apply.
- Veterinary-medicine authorisation and disease status must be checked from current official sources.
- Health and medicinal claims can change a product's legal status.
- A living compliance matrix is more reliable than memorising rules from a book.

## Chapter Summary

A beekeeping business can cross food, animal-health, tax, employment, consumer, environmental, privacy, intellectual-property, and product-safety law. The correct response is not fear or bureaucracy for its own sake; it is systematic mapping.

The compliance matrix turns a complex legal environment into manageable tasks. Each activity is linked to the current competent authority, requirement, evidence, responsible person, and review date. This prevents assumptions such as “I registered as a beekeeper, therefore my packing room is covered” or “the product is natural, therefore health claims are allowed”.

Ireland provides useful current examples. Primary honey producers register with DAFM; food businesses must identify the correct registration authority; AFB is notifiable; veterinary medicines should be checked in the current HPRA/Union databases; Revenue manages tax registration; and the 2026 EU honey rules require more detailed origin declarations for blends. These examples will change over time and are not universal.

The enduring skill is source discipline: use current official sources, document decisions, distinguish law from contracts and voluntary certification, and obtain professional advice when risk or complexity exceeds the business's competence.

6. What is the difference between a licence and notification?
7. What is certification?
8. Why can primary production and processing have different legal requirements?

10. Which types of records does DAFM list for primary honey producers?
11. What does current FSAI guidance say about food-business registration?
12. Why should a home food business still check registration requirements?
13. What is a competent authority?

42. What legal domains appear when workers are hired?
43. Why can a worker's real status differ from the contract label?
44. Which beekeeping hazards belong in workplace safety?
45. What is a workplace risk assessment?
46. Why should apiary work include an anaphylaxis emergency plan?
47. What vehicle rules can apply to commercial beekeeping?
48. Why is load security a legal and safety issue?

50. What should a landowner apiary agreement address?
51. How can nuisance law affect apiary management?
52. What can notifiable disease law require?
53. Is American foulbrood notifiable in Ireland according to current DAFM surveillance information?
54. Why can an exotic pest be regulated differently between countries?
55. Which controls can apply when importing live bees?
56. Why should current DAFM import/export guidance be checked?
57. Where can authorised veterinary medicines for animals in Ireland be checked?
58. Why should withdrawal periods be checked from current product information?
59. Why is an agricultural chemical not automatically a legal hive treatment?
60. Why can online availability fail to prove legal veterinary-medicine supply?
61. Which medicine records can primary honey producers need?
62. How does pesticide law differ from veterinary-medicine law?

64. Why is a building disinfectant not automatically suitable in a hive?

67. How can “local” be made more defensible?

69. How can medicinal claims change product category?
70. Why can pollen or propolis become food supplements subject to separate rules?
71. What legal controls can apply to cosmetics?
72. Why is “natural” not an exemption from cosmetics law?
73. Why can candle production have separate product-safety issues?
74. Why must raw bee venom not be presented as standardised injectable medicine?
75. What is personal data?

77. Does obtaining an email for delivery automatically permit marketing use?
78. Why can cookies and analytics create legal obligations?

81. What does copyright protect in a honey brand?
82. Why must image/music licences be documented?
83. What is product liability?
84. What principle does Irish defective-product law implement in defined circumstances?
85. Why does insurance not remove product liability?
86. What can contracts add beyond legal minimums?
87. Why should retailer specifications be distinguished from law?
