- Current national veterinary-medicine, food-safety, pesticide-residue and animal-health requirements must be checked independently of organic certification.


<a id="chapter-71"></a>

# Chapter 71 — Biodiversity

## Introduction

Biodiversity means the variety of life and the relationships that allow living systems to function. In an apiary landscape it includes much more than honey bees. It includes wild bees, hoverflies, butterflies, moths, beetles, wasps, plants, fungi, soil organisms, birds, mammals, microbes, parasites, predators, and the genetic diversity within populations.

Managed honey bees are important livestock and pollinators, but the number of managed colonies is not a direct measure of biodiversity. A landscape containing thousands of honey-bee colonies but few wild pollinator species, little native vegetation, simplified soils, and few nesting sites can still have low biodiversity.

This distinction is essential for responsible beekeeping. Honey-bee conservation, commercial colony health, crop pollination, and wild-pollinator conservation overlap, but they are not identical objectives. Actions that benefit one group can benefit others, have neutral effects, or—under some conditions—create competition or disease-transmission pressure.

The beekeeper's strongest biodiversity contribution is usually not “put more hives everywhere”. It is to manage colonies responsibly while improving habitat: continuous forage, nesting resources, hedgerows, flower-rich margins, low pesticide exposure, clean water, diverse plant communities, and connected patches of semi-natural land.

## Learning Objectives

After completing this chapter, the reader should be able to:

- explain biodiversity at genetic, species, habitat, ecosystem, and landscape levels;
- distinguish managed honey-bee abundance from wild-pollinator biodiversity;
- explain why genetic diversity matters within honey-bee populations;
- assess floral diversity and seasonal forage continuity;
- identify nesting resources required by different wild pollinators;
- understand the concept of landscape connectivity;
- evaluate carrying capacity and apiary density cautiously;
- describe evidence and uncertainty surrounding competition between managed honey bees and wild pollinators;
- recognise disease spillover/spillback, invasive species, pesticides, land-use change, and climate as biodiversity pressures;
- develop practical biodiversity actions and monitoring at apiary/farm scale.

## 71.1 Levels of Biodiversity

Biodiversity can be considered at several levels.

### Genetic diversity

Variation within a species or population.

### Species diversity

The number and relative abundance of species.

### Habitat diversity

Variation in nesting, feeding, shelter, soil, water, woodland, grassland, hedgerow, wetland, and other habitats.

### Ecosystem and landscape diversity

The mosaic of habitats and ecological processes across a larger area.

Healthy landscapes depend on interactions across all these levels.

## 71.2 Honey Bees Are One Pollinator Species

The western honey bee, *Apis mellifera*, is a highly social species managed by humans across much of the world.

Other pollinators include:

- bumble bees;
- solitary bees;
- hoverflies;
- butterflies;
- moths;
- beetles;
- wasps;
- flies;
- vertebrate pollinators in some regions.

A biodiversity plan should recognise this broader community.

## 71.3 Managed Livestock Versus Wild Populations

A managed honey-bee colony is comparable in some respects to managed livestock: it is owned, moved, bred, fed, treated, and stocked by humans.

Wild pollinators must find their own:

- food;
- nests;
- mates;
- overwintering sites.

Increasing managed livestock does not automatically conserve wild species.

## 71.4 Why Honey Bees Still Matter to Biodiversity

Honey bees contribute to:

- pollination of many crops;
- reproduction of numerous wild plants;
- ecological monitoring through hive products and foraging data;
- public engagement with insects;
- agricultural livelihoods.

The point is not to diminish honey bees. It is to place them accurately within a larger ecological network.

## 71.5 Genetic Diversity Within Honey Bees

Honey-bee populations contain genetic variation affecting:

- disease resistance;
- Varroa response;
- climate adaptation;
- swarming;
- brood timing;
- temperament;
- productivity;
- overwintering.

Maintaining diversity reduces the risk that every colony responds identically to one stressor.

## 71.6 Local Adaptation

Locally adapted bee populations can have advantages in:

- seasonal timing;
- forage use;
- wintering;
- climate response;
- disease ecology.

However, “local” should not become an excuse for extremely small closed populations that lose genetic variation.

## 71.7 Inbreeding

Excessive inbreeding can reduce colony fitness and, because of honey-bee sex determination, can increase diploid drones and brood loss under some mating structures.

A breeding programme should preserve a sufficiently broad genetic base.

## 71.8 Genetic Conservation and Commercial Breeding

Commercial selection can improve useful traits while conservation protects population diversity.

Good breeding balances:

- selection intensity;
- genetic gain;
- inbreeding;
- local adaptation;
- queen availability;
- disease resistance.

The fastest short-term selection is not always the best long-term population strategy.

## 71.9 Plant Diversity

Pollinator diversity depends strongly on plant diversity.

Important dimensions include:

- number of flowering species;
- abundance;
- bloom duration;
- flower shapes;
- nectar quality;
- pollen quality;
- spatial distribution.

One enormous monoculture can provide abundance for a short period while leaving long dearths.

## 71.10 Forage Continuity

A resilient landscape supplies flowers across the whole active season.

A forage calendar should identify gaps between:

- early spring;
- main spring bloom;
- early summer;
- late summer;
- autumn.

Closing a two-week dearth can be more valuable than adding more flowers during an already abundant peak.

## 71.11 Early-Season Forage

Early forage supports:

- queen laying;
- brood growth;
- bumble-bee queens;
- early solitary bees.

Useful plants depend on region and can include:

- willow;
- fruit trees;
- dandelion;
- early shrubs;
- native woodland flowers.

Select locally appropriate species.

## 71.12 Late-Season Forage

Late forage supports:

- winter-bee preparation;
- bumble-bee colonies;
- late solitary bees;
- hoverflies.

Examples can include:

- ivy in parts of Europe;
- late clovers;
- knapweeds;
- native asters and other regional plants.

Avoid planting invasive species simply because bees visit them.

## 71.13 Native Plants

Native plants often support local insect networks beyond nectar alone.

They may provide:

- larval food;
- specialist pollen;
- nesting stems;
- host relationships;
- seeds for birds.

Non-native ornamentals can also provide useful nectar, but habitat design should not be reduced to flower abundance alone.

## 71.14 Floral Quality

Not all pollen is nutritionally equivalent.

Pollen varies in:

- protein;
- amino-acid balance;
- lipids;
- sterols;
- micronutrients;
- secondary compounds.

Diverse diets can reduce dependence on one nutritionally incomplete source.

## 71.15 Nesting Habitat

Wild bees need nests as well as flowers.

Different species use:

- bare soil;
- sandy banks;
- dead wood;
- hollow stems;
- beetle holes;
- cavities;
- tussocky vegetation;
- abandoned rodent nests.

A flower-rich site with no nesting resources may still be poor habitat.

## 71.16 Ground-Nesting Bees

Many solitary bees nest in the ground.

Helpful conditions can include:

- patches of bare or sparsely vegetated soil;
- well-drained banks;
- undisturbed sunny ground;
- low soil compaction in suitable areas.

Covering every surface with dense grass or mulch removes potential nesting habitat.

## 71.17 Cavity-Nesting Bees

Some solitary bees use:

- hollow stems;
- dead wood;
- holes in masonry or timber;
- artificial nesting blocks.

Artificial “bee hotels” can be educational but require maintenance and should not replace natural habitat.

## 71.18 Bee Hotels Are Not a Complete Conservation Strategy

Poorly managed artificial nests can accumulate:

- parasites;
- pathogens;
- mould;
- predators.

Use them selectively, clean/replace materials appropriately, and prioritise natural nesting features.

## 71.19 Bumble-Bee Nesting

Bumble bees may use:

- tussocky grass;
- old mammal burrows;
- cavities;
- compost-like material;
- sheltered banks.

Overtidy management removes these spaces.

## 71.20 Hoverflies Need Different Resources

Hoverfly larvae have diverse ecologies.

Depending on species they may live in:

- aphid colonies;
- wet organic matter;
- ponds;
- plant tissues;
- decaying wood.

Pollinator conservation must therefore include habitats beyond flowers.

## 71.21 Butterflies and Moths

Adult nectar flowers are only one requirement.

Larvae may depend on specific host plants.

Removing “weeds” that are larval food plants can reduce populations even when ornamental nectar flowers remain.

## 71.22 Landscape Connectivity

Small habitat patches are more valuable when organisms can move between them.

Connectivity can be improved through:

- hedgerows;
- road verges;
- field margins;
- river corridors;
- gardens;
- parks;
- flowering transport corridors.

Connectivity is especially important for short-ranging species.

## 71.23 The 200-Metre Vision in Ireland

The All-Ireland Pollinator Plan 2026–2030 promotes a vision of “pockets of nature every 200 metres”.

The idea reflects the limited foraging ranges of many small solitary bees and aims to create a connected landscape.

The plan is a voluntary evidence-based framework, not a statutory law.

## 71.24 Current Irish Pollinator Context

The National Biodiversity Data Centre reports around 100 wild bee species on the island of Ireland and states that about one-third are at risk of extinction.

This illustrates why pollinator conservation cannot be measured only by the number of managed honey-bee hives.

## 71.25 Hedgerows

Well-managed hedgerows can provide:

- flowers;
- nesting structure;
- shelter;
- movement corridors;
- overwintering sites.

Cutting every hedge hard every year can reduce flowering wood.

Management should follow local ecological and legal guidance.

## 71.26 Field Margins

Margins can support biodiversity when they contain:

- diverse native flowering plants;
- tussocky grass;
- undisturbed soil;
- low pesticide drift.

They can also become dominated by a few invasive or nutrient-loving species if poorly managed.

## 71.27 Meadow Management

Flower-rich grassland usually depends on low nutrient input and appropriate mowing/grazing.

Repeated fertilisation can favour vigorous grasses over wildflowers.

Removing cut material can help reduce soil fertility over time where that is the restoration goal.

## 71.28 Mowing Regime

Reducing mowing frequency can allow flowers to bloom and insects to complete life cycles.

However, “never mow anything” is not universally appropriate.

Management can require:

- timed cuts;
- rotational refuges;
- safety visibility;
- control of invasive plants.

## 71.29 Leave Some Winter Structure

Dead stems and seed heads can provide:

- overwintering cavities;
- shelter;
- seeds;
- structural habitat.

Avoid cutting every patch to ground level before winter.

## 71.30 Water

Pollinators need safe water and moist habitats.

Honey bees can use shallow water with landing surfaces.

Wetland edges support many hoverflies and other insects.

Avoid stagnant contaminated water near processing or livestock waste.

## 71.31 Soil

Soil is a biodiversity habitat.

Important considerations include:

- compaction;
- organic matter;
- pesticide residues;
- drainage;
- bare nesting patches;
- microbial diversity.

Pollinator conservation is partly soil conservation.

## 71.32 Pesticides

Pesticides can affect biodiversity through:

- direct toxicity;
- sublethal effects;
- loss of flowering weeds;
- contamination of pollen/nectar;
- aquatic impacts;
- indirect food-web change.

Risk depends on active ingredient, dose, timing, formulation, route, and exposure.

## 71.33 Insecticides

Insecticides are designed to affect insects and therefore deserve particular pollinator-risk attention.

Reduce exposure through:

- integrated pest management;
- avoiding application to flowering plants when prohibited or unsafe;
- following labels;
- drift reduction;
- communication with beekeepers.

## 71.34 Herbicides

Herbicides can reduce floral resources even without direct bee toxicity.

This indirect habitat effect is often overlooked.

Use targeted vegetation management rather than automatically eliminating every non-crop flower.

## 71.35 Fungicides

Fungicides are often considered less acutely toxic to bees than insecticides, but some can interact with bee physiology, nutrition, or other chemicals.

Do not assume “fungicide” means ecologically irrelevant.

## 71.36 Mixtures

Real landscapes expose organisms to mixtures.

Laboratory results for one compound do not automatically predict every field mixture.

Avoid unsupported claims of either complete safety or inevitable catastrophe.

## 71.37 Habitat Simplification

Large areas dominated by few plant species reduce:

- diet diversity;
- nesting diversity;
- refuge;
- season-long forage.

Restoring structural and floral heterogeneity can improve resilience.

## 71.38 Land-Use Change

Biodiversity can be lost through:

- urban expansion;
- drainage;
- hedgerow removal;
- intensive mowing;
- afforestation with low-diversity systems;
- conversion of semi-natural grassland.

Beekeepers can advocate for habitat without pretending hives compensate for habitat destruction.

## 71.39 Climate Change

Climate change can alter:

- flowering time;
- pollinator emergence;
- drought;
- heat stress;
- rainfall;
- forage quality;
- disease/pest ranges.

Responses differ among species and regions.

## 71.40 Phenological Mismatch

A mismatch occurs when interacting species shift seasonal timing at different rates.

Examples can include:

- flowers blooming before specialist pollinators emerge;
- bumble-bee queens emerging before adequate forage;
- honey-bee brood expansion missing a major bloom.

Current research shows widespread phenological shifts, while the magnitude and consequences of mismatch vary among systems.

## 71.41 Extreme Weather

Heat waves, drought, storms, and late frost can affect biodiversity more abruptly than gradual average warming.

Habitat diversity can provide refuges and alternative forage after local failures.

## 71.42 Invasive Plants

Some invasive plants provide abundant nectar.

That does not automatically justify promoting them.

They can:

- displace native flora;
- change soils;
- alter habitat structure;
- reduce specialist resources.

Follow invasive-species guidance.

## 71.43 Invasive Pollinator Pests

Invasive pests such as *Tropilaelaps* can threaten managed honey bees and potentially alter pathogen dynamics in the wider pollinator community.

Biosecurity is therefore part of biodiversity protection.

## 71.44 Disease Spillover and Spillback

**Spillover** can occur when pathogens move from a managed host reservoir into wild species.

**Spillback** can occur when infection circulates through wild hosts and returns to managed populations.

Evidence varies by pathogen and system, but high host density increases opportunities for transmission.

## 71.45 Healthy Managed Colonies Reduce Risk

Beekeepers can reduce disease pressure by:

- controlling Varroa;
- not allowing collapsing colonies to be robbed;
- inspecting introduced bees;
- reducing drifting where practical;
- following movement rules;
- not placing obviously sick colonies in conservation sites.

## 71.46 Competition for Floral Resources

Managed honey bees and wild pollinators can use the same nectar and pollen resources.

Whether this becomes harmful competition depends on:

- colony density;
- floral abundance;
- season;
- species;
- spatial scale;
- habitat quality;
- drought/dearth.

The evidence is context-dependent rather than a simple universal yes/no.

## 71.47 Resource Depletion

A large apiary contains tens or hundreds of thousands of foragers.

During scarce periods, this can substantially increase demand on local flowers.

Sensitive habitats deserve conservative stocking and monitoring.

## 71.48 Wild Pollinator Conservation Sites

Before placing large apiaries near:

- nature reserves;
- rare bee populations;
- flower-rich protected grasslands;
- specialist pollinator sites,

consider ecological objectives and consult land managers/research evidence.

## 71.49 Carrying Capacity

Carrying capacity is not a single fixed number of hives per hectare.

It changes with:

- floral biomass;
- bloom duration;
- weather;
- competing apiaries;
- wild pollinator demand;
- landscape radius.

Monitor colony weight and habitat rather than using a universal stocking formula.

## 71.50 Signs of Excessive Local Stocking

Possible signs include:

- low per-colony nectar gain despite good weather;
- prolonged dearth stress;
- robbing;
- poor pollen intake;
- high drift;
- conflict with conservation objectives.

These signs can have other causes, so investigate carefully.

## 71.51 Apiary Density and Disease

High colony density can increase:

- drifting;
- robbing;
- mite immigration;
- pathogen transmission.

The ecological cost of density is therefore not only floral competition.

## 71.52 Responsible Stocking

A responsible beekeeper considers:

- business need;
- colony health;
- forage;
- neighbouring beekeepers;
- conservation value;
- local wild-pollinator data.

Move or split apiaries when pressure exceeds local resources.

## 71.53 Biodiversity at the Apiary Site

The immediate apiary can include:

- native hedges;
- flowering ground cover;
- bare-soil nesting patches;
- dead wood;
- water;
- unmown refuge zones;
- reduced chemical use.

Avoid creating fire, access, or hygiene hazards.

## 71.54 Biodiversity in a Commercial Yard

Even industrial/business sites can add:

- native flowering strips;
- green roofs;
- reduced mowing;
- nesting habitat;
- rain gardens.

Actions should be designed for measurable habitat, not decorative “bee branding”.

## 71.55 Avoid “Bee-Washing”

Placing one hive on a roof does not prove that a company supports biodiversity.

Evidence-based action includes:

- reducing pesticide use;
- restoring habitat;
- monitoring species;
- connecting patches;
- funding conservation;
- responsible hive density.

## 71.56 Citizen Science

Citizen science can collect useful data on:

- bee distributions;
- flowering times;
- butterfly abundance;
- invasive species;
- pollinator visits.

Follow standard methods so observations can be compared.

## 71.57 Photographic Records

Repeat photos from fixed points can document:

- mowing change;
- hedgerow growth;
- flower abundance;
- drought;
- habitat restoration.

Photos are useful but do not identify every insect reliably.

## 71.58 Flower Counts

Simple transects or quadrats can record:

- flowering species;
- flower units;
- coverage;
- season.

Repeat the same method and timing for trend value.

## 71.59 Pollinator Transects

Walking a fixed route under suitable weather can track broad changes in visible pollinator activity.

Standardise:

- route;
- time;
- weather;
- observation width;
- identification confidence.

## 71.60 Identification Limits

Many wild bees require expert examination for species-level identification.

Use broader groups when certainty is low rather than inventing species names.

Photographs may support later verification.

## 71.61 eDNA and Molecular Monitoring

Environmental DNA and metabarcoding are emerging tools for biodiversity monitoring.

They can detect biological signatures from mixed samples but require:

- reference databases;
- contamination control;
- validated interpretation.

They complement rather than replace field ecology.

## 71.62 Honey and Pollen as Landscape Samples

Honey, pollen, wax, and bee-collected material can contain information about:

- plant sources;
- pesticides;
- microbes;
- environmental contaminants.

This makes colonies potential biomonitoring tools, although foraging bias must be considered.

## 71.63 Genetic Monitoring

Population genetics can help assess:

- diversity;
- introgression;
- local lineages;
- inbreeding;
- gene flow.

Such analyses should be interpreted by qualified population geneticists when used for conservation policy.

## 71.64 Invasive Species Monitoring

Beekeepers are well placed to notice:

- unusual hornets;
- novel mites;
- beetles;
- plant invasions.

Photograph/preserve evidence appropriately and report regulated species to competent authorities.

## 71.65 Biodiversity Metrics

Useful metrics can include:

- flowering species richness;
- season-long flower availability;
- habitat area;
- hedgerow length;
- bare-soil nesting area;
- pollinator species/group counts;
- pesticide use reduction;
- connectivity.

A single metric should not dominate all decisions.

## 71.66 Baseline Before Intervention

Measure before planting or changing management.

Without a baseline, improvement is difficult to demonstrate.

Record:

- habitat map;
- flowering calendar;
- pollinator observations;
- management practices.

## 71.67 Set Specific Objectives

Examples:

- add late-summer forage;
- increase ground-nesting habitat;
- reduce mowing by 50%;
- connect two habitat patches;
- eliminate routine herbicide from non-essential zones.

Specific objectives are more measurable than “help bees”.

## 71.68 Avoid Perverse Actions

A well-intended action can fail.

Examples:

- planting invasive “bee plants”;
- installing hundreds of poorly maintained bee hotels;
- adding many honey-bee hives to a rare wild-bee site;
- sowing unsuitable wildflower mixes;
- mowing before seed set every year.

Use local ecological guidance.

## 71.69 Seed Provenance

Wildflower seed choice should consider:

- native status;
- local genetics where conservation requires it;
- soil;
- climate;
- invasive risk;
- genuine species composition.

Cheap generic “bee mix” can contain unsuitable plants.

## 71.70 Planting Versus Regeneration

Sometimes the best action is not sowing seed but changing management so existing seed banks recover.

Options include:

- reduced fertilisation;
- altered mowing;
- grazing changes;
- soil disturbance targeted to habitat goals.

Assess the site first.

## 71.71 Hedgerow Restoration

Restoration can include:

- filling gaps;
- protecting young plants;
- rotational cutting;
- retaining mature trees;
- managing invasive species.

Avoid destroying old habitat while “installing” new habitat.

## 71.72 Ponds and Wet Features

Small ponds can support:

- hoverflies;
- dragonflies;
- aquatic insects;
- amphibians;
- drinking water.

Design for safety, water quality, and native vegetation.

## 71.73 Dead Wood

Dead wood supports:

- cavity-nesting bees;
- beetles;
- fungi;
- decomposers.

Retain safe standing/fallen material where appropriate.

## 71.74 Light Pollution

Artificial light at night can disrupt moths and other nocturnal insects.

Reduce unnecessary lighting through:

- timers;
- shielding;
- lower intensity;
- warm-spectrum lighting where appropriate.

Safety requirements still matter.

## 71.75 Roads and Transport Corridors

Road verges can become habitat corridors when managed carefully, but they also expose insects to:

- traffic mortality;
- pollution;
- mowing;
- pesticide drift.

Site-specific management is needed.

## 71.76 Urban Biodiversity

Cities can contain high floral diversity in gardens and parks.

Urban biodiversity planning should include:

- native plants;
- nesting substrate;
- reduced pesticide use;
- connected green space;
- responsible honey-bee stocking.

## 71.77 Agriculture and Biodiversity

Farm production and biodiversity are not mutually exclusive.

Potential actions include:

- integrated pest management;
- flowering cover crops;
- field margins;
- hedgerows;
- diverse rotations;
- reduced unnecessary mowing;
- wetland/buffer protection.

Economics and farm practicality should be considered.

## 71.78 Pollination Dependence

Different crops vary in dependence on animal pollination.

Biodiversity supports resilience because different pollinator groups can operate under different:

- weather;
- temperatures;
- flower structures;
- times of day.

Diversity can stabilise pollination services.

## 71.79 Honey Bees and Wild Pollinators Can Complement Each Other

In some agricultural systems, managed honey bees provide abundant pollination while wild species improve coverage or pollination quality.

The relationship is crop- and landscape-specific.

Do not assume either perfect complementarity or unavoidable competition.

## 71.80 Biodiversity Decision Table

| Situation | Main biodiversity question | Action principle |
|---|---|---|
| Large apiary near rare wild-bee reserve | Competition/disease pressure | Consult site managers; consider lower density/relocation |
| Farm has abundant spring flowers but summer dearth | Forage continuity | Add/manage mid- and late-season flowering habitat |
| “Bee hotel” full of mould/parasites | Artificial nesting hygiene | Replace/clean appropriately; increase natural nesting habitat |
| Intensive mowing | Lost flowers/nesting structure | Adopt timed/rotational cuts where safe |
| Invasive plant rich in nectar | Ecological trade-off | Follow invasive-species control; replace with suitable alternatives |
| High herbicide use on non-crop areas | Habitat loss | Target use and allow safe flowering refuges |
| One hive proposed as corporate biodiversity action | Weak metric | Prioritise measurable habitat and pesticide reduction |
| Unusual mite/hornet detected | Biosecurity/invasive risk | Preserve evidence and report as required |

## 71.81 Practical Biodiversity Audit

1. Map habitats within and around the apiary/farm.
2. Record flowering species by month/phenological stage.
3. Identify seasonal forage gaps.
4. Identify nesting resources for ground, cavity, and bumble bees.
5. Record mowing, herbicide, insecticide, and fertiliser practices.
6. Note water, dead wood, hedgerows, ponds, bare soil, and refuges.
7. Estimate managed honey-bee density and nearby apiaries where known.
8. Check protected/rare pollinator information.
9. Select three measurable improvements.
10. Monitor with the same method each year.

## 71.82 Biodiversity Checklist

- [ ] Honey bees are not used as the sole biodiversity metric.
- [ ] Local wild pollinator groups are recognised.
- [ ] Honey-bee genetic diversity is considered.
- [ ] Forage calendar covers the full active season.
- [ ] Native/local plant value assessed.
- [ ] Ground-nesting habitat retained where suitable.
- [ ] Hollow stems/dead wood retained where safe.
- [ ] Bumble-bee nesting/refuge structure considered.
- [ ] Hedgerows managed for flowering/connectivity.
- [ ] Mowing regime supports flowers/refuges.
- [ ] Invasive plants not promoted solely for nectar.
- [ ] Pesticide use reduced/targeted where possible.
- [ ] Herbicide indirect forage loss considered.
- [ ] Water/wet habitat managed safely.
- [ ] Artificial light minimised where practical.
- [ ] Apiary density evaluated against forage/conservation context.
- [ ] Managed colonies kept healthy to reduce disease pressure.
- [ ] Sensitive wild-pollinator sites respected.
- [ ] Baseline data recorded before intervention.
- [ ] Monitoring method repeated consistently.
- [ ] Citizen-science data use standard protocols.
- [ ] Conservation claims are evidence-based.
- [ ] Habitat actions measured rather than only photographed.
- [ ] Local ecological/landowner guidance followed.

## Scientific Notes

### Abundance and diversity are not interchangeable

A site can contain a very large number of individuals from one species but low species diversity. Managed honey-bee abundance therefore cannot stand alone as a biodiversity indicator.

### Competition is context dependent

Resource overlap between honey bees and wild pollinators is well documented, but whether overlap creates population-level harm depends on resource supply, density, timing, species, and habitat. Conservation decisions should respond to local evidence and uncertainty.

### Habitat heterogeneity creates resilience

A landscape containing multiple habitat types is more likely to provide alternative forage, nesting, microclimates, and refuges when drought, storms, or management disturb one component.

### Phenology is shifting

Recent syntheses show widespread climate-related shifts in plant and pollinator timing. Evidence for mismatch consequences varies, but northern and specialist interactions may be particularly vulnerable in some studies.

### Biodiversity is functional as well as numerical

Different pollinator species differ in body size, tongue length, flight temperature, flower handling, and daily activity. Functional diversity can support pollination resilience.

## Key Takeaways

- Biodiversity includes genes, species, habitats, ecosystems, and ecological relationships.
- Managed honey-bee colony numbers are not a direct measure of pollinator biodiversity.
- Wild pollinators need both flowers and nesting/overwintering habitat.
- Forage continuity across the season is more important than one spectacular short bloom.
- Hedgerows, margins, soils, dead wood, water, and mowing regimes can be as important as flower planting.
- Pesticides can affect biodiversity directly and indirectly through resource loss.
- Competition between managed honey bees and wild pollinators is context dependent; sensitive habitats deserve conservative stocking.
- Healthy managed colonies and biosecurity help reduce pathogen pressure.
- Climate change alters phenology, forage, and pest distributions, increasing the value of connected diverse habitats.
- Biodiversity action should begin with a baseline and be measured over time.

## Chapter Summary

Biodiversity is a property of ecological systems, not a count of honey-bee hives. Managed honey bees are valuable pollinators and agricultural animals, but conservation requires attention to the much wider community of wild bees, hoverflies, butterflies, moths, plants, nesting substrates, soils, water, predators, microbes, and genetic diversity.

The strongest interventions usually improve habitat structure and continuity. A landscape should provide flowers through the full season, nesting and overwintering sites, connected hedgerows and margins, low pesticide exposure, safe water, dead wood, soil heterogeneity, and refuges from mowing. In Ireland, the 2026–2030 All-Ireland Pollinator Plan expresses this connectivity goal as “pockets of nature every 200 metres” and reports that around one-third of the island's approximately 100 wild bee species are at risk of extinction.

Responsible beekeeping also requires attention to apiary density. Honey bees and wild pollinators share some resources, and high managed colony densities can intensify floral competition and pathogen movement under resource-limited conditions. The evidence is context dependent, so decisions should be cautious near rare-species sites and protected flower-rich habitats.

A biodiversity programme should therefore be measurable. Baseline habitat maps, flower calendars, pollinator transects, fixed-point photographs, nesting-resource records, pesticide-use reductions, and repeat surveys allow the beekeeper to distinguish real ecological improvement from attractive but unverified “bee-friendly” marketing.

## References

- National Biodiversity Data Centre. *All-Ireland Pollinator Plan 2026–2030*, launched 9 July 2026; voluntary framework with 50 strategic aims, 75 success metrics and the “pockets of nature every 200 metres” vision.
- All-Ireland Pollinator Plan. Current sectoral evidence-based guidance for farmland, communities, businesses, public land and transport networks. Accessed 16 September 2026.
- IPBES. *Assessment Report on Pollinators, Pollination and Food Production* and subsequent biodiversity assessments.
- Potts, S. G. et al. Major reviews on global pollinator status, drivers and responses.
- Recent systematic reviews on climate change, plant reproductive traits and pollinators, including phenology, drought, warming and mismatch evidence.
- Peng, S., Ellison, A. M., and Davis, C. C. “Climate change intensifies plant–pollinator mismatch and increases secondary extinction risk for plants in northern latitudes.” *PNAS*, 2025.
- Scientific literature on managed-honey-bee and wild-pollinator resource competition, disease spillover, apiary density and carrying capacity; interpretation should remain site- and species-specific.
- Current national biodiversity, invasive-species, pesticide, land-management, conservation and protected-site guidance should be checked for the reader’s jurisdiction.


<a id="chapter-72"></a>

# Chapter 72 — Pollinator Conservation

## Introduction

Pollinator conservation is the deliberate protection and restoration of the resources that pollinating animals need to survive and reproduce. It is broader than beekeeping and broader than planting flowers.

A pollinator needs a complete life-cycle habitat. Depending on species, that can include:

- nectar;
- pollen;
- larval host plants;
- bare soil;
- hollow stems;
- old mammal burrows;
- dead wood;
- wetlands;
- shelter from wind and heat;
- overwintering sites;
- freedom from damaging pesticide exposure;
- connected habitat within realistic movement distance.

This is why adding managed honey-bee hives is not automatically a conservation action. Honey bees can be valuable agricultural pollinators and important managed livestock while wild-pollinator conservation requires a different set of objectives and measurements.

The strongest conservation approach protects existing high-quality habitat first, improves the management of ordinary land second, restores missing habitat where needed, and measures whether ecological conditions actually improve.

In Ireland, the All-Ireland Pollinator Plan 2026–2030 provides a current example of an evidence-based voluntary framework. Its overall vision is a landscape with “pockets of nature every 200 metres”, reflecting the short foraging range of many small wild bees. The plan contains 50 strategic aims and 75 success metrics across 10 work packages. It is not law; landowners and organisations remain responsible for how actions are implemented.

## Learning Objectives

After completing this chapter, the reader should be able to:

- distinguish wild-pollinator conservation from managed honey-bee production;
- identify the resource needs of bees, hoverflies, butterflies, moths, and other pollinators;
- apply a conservation hierarchy that protects existing habitat before creating new habitat;
- design season-long forage rather than one short flowering peak;
- conserve nesting and overwintering resources;
- reduce pesticide exposure using risk-based integrated management;
- plan mowing, hedgerow, verge, farm, urban, and business-site management for pollinators;
- understand the roles of soil, water, dead wood, darkness, and connectivity;
- evaluate disease spillover and managed honey-bee stocking near sensitive sites;
- monitor conservation outcomes using practical metrics;
- use the current All-Ireland Pollinator Plan as a voluntary Irish example without treating it as universal law.

## 72.1 Conservation Begins With Objectives

Define what you are trying to conserve.

Examples:

- increase wild bee nesting habitat;
- protect a rare species;
- increase summer floral continuity;
- reduce pesticide exposure;
- restore a species-rich meadow;
- improve crop pollination diversity;
- connect isolated habitat patches.

“Help bees” is too vague to evaluate.

## 72.2 Protect Before You Create

The first priority is often to protect existing high-value habitat.

Old hedgerows, species-rich grassland, sandy banks, wetlands, dead wood, mature trees, and long-established nesting sites can be difficult or impossible to recreate quickly.

Do not destroy old habitat while installing decorative “pollinator habitat” beside it.

## 72.3 Conservation Hierarchy

A practical hierarchy is:

1. protect existing habitat;
2. stop damaging management;
3. improve management of existing land;
4. reconnect fragmented habitat;
5. restore degraded habitat;
6. create new habitat where genuine gaps remain;
7. monitor outcomes and adapt.

Adding managed hives belongs to a separate beekeeping or pollination decision, not automatically to this hierarchy.

## 72.4 Wild Bees

Wild bees include:

- bumble bees;
- mining bees;
- mason bees;
- leafcutter bees;
- sweat bees;
- numerous other solitary/social groups.

They differ in:

- body size;
- flight range;
- nesting;
- tongue length;
- season;
- floral specialisation.

Conservation must therefore support multiple ecological niches.

## 72.5 Bumble Bees

Bumble-bee colonies are annual in many temperate regions.

Key needs include:

- spring forage for queens;
- suitable nest sites;
- abundant summer forage;
- late forage for new queens;
- overwintering sites.

A gap at one stage can limit the whole colony cycle.

## 72.6 Solitary Bees

Most solitary bee females construct their own nests.

Some are:

- ground-nesters;
- cavity-nesters;
- stem-nesters;
- specialists on particular pollen sources.

They can have much shorter foraging ranges than honey bees, making local habitat continuity especially important.

## 72.7 Hoverflies

Adult hoverflies often visit flowers, but larvae can be:

- aphid predators;
- aquatic/filter feeders;
- decomposers;
- plant feeders.

Conserving hoverflies can therefore require wetlands, aphid-rich vegetation, dead organic matter, and plant diversity in addition to flowers.

## 72.8 Butterflies and Moths

Adults may use nectar flowers, while larvae often depend on specific host plants.

Conservation requires:

- larval food plants;
- nectar;
- shelter;
- overwintering structure;
- reduced pesticide exposure.

Removing host “weeds” can negate nectar planting.

## 72.9 Beetles and Other Pollinators

Many beetles, flies, wasps, and other insects pollinate plants opportunistically or as specialists.

Habitat policy should therefore avoid using “bee conservation” as a synonym for all pollinator conservation.

## 72.10 Forage Continuity

Pollinators need food when they are active, not merely when a major crop blooms.

Build a local flowering calendar showing:

- early spring;
- late spring;
- early summer;
- high summer;
- late summer;
- autumn.

Identify gaps rather than counting only species.

## 72.11 Flower Abundance

A few plants cannot support a large pollinator population.

Measure not only species richness but:

- flower abundance;
- area;
- duration;
- accessibility;
- recurring management.

Habitat must produce enough food, not just a checklist of names.

## 72.12 Floral Diversity

Different flowers support different pollinators because of variation in:

- corolla depth;
- flower size;
- pollen chemistry;
- nectar concentration;
- colour;
- flowering time.

Diverse morphology increases functional value.

## 72.13 Native Plants

Native plants often support complex local food webs.

They can provide:

- nectar and pollen;
- specialist host relationships;
- larval food;
- seed;
- nesting material.

Use locally appropriate native species especially in conservation restoration.

## 72.14 Non-Native Plants

Some non-native garden plants provide useful forage.

Use should consider:

- invasive potential;
- local ecological value;
- seasonality;
- pesticide history.

A non-native plant is not automatically harmful, but invasive species should not be promoted merely because bees visit them.

## 72.15 Planting Is Not Always Necessary

Existing seed banks can recover when damaging management stops.

Consider:

- reducing mowing;
- reducing fertiliser;
- adjusting grazing;
- removing invasive cover;
- exposing suitable soil.

Natural regeneration may preserve local genetics and cost less.

## 72.16 Seed Mixes

If sowing is needed, check:

- species list;
- native/local suitability;
- seed origin;
- soil type;
- perennial/annual balance;
- flowering period;
- invasive risk;
- actual percentage of each species.

A package labelled “bee mix” is not automatically ecologically appropriate.

## 72.17 Pollinator-Friendly Trees

Trees and shrubs can provide large quantities of forage per unit ground area.

Examples depend on region but can include:

- willow;
- hawthorn;
- fruit trees;
- maple;
- lime/linden;
- ivy as a climber/late source in some regions.

Trees also create shelter and nesting structure.

## 72.18 Hedgerows as Multi-Function Habitat

Hedgerows provide:

- flowers;
- shelter;
- nesting;
- movement corridors;
- berries/seeds;
- dead wood.

Conservation value depends heavily on cutting regime and species composition.

## 72.19 Rotational Hedgerow Cutting

Many woody species flower on older growth.

Rotational or less frequent cutting can preserve more blossoms than annual severe trimming.

Follow local road-safety and agricultural regulations.

## 72.20 Hedgerow Gaps

Fill appropriate gaps with locally suitable shrubs rather than replacing entire mature hedges.

Retain veteran trees where safe.

Old structural complexity often provides more habitat than uniform new planting.

## 72.21 Field Margins

Margins can combine:

- flowering plants;
- tussock grass;
- bare ground;
- shrubs;
- wet features.

Protect them from spray drift and fertiliser overspread where possible.

## 72.22 Floral Strips

Flower strips can be useful when designed for:

- local soil;
- target pollinators;
- season-long bloom;
- multi-year persistence;
- manageable weed competition.

Evaluate performance rather than assuming sowing equals success.

## 72.23 Farm Cover Crops

Flowering cover crops can provide temporary forage and soil benefits.

Plan:

- flowering timing;
- termination timing;
- pesticide use;
- crop rotation;
- actual accessibility before destruction.

A crop terminated before bloom has no pollinator forage benefit.

## 72.24 Meadow Restoration

Species-rich meadow restoration often requires nutrient management.

Steps can include:

- baseline vegetation survey;
- reduced fertility;
- cutting/grazing regime;
- removal of cuttings;
- targeted seed or green hay where needed;
- invasive control;
- multi-year monitoring.

## 72.25 Mowing Less

Reduced mowing can increase flowers quickly.

However, define a schedule that balances:

- biodiversity;
- access;
- fire risk;
- visibility;
- recreation;
- seed set.

Rotational mowing can retain refuge while keeping areas usable.

## 72.26 Refuge Strips

Leave sections uncut when other areas are mown.

These strips protect:

- larvae;
- pupae;
- nests;
- flowers;
- overwintering insects.

Move refuge locations over time if management requires it.

## 72.27 Verge Management

Road verges can connect habitat but require safety visibility.

Better practice can include:

- fewer full-width cuts;
- later cuts after flowering;
- removing cut material where appropriate;
- avoiding herbicide blanket spraying.

Work with transport authorities.

## 72.28 Parks and Public Land

Public sites can support pollinators through:

- reduced mowing;
- native flowering areas;
- diverse trees;
- dead-wood retention where safe;
- reduced pesticide use;
- interpretation signs.

Public acceptance improves when management looks intentional rather than neglected.

## 72.29 Business Sites

Industrial estates and offices can create habitat in:

- unused grass margins;
- car-park edges;
- roofs;
- drainage areas;
- entrance planting.

The conservation value should come from habitat, not a symbolic hive alone.

## 72.30 Gardens

Gardens collectively form large habitat networks.

Actions include:

- flowers across seasons;
- fewer pesticides;
- nesting patches;
- pond/water features;
- leaving stems;
- avoiding invasive plants.

Small actions become meaningful when repeated across landscapes.

## 72.31 The AIPP 2026–2030 Connectivity Vision

The All-Ireland Pollinator Plan 2026–2030 seeks “pockets of nature every 200 metres”.

This is useful conservation thinking because small solitary bees can have limited foraging ranges.

Connectivity allows short-ranging species to move through otherwise hostile landscapes.

## 72.32 The Plan Is Voluntary

The AIPP is an evidence-based voluntary framework coordinated by the National Biodiversity Data Centre.

It is not a substitute for:

- law;
- planning permission;
- protected-site rules;
- pesticide labels;
- landowner responsibility.

Use it as guidance, not as legal authority.

## 72.33 Protect Rare Species First

If a site supports a rare or threatened pollinator, conservation actions should reflect that species' biology.

Generic mass-flowering seed may be less useful than protecting:

- one nesting bank;
- one host plant;
- one wetland edge;
- one specialised flower resource.

Consult specialists.

## 72.34 Specialist Bees

Some bees depend heavily on particular plant groups.

Conservation must protect the pollen source and nesting habitat together.

A general flower mix may not support a specialist species.

## 72.35 Bare Ground

Many solitary bees need sunny exposed soil.

Create or protect small areas of:

- bare sand;
- compacted earth banks;
- sparsely vegetated slopes

where appropriate and safe.

## 72.36 Soil Compaction

Heavy traffic can destroy ground nests.

Protect known nesting areas from:

- vehicles;
- repeated foot traffic;
- deep cultivation;
- resurfacing.

Signage can help on public sites.

## 72.37 Dead Wood

Dead wood supports cavity-nesting bees, beetles, fungi, and decomposers.

Retain:

- standing dead wood where safe;
- fallen logs;
- drilled natural beetle holes;
- old stumps.

Safety assessment is essential in public areas.

## 72.38 Hollow Stems

Leave some perennial stems standing through winter and into spring.

Some cavity-nesting species use them only after stems break or are cut and weather.

Immediate removal every autumn eliminates this resource.

## 72.39 Artificial Nest Blocks

Artificial nests can support a subset of cavity nesters.

Good design uses:

- appropriate hole sizes;
- dry shelter;
- replaceable/cleanable nesting material;
- predator management.

Discard mouldy or heavily parasitised material.

## 72.40 Bumble-Bee Nest Habitat

Leave:

- tussocky grass;
- rough banks;
- old rodent burrows;
- undisturbed corners.

Avoid disturbing likely nests during mowing.

## 72.41 Overwintering Habitat

Pollinators overwinter as:

- adults;
- queens;
- larvae;
- pupae;
- eggs.

Resources include:

- leaf litter;
- soil;
- stems;
- bark;
- tussocks;
- cavities.

Winter “tidying” can remove entire generations.

## 72.42 Leave Leaf Litter Where Appropriate

Leaf litter supports many invertebrates.

Retain it under hedges and trees where it does not create access or fire problems.

Do not vacuum every natural surface for cosmetic neatness.

## 72.43 Water and Wet Habitat

Ponds and wet margins support hoverflies and many non-bee pollinator associates.

Design shallow margins and native vegetation.

Avoid contamination with fertiliser, pesticides, or wastewater.

## 72.44 Safe Water for Honey Bees

Provide water before bees seek neighbours' ponds or livestock troughs.

Use:

- shallow access;
- landing material;
- regular cleaning.

This is primarily good apiary management, but it can reduce conflict.

## 72.45 Light Pollution

Night lighting can affect moth behaviour and ecological interactions.

Reduce unnecessary light through:

- shielding;
- timers;
- sensors;
- lower intensity;
- appropriate spectrum.

Do not compromise essential safety lighting.

## 72.46 Pesticide-Risk Hierarchy

A conservation hierarchy is:

1. determine whether control is needed;
2. prevent pest problems;
3. use cultural/mechanical controls;
4. choose the least harmful effective pesticide when needed;
5. target application;
6. avoid exposure to flowering/non-target habitat;
7. follow label and legal restrictions;
8. evaluate results.

This is integrated pest management applied to conservation.

## 72.47 Avoid Calendar Spraying

Treat based on monitoring and thresholds where the crop/pest system supports them.

Routine schedule treatment can increase unnecessary exposure and resistance.

## 72.48 Flowering Weeds

Flowering weeds can be major pollinator resources.

If they must be controlled:

- avoid treating while actively visited where label/guidance permits alternative timing;
- mow/remove flowers first only when this does not itself expose pollinators to harmful residues;
- use targeted application.

Follow product-specific rules.

## 72.49 Spray Drift

Drift can contaminate margins beyond the target area.

Reduce through:

- correct nozzles;
- pressure;
- wind limits;
- buffers;
- operator training.

The pesticide label is legally important.

## 72.50 Seed Treatments

Systemic seed treatments can reduce foliar spraying but may create other exposure routes through plant tissues, dust, or residues depending on active ingredient.

Evaluate the actual product and current regulatory status rather than generalising.

## 72.51 Fungicide and Insecticide Mixtures

Tank mixes can produce interactions not obvious from one active ingredient alone.

Use only authorised combinations and follow pollinator restrictions.

Communicate with local beekeepers when relevant.

## 72.52 Communication With Farmers

Beekeepers and growers should exchange:

- apiary locations;
- crop bloom timing;
- planned pesticide applications;
- hive movement;
- contact details.

Communication cannot replace legal label compliance, but it can reduce avoidable exposure.

## 72.53 Disease as a Conservation Issue

Managed bees can carry pathogens and parasites.

Reduce transmission pressure by:

- controlling Varroa;
- preventing colony collapse and robbing;
- monitoring introduced bees;
- keeping sick colonies away from sensitive sites;
- following movement rules.

## 72.54 Spillover Evidence

Pathogen overlap has been documented among managed and wild pollinators, but demonstrating direction and population-level effects is complex.

Use precaution without claiming every wild-bee virus originated from managed honey bees.

## 72.55 Apiary Stocking Near Conservation Sites

Before placing colonies near high-value wild-pollinator habitat, assess:

- flowering-resource abundance;
- current managed-colony density;
- rare species;
- drought/dearth risk;
- disease status;
- land manager objectives.

Lower densities or no new hives may be appropriate in some sensitive sites.

## 72.56 Honey-Bees Are Not a Replacement for Habitat

A protected site degraded by mowing, pesticide use, and habitat loss is not “restored” by installing hives.

Habitat conservation addresses the underlying ecological resource.

## 72.57 Pollination Service Versus Conservation

Commercial crop pollination aims to deliver pollination to a crop.

Wild-pollinator conservation aims to maintain native/wild populations and ecological networks.

These objectives can complement each other but should be measured separately.

## 72.58 Climate Adaptation in Habitat Design

Use habitat diversity to buffer:

- drought;
- heat;
- heavy rain;
- early springs;
- late frost.

Provide species with different flowering times and soil/moisture tolerances.

## 72.59 Drought-Resilient Forage

Select locally appropriate plants that can flower under drier conditions without becoming invasive.

Protect soil organic matter and water infiltration.

Avoid overpromising future climate performance from one species.

## 72.60 Shade and Microclimates

Trees, hedges, slopes, and vegetation create temperature differences.

Microclimate diversity provides refuges during heat waves.

Conservation design should include sun and shade rather than one uniform exposure.

## 72.61 Extreme Rain

Heavy rain can destroy flowers and flood ground nests.

Landscape drainage and elevated nesting areas can provide resilience.

Do not drain valuable wetlands simply to protect one nesting habitat.

## 72.62 Invasive Species Control

Control should be strategic.

Ask:

- what ecological harm occurs?
- what legal obligation applies?
- what replacement habitat will remain after removal?
- what is the least damaging effective method?

Removing a major invasive nectar source without replacement can create a temporary dearth even when removal is ecologically necessary.

## 72.63 Asian Hornet and Other Invasive Predators

In regions where invasive hornets threaten bees, conservation and beekeeping responses should follow official surveillance/control programmes.

Do not use indiscriminate traps that kill large numbers of non-target insects without evidence of benefit.

## 72.64 Tropilaelaps Preparedness

The westward expansion of *Tropilaelaps mercedesae* documented in 2026 reinforces the value of:

- surveillance;
- movement controls;
- identification training;
- rapid reporting;
- healthy managed colonies.

Invasive parasite prevention protects both apiculture and broader pollinator-health systems.

## 72.65 Urban Hive Density

Cities can provide abundant gardens, but urban forage is not unlimited.

Before adding more hives assess:

- existing apiaries;
- green-space area;
- seasonality;
- wild pollinator habitat;
- neighbour/public concerns.

Urban beekeeping should not become competitive hive accumulation.

## 72.66 School Sites

Schools can support conservation through:

- native flowers;
- reduced mowing;
- nesting habitat;
- monitoring;
- education.

Live honey-bee hives add safety and management complexity and are not required for effective pollinator education.

## 72.67 Corporate Sites

Set measurable outcomes such as:

- square metres of restored habitat;
- mowing reduction;
- pesticide reduction;
- flowering months;
- monitoring results.

Avoid “we installed hives, therefore biodiversity improved”.

## 72.68 Public Communication

Explain why conservation areas may look less manicured.

Signs can describe:

- delayed mowing;
- nesting soil;
- dead stems;
- native plants.

Communication reduces complaints that drive premature cutting.

## 72.69 Monitoring Before and After

Record a baseline.

Then repeat:

- flower counts;
- pollinator transects;
- habitat mapping;
- management records;
- rare-species observations.

Evaluate trend over several years.

## 72.70 Weather Standardisation

Pollinator counts are strongly affected by:

- temperature;
- wind;
- cloud;
- rain;
- time of day.

Repeat surveys under comparable conditions.

## 72.71 Species Identification

Use experts or verified resources for difficult taxa.

Record uncertain observations honestly.

A high-quality “bumble bee sp.” record is better than a false species identification.

## 72.72 Occupancy Versus Reproduction

Seeing a pollinator does not prove it is reproducing at the site.

Habitat evaluation should consider:

- nests;
- mating;
- larval resources;
- repeated seasonal presence.

Flowers can attract transient visitors from elsewhere.

## 72.73 Nest Monitoring

Avoid disturbing nests simply to count them.

Use observation, marked areas, or non-destructive methods where possible.

Protected species can have legal disturbance restrictions.

## 72.74 Flower Resource Index

A simple local index can score:

- flowering area;
- number of flowering species;
- bloom duration;
- seasonal gaps.

Use it consistently rather than pretending it is a universal scientific standard.

## 72.75 Mowing Records

Record:

- date;
- area;
- cut height;
- cuttings removed/not;
- refuge retained.

Management records help explain ecological changes.

## 72.76 Pesticide-Use Records

Track:

- active ingredient/product;
- target pest;
- area;
- date;
- flowering status;
- weather;
- alternative controls considered.

Reduction is measurable only if baseline use is known.

## 72.77 Conservation KPIs

Useful indicators can include:

- months with forage;
- percentage reduced mowing;
- habitat area;
- nesting features;
- pesticide-use reduction;
- number of pollinator groups/species observed;
- connectivity improvements.

Avoid judging success from social-media engagement.

## 72.78 Adaptive Management

If an intervention fails, change it.

Examples:

- meadow dominated by grass → reduce fertility/adjust cutting;
- seed mix fails → investigate soil/seed quality;
- bee hotel diseased → remove/replace design;
- late-summer gap persists → add appropriate late plants.

Conservation is iterative.

## 72.79 Cost-Effectiveness

Low-cost actions can be highly effective:

- mow less;
- leave stems;
- protect hedges;
- stop unnecessary spraying;
- preserve bare soil.

Do not assume expensive planting is always superior.

## 72.80 Volunteer and Community Projects

Community projects can scale habitat networks.

Provide:

- standard guidance;
- realistic plant lists;
- monitoring instructions;
- maintenance responsibility;
- multi-year funding.

Planting day is the beginning, not the conservation outcome.

## 72.81 Conservation Decision Table

| Site/problem | Priority | Avoid |
|---|---|---|
| Species-rich old meadow | Protect existing habitat | Ploughing/reseeding with generic mix |
| Mown lawn with few flowers | Change mowing regime | Immediate expensive planting before testing regeneration |
| Rare ground-nesting bee bank | Protect soil/nesting | Heavy traffic or adding many hives nearby |
| Farm seasonal dearth | Add targeted forage gap | More spring flowers when spring is already abundant |
| Invasive nectar plant | Replace/control strategically | Promoting invasion because bees visit it |
| High pesticide use | IPM/risk reduction | Unrecorded calendar spraying |
| Corporate “bee project” | Habitat + metrics | Hives as sole biodiversity action |
| Pollinator counts vary wildly | Standardise survey weather/time | Comparing incomparable survey conditions |

## 72.82 Practical Habitat Audit

### Step 1 — Map

Map habitat patches, hedges, water, bare soil, woodland, grassland, crop and hard surfaces.

### Step 2 — Flower calendar

Record key flowers by month/phenological period.

### Step 3 — Nest resources

Locate ground, cavities, stems, tussocks, banks, dead wood.

### Step 4 — Threats

Record pesticide use, mowing, invasive plants, lighting, traffic, stocking density.

### Step 5 — Targets

Choose 3–5 measurable actions.

### Step 6 — Monitor

Repeat the same surveys and adapt.

## 72.83 Pollinator-Conservation Checklist

- [ ] Conservation objective is specific.
- [ ] Existing high-quality habitat mapped/protected.
- [ ] Flower calendar covers early to late season.
- [ ] Seasonal forage gaps identified.
- [ ] Native/local plant value considered.
- [ ] Specialist pollinator resources identified where relevant.
- [ ] Ground-nesting habitat retained.
- [ ] Hollow stems/dead wood retained where safe.
- [ ] Bumble-bee refuge/nesting habitat considered.
- [ ] Wet habitat protected.
- [ ] Mowing reduced/rotated where practical.
- [ ] Hedgerow cutting supports flowering.
- [ ] Invasive species controlled responsibly.
- [ ] Pesticide use follows IPM and label restrictions.
- [ ] Spray drift reduced.
- [ ] Managed honey-bee colonies kept healthy.
- [ ] Sensitive habitats not overstocked with managed hives.
- [ ] Lighting reduced where unnecessary.
- [ ] Baseline monitoring completed.
- [ ] Survey weather/time standardised.
- [ ] Species ID uncertainty recorded honestly.
- [ ] Habitat and management metrics tracked.
- [ ] Public/community communication planned.
- [ ] Actions reviewed after monitoring.
- [ ] Conservation claims match evidence.

## Scientific Notes

### Pollinator conservation is life-cycle conservation

Adult flower visitation is visible, but population persistence depends on larval resources, nests, overwintering, mating, and survival. Habitat must support the full life cycle.

### Connectivity matters most for short-ranging organisms

Small solitary bees can operate over much shorter distances than honey bees. Frequent habitat patches can therefore be more useful than one distant large flower bed.

### More hives are not always more conservation

Managed honey bees can provide valuable pollination, but high densities can add competition and pathogen pressure. Conservation goals should be measured with wild-pollinator and habitat metrics.

### Climate adaptation favours heterogeneity

Different plant species, slopes, moisture regimes, and shade conditions spread ecological risk across extreme weather events.

### Monitoring is part of conservation

Without baseline and repeat data, attractive actions can persist even when they fail to support target species.

## Key Takeaways

- Protect existing high-quality habitat before creating new habitat.
- Pollinator conservation must support entire life cycles, not only adult nectar feeding.
- Season-long forage, nesting sites, overwintering resources, soil, water, dead wood, and connectivity all matter.
- Pesticide risk should be reduced through integrated pest management and exposure control.
- Less intensive mowing and hedgerow management can produce large benefits at low cost.
- Adding managed honey-bee colonies is not automatically a wild-pollinator conservation action.
- Sensitive conservation sites require careful managed-hive stocking and strong disease control.
- The All-Ireland Pollinator Plan 2026–2030 is a current voluntary Irish framework whose connectivity vision can inspire local planning.
- Conservation should use measurable baselines and repeat monitoring.
- Adaptive management is more valuable than repeating a visually attractive action that does not work.

## Chapter Summary

Pollinator conservation succeeds when landscapes provide the complete resources that pollinators need across their life cycles. Flowers are essential, but so are bare soil, hollow stems, dead wood, tussocks, wetlands, larval host plants, sheltered microclimates, overwintering sites, and safe movement between patches.

The most efficient strategy begins with protection. Existing species-rich meadow, mature hedgerow, nesting bank, or wetland usually contains ecological history that is difficult to recreate. Management can then improve ordinary land by mowing less, reducing pesticide exposure, adding missing seasonal forage, restoring hedgerows, retaining dead stems, and creating connected patches.

Managed honey bees belong in this landscape but should not be confused with a biodiversity metric. Large apiaries can be appropriate for production or pollination while still requiring caution near rare wild-pollinator populations or resource-limited habitat. Healthy colonies and biosecurity reduce pathogen pressure.

In Ireland, the All-Ireland Pollinator Plan 2026–2030 expresses a useful connectivity target: “pockets of nature every 200 metres”. Its value lies in coordinated habitat action and measurable success metrics rather than symbolic hive installation. The same principle applies elsewhere: define the ecological objective, protect what already works, repair missing resources, measure outcomes, and adapt.

## Review Questions

1. What is pollinator conservation?
2. Why is planting flowers alone insufficient?
3. What should be defined before choosing conservation actions?
4. What is the first step in the conservation hierarchy?
5. Why should old habitat be protected before decorative new planting?
6. Name the seven steps in a practical conservation hierarchy.
7. Are managed honey-bee hives automatically a wild-pollinator conservation action?
8. Name four groups of wild bees.
9. How do wild bee species differ ecologically?
10. What do bumble-bee queens need in spring?
11. Why is late forage important to bumble-bee life cycles?
12. What distinguishes solitary bees from social honey bees?
13. Why are local habitat patches especially important for short-ranging solitary bees?
14. Why do hoverflies need more than flowers?
15. Why do butterflies need larval host plants?
16. Are all pollinators bees?
17. What is forage continuity?
18. Why should flower abundance be measured as well as species richness?
19. How does flower shape affect pollinator use?
20. Why are native plants valuable?
21. Can non-native plants ever provide useful forage?
22. Why should invasive plants not be promoted for bee value alone?
23. When can natural regeneration outperform sowing?
24. What should be checked in a commercial wildflower mix?
25. Why can flowering trees be high-value forage?
26. What ecological functions do hedgerows provide?
27. Why can annual severe hedge cutting reduce flowers?
28. What is rotational hedgerow cutting?
29. Why might filling hedge gaps be better than replacing the whole hedge?
30. What can field margins provide?
31. What makes a flower strip effective?
32. When does a cover crop fail to provide pollinator forage?

35. Why does reduced mowing help pollinators?
36. Why is “never mow” not a universal rule?

39. What must verge management still protect?

42. What role do gardens play in landscape conservation?
43. What is the AIPP “pockets of nature every 200 metres” vision?
44. Why is this especially relevant to small solitary bees?
45. Is AIPP a law?

50. Why is dead wood ecologically valuable?
51. Why should hollow stems sometimes remain through winter?
52. What design principles improve artificial nest blocks?
53. Why can poorly managed nest blocks become harmful?
54. What habitat can support bumble-bee nesting?
55. In which life stages can insects overwinter?
56. Why can autumn tidying destroy habitat?
57. What value does leaf litter have?
58. Why are ponds relevant to pollinator conservation?
59. How should honey-bee water sources be designed?
60. How can light pollution affect moths?
61. What is the pesticide-risk hierarchy?
62. Why should pesticide use be monitoring-based where possible?
63. How can flowering weeds create pesticide exposure risk?
64. What determines spray drift?
65. Why can systemic seed treatments have different exposure pathways?
66. Why should tank mixtures be treated cautiously?
67. What should farmers and beekeepers communicate about?
68. How can managed colonies influence pollinator disease ecology?
69. What is pathogen spillover?
70. Why is direction of pathogen transmission difficult to prove?
71. What should be assessed before placing hives near sensitive conservation habitat?
72. Why are honey-bee hives not a replacement for habitat restoration?
73. How does commercial pollination differ from wild-pollinator conservation?
74. How can habitat diversity improve climate resilience?
75. Why should drought-tolerant planting still be locally appropriate?
76. What ecological value do microclimates provide?
77. How can heavy rain affect ground nests?
78. What questions should guide invasive-plant removal?
79. Why can invasive-plant removal temporarily create forage gaps?
80. Why can indiscriminate hornet traps be ecologically harmful?
81. What does Tropilaelaps expansion illustrate about biosecurity?
82. Why should urban hive density be considered?
83. Do schools need honey-bee hives to teach pollinator conservation?
84. Which measurable outcomes can corporate sites report?
85. Why is public communication important for reduced mowing?
86. Why is baseline monitoring required?
87. Which weather variables affect pollinator counts?
88. Why is an uncertain genus/group record better than a false species identification?
89. Why does seeing a bee not prove local reproduction?
90. How can nests be monitored without unnecessary disturbance?
91. What can a local flower-resource index measure?
92. Why are mowing records useful?
93. What belongs in pesticide-use records?

98. Why is a community planting day only the start of conservation?

100. What makes a pollinator-conservation programme evidence-based rather than symbolic?
