## Bibliographic Maintenance Rule

Before release of a new handbook edition:

1. re-check all legal and regulatory sources;
2. re-check URLs for institutional resources;
3. verify that cited 2025–2026 “current” documents remain current;
4. retain DOI-based peer-reviewed citations even if journal landing pages move;
5. remove duplicate references caused by chapter mergers;
6. never delete a foundational source merely because it is old if it remains scientifically or historically necessary;
7. never rely on an old source for current medicine authorisation, disease-reporting law, food labelling, or treatment thresholds.

*This consolidated bibliography should be proofed against the chapter-level references during the final technical review to capture any source added during late corrections.*


<a id="reference-77"></a>

# Reference 77 — Recommended Books

## Reference 77 — Recommended Books

This list is deliberately selective. It is not a catalogue of every beekeeping title in print. Each recommendation has a defined use, and older classics are clearly distinguished from current practical guidance. Disease treatments, medicine authorisations, food law, and legal requirements must always be checked against current competent-authority sources regardless of a book’s reputation.

## Best First Books for New Beekeepers

### *The Beekeeper’s Handbook* — Diana Sammataro and Alphonse Avitabile
**Best for:** beginners who want one structured, practical reference covering equipment, biology, seasonal work, inspections, swarming, health, and honey production.

**Why it remains valuable:** clear illustrations and a systematic teaching approach. Use current local guidance for medicines, pests, and legal requirements.

### *Honey Bee Biology and Beekeeping* — Dewey M. Caron and Lawrence J. Connor
**Best for:** readers who want practical management tied closely to bee biology.

**Why it remains valuable:** bridges classroom-level biology and real apiary decision-making better than many purely practical manuals.

### *First Lessons in Beekeeping* — Keith S. Delaplane
**Best for:** concise introductory study and preparation before handling the first colony.

**Why it remains valuable:** compact enough to read before the season while still grounding management in biological principles.

## Comprehensive Reference Works

### *The Hive and the Honey Bee* — edited by Joe M. Graham / Dadant & Sons
**Best for:** serious intermediate and advanced beekeepers, instructors, and readers needing a broad technical reference.

**Strength:** multi-author treatment of bee biology, equipment, management, products, health, and industry topics.

**Caution:** editions span long periods; treatment and regulatory guidance should be checked against current sources.

### *The ABC and XYZ of Bee Culture* — A. I. Root / current edition
**Best for:** encyclopaedic lookup, terminology, historical perspective, and practical context.

**Strength:** one of the foundational reference traditions in modern apiculture.

**Caution:** older editions contain obsolete treatment, safety, and management advice. Use recent editions and cross-check modern health guidance.

## Honey Bee Biology and Colony Organisation

### *The Biology of the Honey Bee* — Mark L. Winston
**Best for:** deep understanding of anatomy, development, behaviour, reproduction, communication, and colony organisation.

**Why it matters:** management makes more sense when the reader understands what the colony is biologically trying to do.

### *The Wisdom of the Hive* — Thomas D. Seeley
**Best for:** advanced understanding of colony-level regulation, foraging, resource allocation, and social organisation.

**Why it matters:** shows how individual worker decisions generate colony-level behaviour.

### *Honeybee Ecology: A Study of Adaptation in Social Life* — Thomas D. Seeley
**Best for:** ecology, nest-site biology, population density, foraging, and the relationship between wild colony biology and managed systems.

### *Honeybee Democracy* — Thomas D. Seeley
**Best for:** swarm decision-making, communication, and collective choice.

**Why it matters to practice:** provides an unusually clear model of how scout bees evaluate and select nest sites.

### *The Lives of Bees* — Thomas D. Seeley
**Best for:** comparing managed beekeeping systems with the ecology of wild-living honey bee colonies.

**Use with care:** the book stimulates useful management questions but should not be interpreted as a universal instruction to copy wild nest conditions in every commercial or disease-regulated setting.

### *The Buzz about Bees: Biology of a Superorganism* — Jürgen Tautz
**Best for:** accessible advanced biology with strong visual explanation of the colony as a superorganism.

## Anatomy

### *Anatomy and Dissection of the Honeybee* — H. A. Dade
**Best for:** detailed practical anatomy, microscopy, teaching, and diagnostic study.

### *Anatomy of the Honey Bee* — R. E. Snodgrass
**Best for:** foundational morphological reference.

**Caution:** terminology is historically important but some modern anatomical interpretations and names have evolved.

## Queen Rearing and Breeding

### *Queen Rearing and Bee Breeding* — Harry H. Laidlaw Jr. and Robert E. Page Jr.
**Best for:** queen biology, controlled queen production, breeding principles, mating systems, and selection.

### *Bee Genetics and Breeding* — edited by Thomas E. Rinderer
**Best for:** advanced readers studying genetics, selection, population biology, and breeding programmes.

**Caution:** important foundational material; supplement with modern Varroa-resistance and genomic-selection research.

## Bee Health, Varroa, and IPM

### *Tools for Varroa Management: A Guide to Effective Varroa Sampling & Control* — Honey Bee Health Coalition, 9th edition, 2026
**Best for:** current Varroa sampling, integrated management, treatment decision-making, and resistance-aware control in its target region.

**Why it is essential:** unlike a fixed textbook chapter, this guide is periodically updated as thresholds, products, resistance, and evidence change.

**Regional caution:** product authorisations and thresholds differ; always check the competent authority where the bees are kept.

### COLOSS BEEBOOK volumes and methods papers
**Best for:** researchers, technical advisers, and advanced beekeepers who need standardised experimental and diagnostic methods.

**Format:** research-method collection rather than a conventional single-volume practical textbook.

## Foraging, Nectar, and Pollination

### *Nectaries and Nectar* — edited by Susan W. Nicolson, Massimo Nepi, and Ettore Pacini
**Best for:** advanced plant–pollinator physiology, nectar production, floral biology, and ecological context.

### *The Social Organization of Honeybees* — J. B. Free
**Best for:** classic behavioural and social organisation research.

### IPBES *Assessment Report on Pollinators, Pollination and Food Production*
**Best for:** broad global evidence on wild and managed pollinators, drivers of decline, agriculture, and conservation.

**Format:** assessment report rather than conventional beekeeping book.

## History and Global Beekeeping

### *The World History of Beekeeping and Honey Hunting* — Eva Crane
**Best for:** serious historical study of honey hunting, hive development, cultural practices, and the global history of apiculture.

### *Bees and Beekeeping: Science, Practice and World Resources* — Eva Crane
**Best for:** historical-global technical reference and understanding how beekeeping systems vary around the world.

**Caution:** invaluable as a reference, but legal, medicine, disease, and commercial information should be updated from current sources.

## Honey, Beeswax, and Hive Products

### FAO — *Value-added Products from Beekeeping*
**Best for:** beeswax, propolis, pollen, royal jelly, venom, and value-added product concepts.

**Strength:** broad international perspective.

**Caution:** product regulation, cosmetics law, supplement claims, and food-safety systems have evolved; use modern legal sources for commercial production.

### International Honey Commission analytical resources
**Best for:** honey-quality professionals, packers, laboratories, and advanced producers.

**Format:** harmonised methods and technical guidance rather than a narrative beekeeping book.

## Honey Processing and Quality

For extraction, processing, storage, authenticity, and food hygiene, no single general beekeeping book should be treated as the final authority. Serious producers should use a combination of:

- the current Codex *Standard for Honey*;
- current national/EU honey law where applicable;
- International Honey Commission methods;
- current food-hygiene guidance;
- specialised peer-reviewed honey-quality literature.

This is preferable to relying on old processing recipes or folklore thresholds.

## Organic Beekeeping and Sustainability

Organic production is regulated differently across markets. Rather than recommending one general “organic beekeeping book” as universal, readers should use:

- the current organic regulation/certification standard for their market;
- certification-body operating guidance;
- current Varroa/IPM evidence;
- pollinator-conservation and biodiversity guidance appropriate to the landscape.

A book describing “natural” or “treatment-free” beekeeping is not automatically a guide to certified organic production.

## Business and Commercial Beekeeping

Commercial beekeeping economics vary strongly by market, labour, pollination demand, climate, and regulation. Useful business reading should therefore be paired with current local information on:

- food-business registration;
- tax;
- insurance;
- employment;
- product liability;
- consumer law;
- labelling;
- online sales;
- trade marks.

General small-business accounting and cost-control books can be more useful than old beekeeping-specific profit claims.

## Research and Evidence Appraisal

### *Cochrane Handbook for Systematic Reviews of Interventions* — current version
**Best for:** understanding evidence synthesis, bias, meta-analysis, certainty, and the difference between one study and a body of evidence.

### PRISMA 2020 Statement and Explanation
**Best for:** understanding how systematic reviews should be reported and how to judge search completeness and selection transparency.

### CONSORT, STROBE, and ARRIVE guideline resources
**Best for:** learning what high-quality reporting should contain in randomised trials, observational studies, and animal research.

These are not beekeeping books, but they are valuable for Chapter 73’s goal: helping beekeepers judge scientific claims instead of accepting them because they appear in a paper.

## Reading Path by Experience Level

### New beekeeper
1. *First Lessons in Beekeeping*.
2. *The Beekeeper’s Handbook*.
3. The relevant national bee-health and food-law guidance.
4. This handbook’s Chapters 15–38 for apiary setup and management.

### Intermediate beekeeper
1. *Honey Bee Biology and Beekeeping*.
2. *The Hive and the Honey Bee*.
3. *The Wisdom of the Hive*.
4. Current HBHC/COLOSS/competent-authority Varroa resources.

### Queen rearer / breeder
1. *Queen Rearing and Bee Breeding*.
2. COLOSS queen-rearing/instrumental-insemination methods.
3. Modern Varroa-resistance and genomic-selection papers.

### Commercial honey producer
1. *The Hive and the Honey Bee* for broad management.
2. Codex and local honey regulations.
3. International Honey Commission methods.
4. Food-hygiene and traceability guidance.
5. Current market-specific business and insurance information.

### Research-oriented beekeeper
1. *The Biology of the Honey Bee*.
2. *The Wisdom of the Hive*.
3. COLOSS BEEBOOK.
4. Current peer-reviewed reviews and meta-analyses.
5. Cochrane/PRISMA/CONSORT/STROBE resources for evidence appraisal.

## Books to Use with Historical Caution

Older classics can be exceptional sources for bee behaviour, traditional hive systems, history, and observation, while simultaneously containing obsolete recommendations for disease treatment, pesticides, medicines, heating, food handling, or equipment safety.

When reading any older beekeeping text:

1. keep biological observations separate from old treatment prescriptions;
2. verify all medicines against current authorised-product information;
3. verify disease-reporting requirements;
4. verify honey composition and labelling law;
5. verify modern understanding of Varroa-virus biology;
6. verify occupational and food safety.

## Selection Rule for Future Editions

A book should remain in this recommended list only if it provides at least one of the following:

- unusually clear instruction;
- enduring foundational science;
- comprehensive reference value;
- specialist depth not duplicated elsewhere;
- important historical context;
- an actively maintained current technical resource.

Popularity alone is not enough.


<a id="reference-78"></a>

# Reference 78 — Beekeeping Organizations

## Reference 78 — Beekeeping Organizations

This section lists organisations that provide recognised scientific, regulatory, industry, educational, food-safety, conservation, or standards functions relevant to beekeeping. It is intentionally selective. Local associations can be extremely valuable for mentoring and practical training, but their advice should not override current competent-authority rules for disease, veterinary medicines, food safety, or legal compliance.

Websites were checked during the 2026 manuscript cycle and should be re-verified before each new edition.

---

## International Apiculture and Bee-Science Organisations

### Apimondia — International Federation of Beekeepers’ Associations
**Scope:** global.

**Role:** international federation connecting national beekeeping organisations, scientists, industry specialists, and apiculture professionals. It operates scientific commissions and convenes international congresses.

**Useful for:** global apiculture networks, conferences, scientific/technical commissions, policy discussions, and international bee-product topics.

**Official website:** https://apimondia.org/

### COLOSS — Prevention of Honey Bee Colony Losses
**Scope:** global research network.

**Role:** international honey bee research association focused on colony losses, pests, pathogens, management, monitoring, and standardised research methods.

**Useful for:** the COLOSS BEEBOOK, colony-loss surveys, Varroa and Tropilaelaps work, standard research protocols, and current bee-health science.

**Official website:** https://coloss.org/

### International Bee Research Association (IBRA)
**Scope:** international.

**Role:** promotes bee science and beekeeping information worldwide and is associated with major apicultural publications, including the *Journal of Apicultural Research* and *Bee World*.

**Useful for:** scientific literature, specialist publications, research communication, books, and education.

**Official website:** https://ibra.org.uk/

### International Honey Commission (IHC)
**Scope:** international honey and bee-product science network.

**Role:** develops and harmonises analytical approaches for honey and other bee products and contributes to international quality/authenticity science.

**Useful for:** honey-analysis methods, authenticity, unifloral honey characterisation, bee-product quality, and laboratory networks.

**Official website:** https://www.ihc-platform.net/

### Honey Bee Health Coalition
**Scope:** primarily North American agricultural systems, with broader technical value.

**Role:** multi-stakeholder coalition involving beekeepers, growers, researchers, government, industry, conservation organisations, and other partners.

**Useful for:** current Varroa-management guidance, hive-health best practices, forage/nutrition resources, and crop-pest stewardship.

**Official website:** https://honeybeehealthcoalition.org/

---

## International Standards, Animal Health, and Food Organisations

### World Organisation for Animal Health (WOAH)
**Scope:** intergovernmental/global.

**Role:** sets international animal-health standards and maintains Terrestrial Code/Manual resources for listed honey bee diseases and infestations.

**Useful for:** international context on AFB, EFB, varroosis, Tropilaelaps, small hive beetle, acarapisosis, diagnostics, movement, and reporting frameworks.

**Official website:** https://www.woah.org/

### Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)
**Scope:** global.

**Role:** agriculture, food systems, rural development, pollination, and beekeeping development.

**Useful for:** beekeeping development, pollination, value-added hive products, food systems, and international agricultural context.

**Official website:** https://www.fao.org/

### Codex Alimentarius Commission
**Scope:** international food standards.

**Role:** FAO/WHO food-standards programme.

**Useful for:** the *Standard for Honey*, general food-hygiene principles, contaminants, food-labelling frameworks, and international trade reference.

**Official website:** https://www.fao.org/fao-who-codexalimentarius/

### International Organization for Standardization (ISO)
**Scope:** international standards.

**Role:** develops voluntary international standards.

**Useful for:** bee-product specifications such as bee pollen, raw propolis, and royal jelly; laboratory competence; quality-management frameworks.

**Official website:** https://www.iso.org/

---

## European Union Scientific and Regulatory Resources

### European Union Reference Laboratory for Bee Health — ANSES
**Scope:** European Union reference-laboratory network.

**Role:** harmonises and supports official laboratory diagnosis for designated bee-health hazards, including AFB and important parasitic/exotic pests.

**Useful for:** diagnostic methods, reference-laboratory updates, Tropilaelaps and small-hive-beetle surveillance, and EU laboratory networks.

**Official website:** https://sitesv2.anses.fr/en/minisite/abeilles/welcome-website-eu-rl-bee-health

### European Food Safety Authority (EFSA)
**Scope:** European Union scientific risk assessment.

**Role:** independent scientific advice on food/feed safety, pesticides, animal health, contaminants, and related risks.

**Useful for:** pesticide-risk science, food contaminants, bee-health risk assessments, and scientific opinions supporting EU policy.

**Official website:** https://www.efsa.europa.eu/

### European Commission
**Scope:** European Union.

**Role:** EU legislation, policy, implementing/delegated acts, food law, organic production, veterinary medicines, animal health, trade, and consumer regulation.

**Useful for:** current legal texts and authoritative policy context rather than informal summaries.

**Official website:** https://commission.europa.eu/

### European Beekeeping Association (EBA)
**Scope:** European beekeeper network.

**Role:** represents beekeeper interests and supports cooperation on apiculture and bee-product quality.

**Useful for:** European sector perspectives, conferences, and beekeeper-industry issues.

**Website:** verify current official EBA domain before publication because organisational web addresses can change.

---

## Ireland — Government, Food Safety, Conservation, and Beekeeper Organisations

### Department of Agriculture, Food and the Marine (DAFM)
**Scope:** Republic of Ireland.

**Role:** competent government department for agriculture, animal health, veterinary medicines, bee-health measures, organic production, and related regulation.

**Useful for:** beekeeper/apiary requirements, animal health, notifiable disease, veterinary medicines, organic rules, movement/import issues, and official agriculture information.

**Official website:** https://www.gov.ie/en/organisation/department-of-agriculture-food-and-the-marine/

### Food Safety Authority of Ireland (FSAI)
**Scope:** Republic of Ireland.

**Role:** national food-safety authority providing guidance and coordinating food-law enforcement.

**Useful for:** honey composition/labelling, food-business registration, hygiene/HACCP, allergens, traceability, recalls, online food sales, and the 2026 honey-origin rules.

**Official website:** https://www.fsai.ie/

### National Biodiversity Data Centre
**Scope:** Ireland.

**Role:** national biodiversity data, monitoring, citizen science, and coordination of major biodiversity programmes.

**Useful for:** wild-pollinator monitoring, biodiversity evidence, species records, and conservation resources.

**Official website:** https://biodiversityireland.ie/

### All-Ireland Pollinator Plan
**Scope:** island of Ireland; voluntary framework.

**Role:** evidence-based pollinator-conservation programme managed by the National Biodiversity Data Centre.

**Useful for:** habitat actions for wild bees, hoverflies, moths, farmland, businesses, public land, transport corridors, communities, and the 2026–2030 “pockets of nature every 200 metres” landscape vision.

**Official website:** https://pollinators.ie/

### Federation of Irish Beekeepers’ Associations (FIBKA)
**Scope:** Ireland.

**Role:** federation of local beekeeping associations providing education, member support, representation, examinations, and beekeeping resources.

**Useful for:** finding local associations, training, mentoring, practical education, meetings, and member services.

**Official website:** https://irishbeekeeping.ie/

### Irish Beekeepers’ Association CLG (IBA)
**Scope:** Ireland.

**Role:** national beekeeping membership organisation supporting education, practical beekeeping, bee health, and beekeeper interests.

**Useful for:** training, events, association support, practical information, and community connections.

**Official website:** https://irishbeekeepers.ie/

### Teagasc
**Scope:** Republic of Ireland.

**Role:** Agriculture and Food Development Authority providing agricultural research, advisory, education, and training services.

**Useful for:** farm context, environmental management, business planning, land use, and agricultural advisory resources relevant to apiary sites and pollination relationships.

**Official website:** https://www.teagasc.ie/

---

## United Kingdom and Northern Ireland

### National Bee Unit (NBU) / BeeBase
**Scope:** England and Wales, with resources of broader technical value; statutory structures differ elsewhere in the UK.

**Role:** government bee-health service providing inspection, surveillance, statutory pest/disease information, training, and BeeBase resources.

**Useful for:** AFB/EFB, exotic pests, inspection resources, pest/disease identification, and current statutory guidance.

**Official website:** https://www.nationalbeeunit.com/

### Department of Agriculture, Environment and Rural Affairs (DAERA), Northern Ireland
**Scope:** Northern Ireland.

**Role:** agriculture, animal health, environment, and statutory bee-health responsibilities.

**Useful for:** Northern Ireland foulbrood guidance, bee-health regulation, reporting, and local agricultural rules.

**Official website:** https://www.daera-ni.gov.uk/

### Agri-Food and Biosciences Institute (AFBI)
**Scope:** Northern Ireland with wider scientific relevance.

**Role:** laboratory, research, and diagnostic services in agriculture and biosciences.

**Useful for:** honey bee pest/disease testing and laboratory information.

**Official website:** https://www.afbini.gov.uk/

### British Beekeepers Association (BBKA)
**Scope:** England and affiliated networks.

**Role:** beekeeper education, examinations, local associations, public engagement, and practical resources.

**Useful for:** training and association-level practical support.

**Official website:** https://www.bbka.org.uk/

### Bee Improvement and Bee Breeders Association (BIBBA)
**Scope:** UK and international interest.

**Role:** honey bee improvement, breeding, local adaptation, and conservation of appropriate bee populations.

**Useful for:** breeding discussions, native/local bees, queen rearing, and genetic improvement.

**Official website:** https://bibba.com/

---

## North America

### USDA Agricultural Research Service (ARS) — Bee Research Programmes
**Scope:** United States; international research relevance.

**Role:** federal agricultural research covering honey bee breeding, genetics, Varroa, nutrition, pathogens, and pollination.

**Useful for:** primary research and technical resources.

**Official website:** https://www.ars.usda.gov/

### United States Department of Agriculture — APHIS
**Scope:** United States.

**Role:** animal/plant health, import/export controls, pests, and biosecurity.

**Useful for:** import/export and regulated pest context; specific beekeeping requirements vary by state as well.

**Official website:** https://www.aphis.usda.gov/

### American Beekeeping Federation (ABF)
**Scope:** United States.

**Role:** beekeeper trade/membership organisation representing commercial and hobbyist interests.

**Useful for:** industry networking, conferences, policy, education, and commercial beekeeping context.

**Official website:** https://abfnet.org/

### Canadian Honey Council
**Scope:** Canada.

**Role:** national apiculture industry organisation.

**Useful for:** Canadian industry information, honey, trade, bee health, and producer representation.

**Official website:** https://honeycouncil.ca/

---

## Australia and New Zealand

### Australian Honey Bee Industry Council (AHBIC)
**Scope:** Australia.

**Role:** national representative body for the honey bee industry.

**Useful for:** industry biosecurity, policy, Varroa-era management context, market issues, and national sector information.

**Official website:** https://honeybee.org.au/

### Plant Health Australia / BeeAware
**Scope:** Australia.

**Role:** biosecurity and pest-preparedness resources, including honey bee pests and diseases.

**Useful for:** pest identification, biosecurity plans, exotic threats, and surveillance.

**Official website:** https://beeaware.org.au/

### Apiculture New Zealand
**Scope:** New Zealand.

**Role:** national apiculture industry body.

**Useful for:** New Zealand beekeeping industry, biosecurity, market, education, and sector information.

**Official website:** https://apinz.org.nz/

---

## How to Use Organisations Correctly

A beekeeper may need several organisations for one problem:

- **local association** for mentoring and practical help;
- **competent authority** for notifiable disease, legal movement, or veterinary medicine;
- **food-safety authority** for honey processing, labelling, and recalls;
- **research network/university** for scientific evidence;
- **standards body/laboratory network** for analytical methods;
- **industry association** for markets, training, advocacy, and professional networking;
- **conservation organisation** for wild-pollinator and habitat work.

No single membership organisation replaces the competent authority.

## Final Publication Check

Before release, verify:

1. organisation still exists under the stated name;
2. official domain resolves correctly;
3. jurisdiction and regulatory role remain accurate;
4. no unofficial social-media page has replaced the institutional source;
5. new official bee-health or food-safety body has not superseded a listed organisation.


<a id="reference-79"></a>

# Reference 79 — Useful Websites

## Reference 79 — Useful Websites

This section prioritises official, scientific, regulatory, and high-value technical websites. It is not a directory of commercial suppliers or general beekeeping blogs. Regulatory links are especially time-sensitive and must be checked again before each edition.

Access/update review for this manuscript: September 2026.

---

## International Bee Health and Research

### COLOSS
https://coloss.org/

**Use for:** international colony-loss research, BEEBOOK standard methods, Varroa, Tropilaelaps, bee-health task forces, current publications, and research networks.

### COLOSS BEEBOOK
https://coloss.org/activities/beebook/

**Use for:** peer-reviewed standard methods in honey bee biology, pests/pathogens, breeding, and hive-product research.

### Apimondia
https://apimondia.org/

**Use for:** international beekeeping federation activities, scientific commissions, congresses, and global apiculture networks.

### International Bee Research Association (IBRA)
https://ibra.org.uk/

**Use for:** bee-science publications, *Journal of Apicultural Research*, *Bee World*, specialist books, and research communication.

### World Honey Bee Health
https://worldhoneybeehealth.com/

**Use for:** distribution information on major honey bee pests and pathogens; associated with the COLOSS World Honey Bee Health effort. Verify current distribution information before making movement or regulatory decisions.

---

## International Animal-Health and Diagnostic Standards

### World Organisation for Animal Health (WOAH)
https://www.woah.org/

**Use for:** international animal-health standards, Terrestrial Code, Terrestrial Manual, listed bee diseases/infestations, diagnostic and trade context.

### WOAH — Diseases of Bees
https://www.woah.org/en/disease/diseases-of-bees/

**Use for:** consolidated international overview of listed honey bee diseases and infestations.

### European Union Reference Laboratory for Bee Health — ANSES
https://sitesv2.anses.fr/en/minisite/abeilles/welcome-website-eu-rl-bee-health

**Use for:** official/reference diagnostic methods, EU National Reference Laboratory network, AFB, Varroa, small hive beetle, and Tropilaelaps updates.

---

## Varroa and Hive-Health Management

### Honey Bee Health Coalition
https://honeybeehealthcoalition.org/

**Use for:** current Varroa-management guidance, hive-health best management practices, forage/nutrition resources, and crop-pest stewardship.

### USDA Agricultural Research Service
https://www.ars.usda.gov/

**Use for:** honey bee breeding, Varroa, nutrition, genetics, pathogen, pollination, and agricultural research. Search within ARS for Bee Research Laboratory and Honey Bee Breeding, Genetics and Physiology programmes.

### Penn State Extension — Beekeeping
https://extension.psu.edu/

**Use for:** research-based practical extension material, including Nosema, pests, diseases, colony management, and pollinator topics. Search the site for the current honey bee publication rather than relying on an old saved PDF.

### University of Florida Honey Bee Research and Extension Laboratory
https://entnemdept.ufl.edu/honey-bee/

**Use for:** honey bee pests, management, extension, research, and identification resources.

---

## Honey Standards, Quality, and Authenticity

### Codex Alimentarius
https://www.fao.org/fao-who-codexalimentarius/

https://www.ihc-platform.net/

### International Honey Commission — Publications
https://www.ihc-platform.net/publications/

### ISO
https://www.iso.org/

https://commission.europa.eu/

https://eur-lex.europa.eu/

https://www.efsa.europa.eu/

## Research Literature and Evidence Finding

### PubMed
https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/

**Use for:** biomedical and life-science literature searches, including bee venom allergy, microbiology, toxicology, and some bee-health research.

### Crossref
https://search.crossref.org/

**Use for:** DOI and bibliographic verification.

### Google Scholar
https://scholar.google.com/

**Use for:** broad discovery of scientific literature and citation chains. It is a discovery tool, not a quality filter; verify the actual paper and journal.

### Directory of Open Access Journals
https://doaj.org/

**Use for:** finding open-access journals and articles. Inclusion does not remove the need to appraise study quality.

---

## Weather, Mapping, and Phenology

Local weather and plant phenology often matter more than general climate averages. Useful sources include:

- national meteorological services;
- agricultural extension crop-development services;
- verified local weather stations;
- biodiversity/plant-recording schemes;
- the beekeeper’s own multi-year bloom and hive-scale records.

Never use a generic flowering calendar as if it were valid for every altitude, latitude, cultivar, or year.

---

## How to Judge a Beekeeping Website

Prefer a website when it provides:

1. identifiable institution or authorship;
2. publication/update date;
3. references or evidence trail;
4. clear jurisdiction;
5. distinction between law and advice;
6. current product/medicine information where relevant;
7. correction or update mechanisms.

Be cautious when a page:

- gives a fixed treatment recipe without jurisdiction or product label;
- claims one visible sign proves a disease;
- uses “natural” as proof of safety;
- sells the same remedy it claims solves every problem;
- presents testimonials as clinical evidence;
- makes disease-treatment claims for foods/supplements without regulatory basis;
- has no date despite discussing fast-changing law or medicine authorisation.

## Link-Maintenance Rule

Before final publication and before each new edition:

- test every URL;
- replace redirected legacy links with current institutional landing pages;
- verify the page still belongs to the stated organisation;
- record the current access date for legal/regulatory resources;
- prefer a durable institutional home page when deep links change frequently;
- never preserve a dead link merely because it appeared in an earlier edition.

## Annual site review

- [ ] Access still safe.
- [ ] Stands stable.
- [ ] Vegetation managed.
- [ ] Flood/drainage condition acceptable.
- [ ] Public flight paths acceptable.
- [ ] Water available.
- [ ] Apiary density still appropriate to forage.
- [ ] New pesticide or land-use risks reviewed.
- [ ] Nearby colony density/robbing risk reviewed.
- [ ] Site records updated.

# Appendix R — Traceability Lot-Code Template

One practical structure:

`APIARY-HARVESTDATE-PROCESSBATCH-PACKRUN`

Example:

`DUB03-20260822-E04-P02`

Meaning:

- `DUB03` — apiary identifier;
- `20260822` — harvest date;
- `E04` — fourth extraction/processing batch;
- `P02` — second packing run.

The exact format is optional. The required outcome is unique, durable, and reconstructable traceability.

## Lot-code rules

- never reuse a code for a different product;
- preserve source-lot links after blending;
- record rework explicitly;
- keep customer/distribution links where required;
- do not encode information so cryptically that only one person can interpret it.

| Internal lot created | |
| Processing/packing batches | |
| Quantity produced | |
| Customer / channel | |
| Date dispatched | |
| Quantity dispatched | |
| Remaining stock | |

**Internal traceability should connect the middle of the chain, not only supplier and customer names.**

---

# Appendix T — Mock Recall Exercise

## Scenario

Define a hypothetical affected lot and reason, for example:

- wrong origin percentage;
- unauthorised residue;
- glass contamination;
- high moisture/fermentation;
- incorrect allergen label in a flavoured honey product.

## Test

- [ ] Identify affected source lot.
- [ ] Identify all derived processing/packing lots.
- [ ] Determine total quantity produced.
- [ ] Determine quantity still in stock.
- [ ] Identify every customer/distribution destination.
- [ ] Calculate quantity unaccounted for.
- [ ] Produce draft withdrawal/recall communication.
- [ ] Time the exercise.
- [ ] Record gaps and corrective actions.

## Mock recall result

| Measure | Result |
|---|---|
| Start time | |
| Trace completed | |
| % product accounted for | |
| Missing records | |
| Corrective actions | |
| Responsible person | |
| Follow-up completion date | |

## Breakage response

1. Stop production.
2. Segregate exposed food and packaging.
3. Remove visible fragments using controlled tools.
4. Clean the defined contamination zone.
5. inspect and document.
6. discard affected food where safety cannot be demonstrated.
7. release only after verification.

## Calculation

`full cost per saleable unit = total attributable annual cost ÷ saleable units`

# Appendix AF — Annual Operation Review

At the end of each season, review:

### Colony performance
- winter survival;
- queen replacement;
- Varroa trends;
- swarming;
- disease events;
- productivity;
- feed use.

### Product performance
- harvest mass;
- moisture;
- extraction loss;
- product returns;
- fermentation;
- authenticity/origin issues;
- lot traceability.

### Business performance
- revenue by product/channel;
- full cost per unit;
- labour hours;
- capital utilisation;
- insurance claims/incidents;
- customer retention;
- unsold stock.

### Sustainability
- forage gaps;
- apiary density;
- habitat actions;
- pesticide incidents;
- fuel/transport use;
- waste and wax recycling.

### Next-year actions
1. keep what worked;
2. stop or redesign what failed;
3. define three measurable improvements;
4. assign dates and responsible people;
5. update the annual monitoring and purchasing plan.

---

## Appendix Use Rule

Forms are only useful when they change decisions. Do not collect data because a template exists. Define who records the information, when it is reviewed, what triggers action, how long it must be retained, and whether a legal or certification body requires a different format.


<a id="reference-81"></a>

# Reference 81 — Tables

## Reference 81 — Tables

This section consolidates the most useful lookup tables from the handbook into one reference location. It does not replace the explanatory chapters. Values that depend on law, climate, product authorisation, or regional thresholds are deliberately identified as context-dependent.

---

## Table 81.1 — Honey Bee Development: Typical Timelines

| Caste | Egg | Larva before capping | Capped development | Typical emergence from egg |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Queen | ~3 days | ~5–6 days | ~7–8 days | ~16 days |
| Worker | ~3 days | ~6 days | ~12 days | ~21 days |
| Drone | ~3 days | ~6–7 days | ~14–15 days | ~24 days |

**Use:** planning queen rearing, brood assessments, splits, and emergence timing.

**Caution:** biological development varies with temperature, genetics, nutrition, and colony conditions. These are standard practical approximations, not exact clocks.

---

## Table 81.2 — Adult Castes at a Glance

| Feature | Queen | Worker | Drone |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sex | Female | Female | Male |
| Normal reproductive role | Principal egg layer | Normally non-reproductive | Mates with queen |
| Sting | Present, specialised | Present | Absent |
| Pollen baskets | No | Yes | No |
| Wax glands | Not functionally used as in workers | Functional | No practical comb-building role |
| Development | Fastest | Intermediate | Slowest |
| Typical number in colony | Usually 1 | Thousands | Seasonal; hundreds–thousands |

---

## Table 81.3 — Routine Inspection Sequence

| Stage | Main questions |
|---|---|
| Before opening | Weather safe? Flight normal? Robbing? Dead bees? Structural problems? |
| Queen status | Eggs? Young larvae? Queen seen/marked? Evidence of queen failure? |
| Brood | Pattern? Quantity? Age range? Abnormal larvae/cappings? |
| Adult population | Enough bees to cover brood? Population increasing or shrinking? |
| Food | Honey/nectar? Pollen? Risk of starvation? |
| Swarm status | Queen cells? Congestion? Backfilling? |
| Parasites/disease | Varroa monitoring due? Abnormal brood/adults? |
| Equipment | Space? Comb condition? Moisture/leaks? |
| Close-out | Action minimal and justified? Follow-up date recorded? |

---

## Table 81.4 — Colony Emergency Priorities

| Finding | Immediate priority | Avoid |
|---|---|---|
| Active starvation | Put safe accessible food at cluster | Long cold inspection |
| Severe robbing | Remove exposed feed/honey; reduce defensible entrance | Open supers and spilled syrup |
| Transport overheating | Restore ventilation/shade | Sealing populous hive without airflow |
| Storm-opened hive | Reassemble, weatherproof, stop honey exposure | Randomly moving comb among colonies |
| Sudden mass mortality | Preserve evidence and investigate | Discarding all dead bees before sampling |
| Suspicious brood disease | Stop transfers; isolate tools; seek confirmation | Strengthening other colonies with suspect brood |
| Flooding | Protect people first; move only if safe | Entering dangerous floodwater |

---

## Table 81.5 — Queen Problem Differential

| Observation | Important possibilities |
|---|---|
| No eggs, no young larvae | Queenlessness, brood break, newly introduced/virgin queen, recent swarm |
| Small irregular worker-brood area | Failing queen, disease, nutrition, chilled brood, Varroa-associated loss |
| Multiple eggs/cell, drone brood in worker cells | Laying workers or occasionally inexperienced newly laying queen |
| Repeated supersedure cells | Queen quality/age, injury, colony response |
| Queen present but little brood | Season, nutrition, queen failure, disease/parasites, brood-space restriction |

---

## Table 81.6 — Brood Disease / Stress Differential

| Sign | Main differentials | Confirmation principle |
|---|---|---|
| Patchy brood | AFB, EFB, Varroa, queen failure, chilled brood, nutrition, hygienic removal | Examine individual cells + history; sample if suspicious |
| Sunken/perforated capped cells, brown remains | AFB among important possibilities | Stop transfer and obtain competent confirmation |
| Twisted yellow/brown open larvae | EFB, chilling, other larval disorders | Field/lab confirmation as needed |
| White/grey/black mummies | Chalkbrood | Characteristic mummies + colony context |
| Sac-like larvae | Sacbrood | Pattern + laboratory confirmation if atypical |
| Uncapped pupae/deformed adults | Varroa-associated viral damage, hygienic removal | Quantify Varroa; assess virus/disease context |

---

## Table 81.7 — Adult-Bee Syndrome Differential

| Sign | Important possibilities |
|---|---|
| Crawling/trembling | CBPV, acute viruses, pesticide exposure, starvation, tracheal mites, cold/injury |
| Deformed wings | Varroa-associated DWV strongly important; developmental injury also possible |
| Dark hairless adults | CBPV among differentials; abrasion/robbing/age also possible |
| Faecal staining | Confinement, feed/digestive problems, nosemosis among possibilities |
| Sudden entrance mortality | Toxic exposure, robbing/fighting, acute disease, overheating, transport stress |

---

## Table 81.8 — Major Honey Bee Parasites

| Parasite | Location | Key biological feature | Practical detection |
|---|---|---|---|
| *Varroa destructor* | Adult bees + capped brood | Reproduces in capped brood; feeds on host tissues; major virus vector | Validated adult-bee sample; brood/natural-fall methods as supporting tools |
| *Tropilaelaps* spp. | Mainly brood-associated | Rapid reproduction, strong brood dependence, emerging range | Brood examination, mite fall, adult sampling, official/molecular confirmation |
| *Acarapis woodi* | Adult thoracic tracheae | Microscopic internal mite | Tracheal dissection or validated lab method |

---

## Table 81.9 — Varroa Monitoring Methods

| Method | Main use | Bees sacrificed? | Main limitation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Alcohol/detergent wash | Quantitative adult-bee infestation estimate | Yes | Queen must be excluded; technique/sample matter |
| Sugar roll | Non-lethal estimate | Usually no | Humidity and technique affect performance |
| Natural mite fall | Presence/trend/treatment drop | No | Not always a direct substitute for adult-bee infestation |
| Brood uncapping | Reproduction/presence in brood | Brood destroyed locally | Weak as sole whole-colony threshold measure unless protocol validates it |

**Calculation:** `mites per 100 bees = (mites recovered ÷ bees sampled) × 100`.

**Threshold rule:** use current regional guidance; do not treat one number as universal.

---

## Table 81.10 — Varroa Control Decision Factors

| Factor | Why it matters |
|---|---|
| Current measured mite pressure | Determines urgency |
| Season / winter-bee timing | Damage can become irreversible before visible collapse |
| Brood amount | Determines how many mites are protected under cappings |
| Honey supers / harvest status | Controls legal food-compatible options |
| Temperature | Affects efficacy and colony safety of some products |
| Previous active ingredients | Resistance management |
| Neighbouring collapse / robbing | Reinvasion risk |
| Queen/colony strength | Tolerance of manipulations/treatments |
| Post-treatment test plan | Required to verify control |

---

## Table 81.11 — Disease, Parasite, Pest, Predator, Stress

| Category | Meaning | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Infectious disease | Harm caused by pathogen | AFB, EFB, chalkbrood, sacbrood |
| Parasite | Lives on/in host and takes resources | Varroa, Tropilaelaps, tracheal mite |
| Hive pest/scavenger | Damages comb/stores/nest | Small hive beetle, wax moth |
| Predator | Captures/consumes bees | Hornets, some wasps, birds |
| Environmental/toxic stress | Non-infectious harmful condition | Starvation, pesticide poisoning, heat, chilling |

---

## Table 81.12 — Nectar Flow: Observation to Decision

| Observation | Interpretation | Management response |
|---|---|---|
| Flowers abundant, hive weight flat | Source may be weak or colony unable to exploit it | Check bee visitation, weather, colony strength |
| Sustained multi-day gain | Useful flow active | Provide storage space before congestion |
| Strong daytime gain, large overnight loss | Dilute nectar being ripened | Do not equate gross gain with final honey mass |
| Fresh white wax + rapidly filling supers | Strong intake/construction | Add space appropriately |
| Weight declines after bloom | Dearth/consumption | Protect stores; control robbing |
| Sudden large loss | Swarm or management event | Investigate immediately |

---

## Table 81.13 — Harvest Readiness

| Finding | Interpretation | Action |
|---|---|---|
| Mostly capped; representative moisture comfortably within specification | Mature crop likely | Harvest if treatment/colony-store checks pass |
| Many uncapped cells; low uniform measured moisture | Could be mature but uncapped | Harvest may be acceptable within quality system |
| Many uncapped cells; variable/high moisture | Uneven ripening | Delay or segregate wet frames |
| Brood in super | Not ordinary harvest comb | Segregate brood frame |
| Treatment withdrawal incomplete | Legal/food-safety restriction | Do not harvest affected honey for sale |
| Chemical/flood/fire taint suspected | Possible contamination | Segregate and obtain competent assessment |

---

## Table 81.14 — Honey Quality Parameters

| Parameter | What it helps assess | What it does not prove alone |
|---|---|---|
| Moisture | Fermentation risk, composition, storage margin | Authenticity |
| Water activity | Microbial water availability | Total composition/origin |
| HMF | Heat/storage history indicator | Exact heating history or syrup addition |
| Diastase | Enzyme status affected by source/heat/age | Exact age or authenticity |
| Conductivity | Broad honey-type/mineral classification | Exact botanical origin |
| Free acidity | Composition/fermentation clues | Cause of high acidity alone |
| Sugar profile | Composition and some authenticity/origin clues | Every fraud type |
| Pollen | Botanical/geographical support | Exact nectar percentage without expert interpretation |
| Stable isotopes | Some exogenous sugar adulteration | Every adulteration route |
| NMR / MS fingerprint | Broad authenticity/origin patterns | Certainty without valid reference data |

---

## Table 81.15 — Selected EU Honey Composition Examples (Check Current Law)

The following are examples from the current EU honey framework during the 2026 manuscript cycle. They are **not universal global operating targets**.

| Parameter | General EU example | Important caveat |
|---|---|---|
| Moisture | ≤20% for honey in general | Named exceptions exist (e.g. heather/baker’s honey categories) |
| Free acidity | ≤50 meq/kg in general | Baker’s honey differs |
| Conductivity | Often ≤0.8 mS/cm for many blossom honeys | Honeydew, chestnut, and named botanical exceptions differ |
| Diastase | Generally ≥8 Schade | Naturally low-enzyme honey exception exists with low HMF condition |
| HMF | Generally ≤40 mg/kg after processing/blending | Tropical-origin exception exists |

**Before printing:** verify consolidated EU Directive 2001/110/EC and current national transposition.

---

## Table 81.16 — Extraction Equipment: Main Trade-offs

| Equipment | Strength | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| Tangential extractor | Supports fragile comb; effective on viscous honey | Frames need turning/reversal; slower |
| Radial extractor | High capacity; both sides extract in normal cycle | Fragile/very viscous comb can still be difficult |
| Manual drive | Simple; low cost; direct speed feel | Labour/time |
| Motorised drive | Throughput, repeatability, automation | Electrical/mechanical hazards and cost |
| Coarse strainer | Removes wax/debris with minimal product alteration | Does not create polished ultra-clear product |
| Fine filtration | Removes finer material | Can clog, require warming/pressure, and may alter pollen content/product category |

---

## Table 81.17 — Honey Storage Changes

| Observation | Likely issue | Response principle |
|---|---|---|
| Fine uniform crystallisation | Normal physical change | Accept if product specification permits |
| Coarse crystals | Texture defect | Controlled re-liquefaction/rework if otherwise sound |
| Liquid layer over crystals | Phase separation | Check moisture by phase/lot; assess fermentation risk |
| Gas + alcoholic/sour odour | Fermentation likely | Segregate; do not release as ordinary table honey |
| High-temperature history | HMF/enzyme/aroma risk | Review duration; test if needed |
| Missing lot label | Traceability failure | Quarantine until identity is resolved |

---

## Table 81.18 — Hive-Product Preservation Priorities

| Product | Main stability risk | Typical preservation principle |
|---|---|---|
| Honey | Moisture, heat, fermentation, contamination | Dry sealed storage; controlled temperature |
| Comb honey | Heat-softened wax, storage pests, odour | Cool protected packing; validated physical pest control |
| Beeswax | Heat/fire, odour, residues/adulteration | Clean source separation; cool dry storage |
| Bee pollen | High moisture/microbial growth | Rapid freezing or validated drying; sealed storage |
| Propolis | Oxidation, contamination, solvent/product stability | Clean collection; cool/dark storage; defined extraction system |
| Royal jelly | Rapid biochemical deterioration | Strict cold chain / freezing or validated preservation |
| Bee venom | Moisture, contamination, allergen exposure | Dry controlled storage; specialist safety/quality system |

---

## Table 81.19 — Traceability Levels

| Level | Minimum question answered |
|---|---|
| External backward | Who supplied this material/product? |
| External forward | Which customer/business received it? |
| Internal | Which incoming lots became which process/pack lots? |
| Apiary-level | Which colony/apiary/harvest generated the material? |
| Mass balance | Are quantities and blend percentages reconstructable? |
| Recall-ready | Can all affected product be located quickly and accounted for? |

---

## Table 81.20 — Example Lot Architecture

| Component | Example | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| Apiary | DUB03 | Apiary code |
| Harvest date | 20260822 | 22 Aug 2026 |
| Extraction batch | E04 | Fourth extraction batch |
| Packing run | P02 | Second packing run |
| Complete code | DUB03-20260822-E04-P02 | Traceable lot identifier |

A different coding scheme is acceptable if unique, documented, durable, and reconstructable.

---

## Table 81.21 — Food-Safety Hazard Categories

| Hazard | Examples in beekeeping/honey business | Typical controls |
|---|---|---|
| Biological | Fermentation yeasts, pests, contaminated raw products | Moisture control, hygiene, cold chain where required, pest exclusion |
| Chemical | Veterinary residues, pesticides, cleaning chemicals, fuel, lubricants | Authorised-use records, segregation, supplier control, rinse/dry verification |
| Physical | Glass, metal, plastic, wood, insects | Equipment inspection, strainers, glass policy, housekeeping |
| Allergen | Nuts/milk/spices in value-added products; propolis sensitivity | Supplier specs, segregation, label control, cleaning validation |

---

## Table 81.22 — Business Cost Structure

| Cost category | Examples |
|---|---|
| Colony biological inputs | Queens, replacement colonies, feed, monitoring |
| Production consumables | Frames, foundation, treatments, fuel |
| Processing | Extraction, heating, electricity, cleaning, laboratory testing |
| Packaging | Jars, lids, labels, cartons |
| Sales | Market fees, website, payment fees, shipping |
| Fixed overhead | Insurance, certification, rent, software, licences |
| Labour | Paid labour + owner labour |
| Capital | Depreciation, finance, maintenance, replacement reserve |
| Loss | Winter mortality, rejected product, breakage, unsold stock |

---

## Table 81.23 — Make-or-Buy Equipment Decision

| Question | Why it matters |
|---|---|
| Annual throughput? | Determines capacity need |
| Labour cost saved? | Equipment may buy time rather than only volume |
| Utilisation rate? | Low-use assets have high cost/unit |
| Outsourcing price? | Benchmark against ownership |
| Cleaning/changeover? | Throughput is not only machine speed |
| Breakdown consequence? | Downtime during harvest can be costly |
| Resale/useful life? | Affects total cost of ownership |
| Food/legal compliance? | Cheap unsuitable equipment can be unusable |

---

## Table 81.24 — Conservation Action Hierarchy

| Priority | Action |
|---|---|
| 1 | Protect existing high-quality habitat and nesting sites |
| 2 | Reduce pesticide exposure and unnecessary disturbance |
| 3 | Restore season-long diverse forage |
| 4 | Improve nesting resources and soil/hedgerow structure |
| 5 | Connect habitat patches across the landscape |
| 6 | Monitor wild pollinators and vegetation outcomes |
| 7 | Evaluate managed honey-bee stocking relative to forage/sensitive habitat |

**Important:** adding honey bee hives is not automatically a wild-pollinator conservation action.

---

## Table 81.25 — Scientific Evidence Appraisal

| Question | Stronger evidence indicator |
|---|---|
| Was there a control group? | Appropriate contemporaneous control |
| Randomisation? | Allocation not chosen by investigator/condition |
| Replication? | Multiple independent colonies/apiaries, not only many bees from one colony |
| Effect size reported? | Magnitude + confidence interval, not only p-value |
| Field relevance? | Conditions resemble intended management context |
| Repeated independently? | Multiple research groups/locations |
| Review evidence? | Systematic review/meta-analysis or guideline synthesis |
| Product/legal fit? | Intervention authorised and operationally applicable |

---

## Table 81.26 — Technology Adoption

| Criterion | Question |
|---|---|
| Problem fit | What decision does the technology improve? |
| Validation | Has it been independently tested in realistic apiaries? |
| Error | What are false positive/negative consequences? |
| Cost | Purchase + subscription + calibration + replacement? |
| Labour | Does it truly save work after maintenance/data review? |
| Interoperability | Can data be exported? |
| Ownership/privacy | Who owns colony/location/customer data? |
| Failure mode | What happens without power/network/vendor? |
| Biological ground truth | How will sensor output be checked against real colonies? |

---

## Table 81.27 — Record-Retention Priority

| Record | Why retain it |
|---|---|
| Colony inspections | Management history and diagnosis |
| Queen lineage | Breeding and performance |
| Varroa results | Risk trend and treatment timing |
| Veterinary medicines | Legal compliance, residues, efficacy |
| Movements | Biosecurity and tracing |
| Harvest/moisture | Food quality and lot identity |
| Processing/blending | Origin percentages and mass balance |
| Packing/distribution | Recall capability |
| Supplier/customer records | One-step-back/forward traceability |
| Incidents/claims | Safety and insurance evidence |
| Organic/certification records | Audit evidence |

Retention periods must follow applicable law, certification, tax, insurance, and customer requirements.

---

## Table 81.28 — What Not to Treat as Universal

| Topic | Why no universal number/rule exists |
|---|---|
| Varroa treatment threshold | Region, season, virus pressure, method, management differ |
| Feeding amount | Colony size, stores, weather, feed type differ |
| Winter stores | Climate/hive type/colony size differ |
| Harvest moisture target | Legal market, storage, floral source, yeast risk differ |
| Honey shelf life | Product, packaging, storage, quality specification differ |
| Apiary carrying capacity | Landscape and competing pollinators/colonies differ |
| Organic rules | Certification system and jurisdiction differ |
| Medicine choice | Authorisation and resistance differ |
| Insurance cover | Business model, contract, jurisdiction, insurer differ |

---

## Table Register Maintenance

During final layout:

1. assign final table numbers consistently by chapter/reference section;
2. remove any consolidated table that adds no lookup value beyond the chapter;
3. check every unit and abbreviation;
4. update legal tables immediately before publication;
5. ensure tables fit print width or are redesigned rather than reduced to unreadable type;
6. provide accessible text descriptions for complex tables in digital editions.

PBH-v1 has passed render/accessibility proof and the final editorial visual spot-check.

Issue #100 remains open because figure placement, page-context review and final PDF/EPUB/print proof are still required.

---

## 1. Inventory Audit

The final pre-production audit confirms:

- every canonical Chapter **1–74** now has a dedicated standard file named `docs/illustration-plans/chapter-NN-illustration-plan.md`;
- the formatting validator discovered the remaining Chapter 22–25 naming gap and dedicated standard plans were added before publication assembly was approved;
- the archived noncanonical Honey Quality draft and archived plan remain under `docs/reference/` and are **not** part of the canonical figure sequence;
- the chapter plans currently define **760** planned figures in the machine-readable visual-production manifest.

The canonical plan naming gap is closed. The selected Version 1 A_CORE artwork has now been produced and reconciled; lower-priority planned visuals remain source material unless promoted during final layout.

---

## 2. Figure Identification System

Use:

`Figure <chapter>.<sequence>`

Examples:

- `Figure 6.1` — external worker anatomy;
- `Figure 41.6` — adult-bee Varroa wash procedure;
- `Figure 67.1` — traceability chain;
- `Figure 74.1` — future-of-beekeeping systems map.

Rules:

1. figure numbers restart at `.1` for every chapter;
2. a multi-panel figure remains one figure if panels are parts of one teaching concept;
3. a visual reused in another chapter should normally be cross-referenced, not silently duplicated and renumbered;
4. if the reused visual is materially adapted, create a new figure and record its source relationship;
5. reference-section visuals, if any, use `Figure R82.x`, `R83.x`, etc., to avoid collision with chapter figures.

---

## 3. Master Asset Metadata

Every final illustration asset must carry the following metadata in the production register:

| Field | Required content |
|---|---|
| Figure ID | e.g. `41.6` |
| Chapter | Chapter number and title |
| Working title | Human-readable title |
| Visual type | anatomical / process / decision flow / comparison / map / chart / technical cutaway / behavioural |
| Purpose | The decision or concept the figure teaches |
| Final caption | Reader-facing caption |
| Source basis | Chapter text + cited scientific/technical sources |
| Provenance | Original commissioned / generated / adapted / licensed |
| Rights | Commercial print + digital rights status |
| Accuracy reviewer | Person/role/date |
| Accessibility | Alt text / greyscale / colour-independent coding |
| Print size | single-column / double-column / full page |
| Resolution/vector status | production requirement |
| Production status | planned / drafted / technical review / approved / placed |
| Revision ID | final asset version |

No asset is **APPROVED** merely because it looks attractive.

---

## 4. Visual Classes

### 4.1 Anatomical and biological illustrations

Use for:

- external/internal honey bee anatomy;
- queen/drone/worker differences;
- brood stages;
- glands and reproductive structures;
- Varroa/Tropilaelaps/tracheal mites;
- disease cell-level signs;
- pollen, wax, propolis, royal jelly, and venom origins.

**Production requirement:** scientifically accurate proportions, structures, developmental stage, orientation, and scale cues.

### 4.2 Hive and equipment technical illustrations

Use for:

- hive types/components;
- frames/foundation;
- feeders;
- queen-rearing equipment;
- transport ventilation;
- honey extraction/processing;
- wax rendering;
- cold-chain and storage systems.

**Production requirement:** mechanical geometry must be internally consistent. Do not combine dimensions from incompatible hive systems.

### 4.3 Process illustrations

Use for:

- inspections;
- feeding;
- swarm capture;
- splits;
- queen introduction/rearing;
- Varroa sampling;
- disease sampling;
- harvest and extraction;
- creamed-honey production;
- pollen/propolis/royal-jelly handling;
- traceability and recall.

**Production requirement:** safe sequence, explicit stop points, and no off-label treatment or unsafe equipment shortcuts.

### 4.4 Comparative plates

Use when visual comparison improves diagnosis or choice:

- healthy versus abnormal brood;
- AFB versus EFB versus chilled brood/sacbrood;
- Varroa versus Tropilaelaps;
- dry versus wet cappings;
- old versus new comb;
- good versus poor apiary site;
- balanced versus unbalanced extractor.

**Production requirement:** comparison must not imply that appearance alone provides definitive diagnosis where laboratory/inspector confirmation is required.

### 4.5 Maps and landscape illustrations

Use for:

- forage mosaics;
- apiary placement;
- pollinator habitat connectivity;
- dated pest-distribution examples;
- movement/traceability routes.

**Production requirement:** date all distributions that can change, especially exotic/invasive pests. Avoid decorative global maps with unsupported precision.

### 4.6 Charts and data graphics

Use for:

- colony seasonal dynamics;
- scale-hive curves;
- crystallisation;
- time–temperature quality effects;
- IPM decision logic;
- business break-even/capacity concepts;
- evidence hierarchy.

**Production requirement:** axes, units, uncertainty, and conceptual-versus-measured status must be explicit.

---

These visuals have unusually high teaching value and should receive first production priority.

- IPM cycle.

### Honey and products

- nectar-to-honey pathway;
- refractometer sampling/use;

### Business and sustainability

## 7. Consolidation Opportunities

Final layout should reduce needless repetition by reusing or adapting approved visuals.

### Candidate A — Varroa life cycle
Primary figure should be produced once at high technical quality. Chapters 40, 41, 45, and 46 can cross-reference or use simplified derivative panels.

### Candidate B — Healthy brood reference
One anatomically accurate healthy brood plate can support disease chapters, with disease-specific comparison panels built from the same visual system.

### Candidate C — Traceability chain
Chapters 48, 50, 52, 64, 66, 67, and the appendices all use lot-flow concepts. Use one core visual grammar rather than unrelated arrows in every chapter.

### Candidate D — Evidence ladder
Chapter 56 (propolis claims), Chapter 59 (venom), Chapter 73 (research), and Chapter 74 (future technologies) benefit from a shared visual language for “laboratory → field → replicated evidence → guideline/authorisation”.

### Candidate E — Honey quality indicators
Moisture, HMF, diastase, conductivity, crystallisation, and phase separation recur across Chapters 48–53 and the archived Honey Quality reference. Consolidate into a consistent data-graphic family.

### Candidate F — Food-processing zones
Extraction, processing, storage, pollen, royal jelly, and food-safety chapters should share standard clean/incoming/quarantine zone symbols.

---

## 8. Visuals That Should Not Be Combined

Some apparent duplicates teach different questions and should remain distinct:

- **AFB/EFB clinical signs** versus **laboratory sampling workflow**;
- **Varroa monitoring** versus **treatment selection**;
- **comb-honey product defects** versus **beeswax source/contaminant streams**;
- **managed honey-bee forage** versus **wild-pollinator conservation habitat**;
- **food traceability** versus **bee/queen breeding pedigree**.

Consolidation must never remove diagnostic nuance.

---

## 9. Style System

Final illustrations should follow `docs/standards/ILLUSTRATION_STANDARDS.md` and these book-wide conventions:

- clean scientific editorial style rather than cartoon decoration;
- realistic bee, comb, equipment, and plant proportions;
- restrained colour palette suitable for print;
- labels outside critical anatomical detail where possible;
- leader lines with clear endpoints;
- consistent worker/queen/drone iconography;
- consistent healthy/suspect/hazard symbol system;
- no red/green-only coding;
- metric units primary where dimensions are shown;
- scale bars for micro/macro comparisons;
- dashed line = conceptual/hidden route, solid line = physical route or observed sequence;
- arrows require an explicit meaning in the legend.

---

## 10. Accessibility Requirements

Every final figure must:

1. remain understandable in greyscale;
2. avoid relying only on colour hue;
3. use readable labels at final print size;
4. include alt text in EPUB/digital editions;
5. avoid text embedded so small that zoom is required in print;
6. distinguish conceptual diagrams from literal anatomical/engineering views;
7. provide scale or magnification where size can be misunderstood.

---

## 11. Rights and Provenance

Preferred order:

1. original technical illustration commissioned/generated specifically for this handbook;
2. original author-owned photograph/diagram adapted into a figure;
3. openly licensed source compatible with commercial print/digital use;
4. specifically licensed third-party asset with documented permission.

Never rely on “found on the internet”.

For every adapted asset, retain:

- source URL/publication;
- creator;
- licence/permission;
- date obtained;
- permitted uses;
- modifications made;
- attribution text if required.

No font files, proprietary source assets, or unlicensed stock material should be distributed with the book repository.

---

## 12. Accuracy Review Gates

### Gate A — manuscript alignment
Does the figure teach exactly what the final chapter says?

### Gate B — technical review
Are anatomy, equipment, disease signs, units, directions, and processes correct?

### Gate C — safety/legal review
Does the image accidentally show unsafe handling, off-label medicines, contamination, or a legally false claim?

### Gate D — visual proof
Is it readable at final print size and in greyscale?

### Gate E — rights/provenance
Can it legally be published commercially in all intended formats?

Only after all five gates is status changed to **APPROVED FOR LAYOUT**.

---

## 13. Production Status Vocabulary

- `PLANNED` — described in chapter plan only.
- `BRIEF READY` — consolidated art brief approved.
- `DRAFT ART` — first asset produced.
- `TECH REVIEW` — awaiting/under scientific or mechanical review.
- `REVISION` — corrections required.
- `APPROVED` — technically/legally/accessibly approved.
- `PLACED` — inserted into final layout.
- `PROOF VERIFIED` — checked in final PDF/EPUB/print proof.

At the current publishing checkpoint, the rationalised Version 1 **A_CORE** production set is complete. Produced SVGs have been reconciled into PBH-v1 and passed proof-level render/accessibility QA plus editorial spot-check. Remaining statuses are principally **PLACED** and **PROOF VERIFIED** during final layout/export.

---
