# Reference 83 — Diagrams

## Reference 83 — Diagrams

## Status

**SELECTED V1 DIAGRAM PRODUCTION COMPLETE — READY FOR LAYOUT PROOF**

This register defines the book-wide system for process flows, biological timelines, decision trees, data graphics, maps, technical cutaways, comparison matrices, and research/evidence diagrams. The selected Version 1 production set has now been produced and reconciled into the PBH-v1 visual language.

Issue #101 remains open because diagram placement, page-context review and final PDF/EPUB/print proof are still required.

---

## 1. Why Diagrams Need Their Own Register

A photograph can show what something looked like. An illustration can show anatomy or equipment clearly. A diagram answers a different question: **how are things related, what happens next, what decision follows, or how does a variable change?**

The handbook uses diagrams for:

- biological development;
- seasonal dynamics;
- disease/parasite life cycles;
- diagnostic reasoning;
- management decision paths;
- food-processing workflows;
- traceability;
- business economics;
- habitat networks;
- evidence appraisal;
- technology adoption.

A consistent diagram system reduces cognitive load and prevents contradictory visual conventions between chapters.

---

## 2. Diagram Classes

### D1 — Timeline
Use for development, queen rearing, seasonal events, treatment windows, product preservation, or historical sequences.

**Standard:** time runs left to right unless a circular annual cycle is clearly superior.

### D2 — Decision tree
Use when the reader observes a condition and must choose among next steps.

**Standard:** start condition at top; yes/no or named branches; stop/seek-authority states visually distinct.

### D3 — Process flow
Use for defined operations such as extraction, traceability, sampling, cleaning, or recall.

**Standard:** physical product/material movement uses solid arrows; information/record flow uses dashed arrows.

### D4 — Life cycle
Use for honey bee development, Varroa/Tropilaelaps, pathogens, and pests.

**Standard:** life stages visually distinct and biologically ordered; host stage shown explicitly where relevant.

### D5 — Causal pathway
Use for stress interactions such as Varroa → virus → shortened worker lifespan → colony decline.

**Standard:** arrows mean directional mechanism/effect only when supported; correlation is shown differently.

### D6 — Comparison matrix
Use for hive types, monitoring methods, disease differentials, equipment, insurance, product preservation, or technology choices.

### D7 — Technical cutaway
Use for hive airflow, tracheal mites, extraction equipment, tank layers, comb cells, or processing systems.

### D8 — Landscape/map diagram
Use for forage, apiary positioning, habitat connectivity, movement, or dated pest distribution.

### D9 — Data/chart diagram
Use for scale-hive curves, population cycles, time–temperature quality change, costs, or experimental results.

### D10 — Evidence/system model
Use for HACCP, IPM, research hierarchy, legal/compliance matrix, or future-scenario systems.

---

## 3. Arrow and Line Conventions

| Visual convention | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Solid arrow | Physical transfer, biological progression, or defined process step |
| Dashed arrow | Information flow, indirect influence, possible route, or record linkage |
| Double-headed arrow | Interaction or reversible exchange; use sparingly |
| Dotted boundary | Conceptual zone, uncertain boundary, or optional system scope |
| Solid boundary | Physical container, hive, defined legal/process unit |
| Red/triangle hazard symbol | Hazard/stop point; must also have text or shape so colour is not required |
| Shield/check symbol | Verified control, not merely “safe” |
| Question diamond | Decision point |
| Rounded terminal | Start/end/stop/escalate state |

No arrow should exist merely because the graphic “looks dynamic”.

---

## 4. Colour and Accessibility

Colour is secondary to structure.

Recommended semantic families:

- colony/bee biology — warm neutral tones;
- food/product stream — amber/gold family;
- records/data — cool neutral family;
- hazards — high-contrast warning treatment plus symbol/label;
- regulated/authority action — distinct outline and explicit `CHECK CURRENT LOCAL REQUIREMENTS` label;
- environmental habitat — natural green/blue family, still differentiated by pattern/label.

Requirements:

- all diagrams must work in greyscale;
- no red/green-only pass/fail coding;
- font size tested at final print dimensions;
- line weights survive PDF downsampling;
- alt text summarises relationships, not merely colours.

---

## 5. Units and Quantities

- SI/metric units are primary.
- Temperature: °C, with °F only where a regional edition explicitly requires it.
- Mass: g, kg.
- Volume: mL, L.
- Distance: mm, cm, m, km.
- Concentration/quality values: always include units.
- Time: minutes, hours, days, weeks; biological “day X” starts must be defined.
- Mite infestation: state the method and denominator, e.g. mites per 100 adult bees.

A number without a method or unit should never be placed in a scientific diagram.

---

## 6. Uncertainty Conventions

When evidence is approximate or context-dependent:

- use ranges instead of fake precision;
- label curves `conceptual` when not derived from a specific dataset;
- use shaded bands for natural variation;
- date pest-distribution maps;
- mark region-specific thresholds as examples, not universal constants;
- use `possible` rather than `diagnostic` for non-specific signs;
- use `association` instead of causal arrows when causation is not established.

---

## 7. Book-Wide Core Diagrams

### Diagram family A — Honey bee development
Primary chapters: 7, 8, 9, 10.

Core outputs:
- caste development timeline;
- egg–larva–pupa–adult life cycle;
- worker task progression with plasticity;
- queen maturation/mating/laying timeline.

Reuse rule: one common time scale and visual stage vocabulary.

### Diagram family B — Colony annual system
Primary chapters: 13, 14, 34, 35.

Core outputs:
- adult population, brood, stores, and forage conceptual cycle;
- climate-region comparison;
- management overlays by biological stage rather than month.

### Diagram family C — Swarming and reproduction
Primary chapters: 28, 29, 30, 32.

Core outputs:
- swarm-development cues;
- swarm capture decision path;
- split sequence;
- queen-cell development and mating-nuc pathway.

### Diagram family D — Bee-health diagnosis
Primary chapters: 38–46.

Core outputs:
- emergency triage;
- brood-disease differential;
- adult-bee differential;
- sample/confirmation pathway;
- Varroa life cycle;
- Tropilaelaps life cycle;
- Nosema life cycle;
- virus/Varroa causal pathway;
- IPM cycle.

### Diagram family E — Honey flow and production
Primary chapters: 47–54.

Core outputs:
- flower → nectar → colony intake → ripening → harvested honey;
- scale-hive daily mass curve;
- harvest decision flow;
- extraction workflow;
- time–temperature quality model;
- crystallisation/nucleation model;
- comb-honey workflow;
- wax-rendering separation.

### Diagram family F — Other hive products
Primary chapters: 55–59.

Core outputs:
- wax source streams;
- pollen collection/preservation chain;
- propolis collection/extraction branches;
- royal-jelly queen-cell/cold-chain process;
- venom collection/quality/safety system.

### Diagram family G — Business and food control
Primary chapters: 60–69.

Core outputs:
- business cash/capacity model;
- equipment TCO decision;
- marketing funnel;
- online order-to-lot path;
- farmers-market food flow;
- HACCP/control system;
- one-step-back/internal/one-step-forward traceability;
- compliance matrix;
- insurance risk-transfer map.

### Diagram family H — Sustainability and evidence
Primary chapters: 70–74.

Core outputs:
- organic certification system;
- biodiversity levels;
- pollinator habitat connectivity;
- evidence hierarchy;
- paper appraisal flow;
- technology adoption decision;
- scenario planning rather than deterministic forecast.

---

## 8. Reuse and Consolidation Rules

### Reuse directly
Use the same approved diagram when the teaching question is identical. Add a chapter cross-reference rather than redraw.

Good candidates:
- Varroa life cycle;
- colony traceability chain;
- evidence hierarchy;
- honey time–temperature quality concept;
- annual colony cycle.

### Adapt from a master
Use a common master diagram with highlighted subpaths when chapters focus on different parts.

Example:
- master `hive product traceability` diagram;
- Chapter 48 highlights harvest;
- Chapter 50 highlights blending;
- Chapter 67 highlights recall.

### Do not consolidate
Keep separate when simplification would remove important differences.

Examples:
- AFB and EFB disease progression;
- Varroa versus Tropilaelaps reproductive biology;
- food-safety recall versus animal-disease tracing;
- pollen cold chain versus royal-jelly cold chain.

---

## 9. Decision-Tree Safety Rules

Every decision tree involving health, chemicals, food, or emergencies must include explicit stop/escalation nodes where relevant.

Examples:

- suspected notifiable disease → **STOP MOVEMENT / CONTACT COMPETENT AUTHORITY**;
- severe sting reaction → **EMERGENCY MEDICAL RESPONSE**;
- uncertain chemical contamination → **SEGREGATE PRODUCT / DO NOT BLEND**;
- violent extractor imbalance → **STOP / ISOLATE POWER / WAIT FOR COMPLETE STOP**;
- treatment failure → **DIAGNOSE FAILURE / DO NOT INCREASE DOSE OFF-LABEL**.

---

## 10. Maps

Maps require a source/date field.

### Stable maps
Can show:
- countries/regions;
- broad climatic context;
- general historical development.

### Dynamic maps
Require explicit publication date/source:
- Tropilaelaps distribution;
- small hive beetle regulated zones;
- disease outbreaks;
- pesticide restrictions;
- organic-certification boundaries if mapped.

Do not freeze a 2026 pest-distribution map into future editions without re-verification.

---

## 11. Data Charts

A chart must state whether it is:

- measured data from a cited study;
- an illustrative worked example;
- a conceptual relationship;
- a simulated scenario.

Measured charts require:
- source;
- sample/population;
- units;
- time range;
- uncertainty where applicable.

Conceptual charts must not use pseudo-precise values that make them appear empirical.

---

## 12. Diagram Production Register Fields

| Field | Requirement |
|---|---|
| Diagram ID | chapter.sequence |
| Class | D1–D10 |
| Chapter | location |
| Question answered | one sentence |
| Inputs/source | scientific or operational basis |
| Nodes/stages | defined list |
| Arrow meaning | explicit |
| Units | where applicable |
| Uncertainty | range/notes |
| Jurisdiction/date | if applicable |
| Reuse master | yes/no + source ID |
| Accessibility | alt text/greyscale |
| Technical reviewer | role/date |
| Status | planned → proof verified |

---

## 13. Diagram Quality Audit

Reject or revise a diagram if it:

- has decorative arrows with no defined meaning;
- uses icons without labels for technical concepts;
- makes one diagnostic sign equal one disease;
- contains a universal threshold not supported by the text;
- uses wrong hive geometry;
- implies treatment reaches capped mites when it does not;
- confuses honey moisture with water activity;
- shows traceability without internal lot links;
- equates managed honey bee abundance with biodiversity;
- turns a model prediction into certainty;
- cannot be read when printed in greyscale.

---

## 14. Completion Criteria for Reference 83 / Issue #101

- [x] master diagram grammar defined;
- [x] major diagram families identified;
- [x] reuse/consolidation rules defined;
- [x] safety/uncertainty conventions defined;
- [x] final Version 1 production selection reconciled against the chapter-plan inventory;
- [x] selected A_CORE diagram/illustration set produced (**230 / 230** across the complete visual programme);
- [x] technical/scientific review recorded across the production waves;
- [x] SVG accessibility metadata and source-level legibility checks complete;
- [x] full-book PBH-v1 reconciliation and editorial spot-check complete;
- [ ] diagrams placed in final layout;
- [ ] PDF/EPUB/print proofs verified.

**Current state: READY_FOR_LAYOUT_PROOF. Issue #101 remains open only for placement and final-format proof gates.**

## 1. Photograph Selection Rules

Use a photograph when the reader benefits from seeing:

- authentic surface texture or colour;
- natural variation among colonies/products/sites;
- a real disease or pest presentation;
- a field setup whose context matters;
- actual wear/damage/contamination;
- a practical body position or handling setup;
- product grading appearance;
- landscape/habitat conditions.

Prefer illustration/diagram instead when:

- anatomy must be labelled internally;
- a process must show hidden stages;
- exact geometry matters;
- several alternative states need controlled comparison;
- a dangerous procedure would be unsafe to stage;
- a photograph would be cluttered or ambiguous.

---

## 2. Photograph Metadata

Every final photograph must have:

| Field | Requirement |
|---|---|
| Photo ID | `Photo <chapter>.<sequence>` |
| Chapter | placement |
| Subject | precise description |
| View | macro / close-up / medium / wide / overhead |
| Required context | scale, hive part, environment, comparison reference |
| Safety conditions | PPE / traffic / chemical / electrical / allergy controls |
| Caption purpose | what the reader should learn |
| Capture/source | original / licensed / institutional |
| Creator | photographer/source |
| Date | capture or source date |
| Location | only when relevant and publishable |
| Consent | people/property where required |
| Rights | commercial print/digital permission |
| Editing | crop/exposure only; no deceptive content manipulation |
| Technical reviewer | role/date |
| Status | planned / sourced / reviewed / approved / placed |

---

## 3. Core Shot List — Foundations and Biology

### Photo 1.1 — Working Apiary Context
Wide but informative image showing a small well-managed apiary with safe stands, entrances, working space, and surrounding forage.

### Photo 3.1 — Honey Bee on Flower
Macro showing real pollen contact with floral reproductive structures, not merely a bee sitting on petals.

### Photo 6.1 — Worker Macro
High-resolution dorsal/side worker view for external morphology context; technical labels remain in the illustration plate.

### Photo 8.1 — Queen Among Workers
Clearly identifiable queen on comb, with worker comparison and no digital enlargement that misrepresents relative size.

### Photo 9.1 — Worker Carrying Pollen
Macro of corbicular pollen pellet on returning forager.

### Photo 10.1 — Drone and Worker on Comb
Real size/appearance comparison.

### Photo 12.1 — Fanning at Entrance
Workers aligned at hive entrance in ventilation/scenting context.

### Photo 12.2 — Grooming Behaviour
Close-up if a technically clear original image can be captured.

### Photo 13.1 — Healthy Brood-Nest Frame
Whole-frame view with brood, pollen, and stores visible; used to teach “normal” before disease images.

### Photo 14.1 — Winter Cluster Context
Only if obtainable without harmful hive opening; prefer an observation hive/thermal-safe or already exposed educational context. Do not disturb a colony merely to make the photograph.

---

## 4. Apiary Setup and Equipment

### Photo 15.1 — Good Apiary Site
Shows drainage, stable stands, access, safe flight paths, wind shelter, and vegetation context.

### Photo 15.2 — Poor Drainage Example
Real waterlogged/flood-prone placement photographed safely and without implying the example colony should remain there.

### Photo 17.1 — Representative Hive Systems
Controlled side-by-side equipment photograph with scale reference; technical geometry remains diagrammatic.

### Photo 18.1 — Hive Component Set
Clean exploded equipment laid out in order.

### Photo 19.1 — Fresh Foundationless Comb
Straight new comb with visible edge attachment, photographed vertically.

### Photo 19.2 — Old Brood Comb
Darkened comb showing cocoon accumulation for comparison with new honey comb.

### Photo 20.1 — Essential Inspection Kit
Only equipment genuinely needed for a normal inspection; avoid commercial product clutter.

### Photo 21.1 — Correct Protective Clothing Fit
Veil clearance, closed zips, cuffs, footwear; model consent required.

### Photo 22.1 — Safe Smoker Condition
Well-maintained smoker, bellows, fuel and fire-safe placement; do not stage unsafe flames.

### Photo 22.2 — Hive Tools
Clean representative tools with wear/damage comparison if useful.

### Photo 23.1 — First-Aid / Emergency Preparedness Kit
Clearly labelled and contextually safe; avoid implying contents replace emergency medical care.

### Photo 29.2 — Swarm Boxed Safely
Captured bees entering a hive/nuc; no risky ladder staging.

### Photo 30.1 — Newly Made Split
Real nucleus/split setup with appropriate frames and entrance.

Clear photo of cage position and attendant context; exact procedure remains in text/diagram.

Well-developed queen cells on a grafting bar, with scale.

Straps, screens/ventilation, stable load. Vehicle stationary during photography.

Notebook/device example with no private customer/location data visible.

### Photo 38.2 — Storm-Damaged Hive

## 6. Bee Health — High Diagnostic Value

Disease photographs must have known provenance and, where diagnosis is claimed, inspector/laboratory confirmation whenever practical.

### Photo 39.1 — Healthy Larvae Reference
Pearly white C-shaped larvae at several ages.

### Photo 39.2 — Chalkbrood Mummies
White/grey/dark mummies in cells or on hive floor.

### Photo 39.3 — Sacbrood Example
Confirmed or well-documented sac-like larva; avoid relying on unverified internet imagery.

### Photo 40.1 — Adult Female Varroa on Bee
Macro with realistic scale.

### Photo 40.2 — Tropilaelaps Specimen
Use only an authoritative/rights-cleared confirmed specimen; label magnification.

### Photo 41.1 — Varroa Wash Equipment and Result
Actual sample container and recovered mites; queen absent.

### Photo 41.2 — Deformed-Wing Bee
Clear phenotype with colony context; caption states phenotype is not a quantitative mite count.

### Photo 42.1 — Nosema-Like Spores Under Microscope
Laboratory image with magnification and method; do not claim species ID from morphology alone.

### Photo 43.1 — AFB Brood Frame
Confirmed case showing patchy sealed brood and suspicious cappings.

### Photo 43.2 — AFB Adherent Scale
Macro confirmed/provenance-controlled.

### Photo 44.1 — EFB Larvae
Confirmed or authoritative image showing abnormal larval colour/position.

### Photo 45.1 — CBPV-Type Adult Signs
Only if diagnosis/provenance is strong; avoid presenting one dark bee as confirmation.

### Photo 46.1 — Monitoring in Practice
Beekeeper performing a validated mite sample with PPE and record sheet.

**Rights priority for diagnostic photos:** original inspector/laboratory collaboration or clearly licensed institutional image; avoid generic stock libraries.

## 9. Business, Food Safety, and Traceability

### Photo 60.1 — Small Commercial Beekeeping Work Area
Shows scale-up reality: hive parts, storage, clean zones, transport—not a staged lifestyle image.

### Photo 61.1 — Capacity Bottleneck Example
Extractor/uncapping/tank setup demonstrating throughput constraint.

### Photo 62.1 — Honey Retail Shelf
Use an original controlled setup to demonstrate brand hierarchy, label readability, and pack sizes; avoid showcasing third-party brands without need.

### Photo 63.1 — Brand Packaging System
Author-owned mock/final labels across product range after legal review.

### Photo 64.1 — Shipping Pack Test
Jar protection, leak containment, carton, lot code and parcel label with personal data obscured.

### Photo 65.1 — Farmers’ Market Stall
Clean, weather-protected, price-visible, traceable display; people shown only with consent.

### Photo 66.1 — Clean Honey-Room Zone
Hand-wash, covered tanks, clean tools and chemical segregation.

### Photo 66.2 — Deliberate Hygiene Failure Training Plate
Controlled examples such as wet tank, dirty hose end, unlabelled chemical, glass breakage hazard—staged without contaminating real food.

### Photo 67.1 — Physical Lot Labels
Tank, drum, jar case and retained sample carrying linked identifiers.

### Photo 68.1 — Compliance Files
Organised digital/paper records without exposing personal or confidential information.

### Photo 69.1 — Asset/Insurance Documentation
Photographing hive equipment/vehicle/processing assets for claims evidence; no insurance-brand promotion.

---

## 10. Sustainability, Biodiversity, and Research

### Photo 70.1 — Organic Apiary Landscape Context
If a certified organic operation is photographed, certification/permission must be verified; otherwise caption as illustrative landscape, not certified status.

### Photo 71.1 — Flower-Rich Habitat Mosaic
Hedgerow, meadow, bare ground, dead stems/wood and connected habitat elements.

### Photo 71.2 — Wild Bee, Hoverfly, Butterfly/Moth Examples
High-quality correctly identified representatives; do not label every flower visitor “bee”.

### Photo 72.1 — Pollinator-Friendly Mowing Contrast
Same habitat type under intensive mowing versus rotational/late-cut management if a valid comparable site exists.

### Photo 72.2 — Ground-Nesting Habitat
Bare/sparsely vegetated sunny soil with scale and context.

### Photo 73.1 — Field Experiment Setup
Colonies with clearly labelled treatment/control groups or sensor research setup; research permission required.

### Photo 73.2 — Laboratory Diagnostic Context
PCR/microscopy/sampling bench photographed without implying a specific test is simple home practice.

### Photo 74.1 — Precision Apiary
Scale/sensors/remote monitoring on a real hive with conventional observation still evident.

### Photo 74.2 — Future Pest Surveillance
Authoritative surveillance/sample identification context for emerging pests rather than speculative science-fiction imagery.

---

## 11. People, Consent, and Privacy

When identifiable people appear:

- obtain model consent appropriate to commercial publication;
- avoid identifiable minors unless essential and properly authorised;
- obscure private vehicle plates, addresses, phone numbers, order labels, customer records, and health information;
- avoid publishing precise coordinates of valuable/remote apiaries when theft/biosecurity risk exists;
- do not imply endorsement by a person or organisation without permission.

---

## 12. Property and Location Permissions

Photographs on farms, laboratories, food premises, businesses, protected sites, or private land require appropriate permission where necessary.

For regulated/certified operations, captions must not imply official approval beyond the actual status documented.

---

## 13. Editing Rules

Allowed routine edits:

- exposure/white balance;
- crop/straighten;
- dust-spot removal that is not diagnostic content;
- background distraction reduction where it does not alter evidence;
- privacy redaction;
- annotation overlays kept as separate production layers.

Not allowed:

- adding/removing disease signs;
- moving mites or eggs;
- making brood patterns more dramatic;
- changing honey colour to fit a varietal claim;
- removing contamination in a photograph used to teach contamination;
- compositing separate events into one “documentary” photo without disclosure.

Diagnostic photos are evidence-like teaching assets and must preserve reality.

---

## 14. Technical Image Requirements

For final print photographs:

- source resolution sufficient for intended print dimensions, normally targeting effective ~300 ppi at placement size;
- retain original high-resolution master;
- use non-destructive editing workflow;
- colour-manage consistently during layout;
- avoid aggressive sharpening/compression;
- test dark brood and microscope images in print proof;
- retain crop flexibility for EPUB/print variants.

Do not distribute proprietary font files or unlicensed source files with the repository.

---

## 15. Caption Rules

A strong caption answers:

- what is visible;
- why it matters;
- what the reader should **not** conclude if the sign is non-specific.

Example:

> Deformed wings on a newly emerged worker are strongly associated with Varroa–DWV damage in many colonies, but the visible phenotype does not quantify current Varroa infestation. Perform validated mite monitoring.

Avoid captions such as “Sick bee” or “Healthy hive” with no diagnostic value.

---

## 16. Rights Priority

Preferred sources:

1. original project photography;
2. commissioned photography with full commercial rights;
3. government/university/institutional imagery with explicit compatible licence/permission;
4. commercial licensing only when unique material cannot reasonably be created.

For every non-original image record licence, attribution, modifications, and permitted formats/territories.

---

- [x] shot list exists;
- [x] purpose and provenance fields defined;
- [x] diagnostic-photo verification rules defined;
- [x] consent/privacy/property rules defined;
- [ ] final photographs sourced/captured;
- [ ] rights cleared;
- [ ] diagnostic identifications verified;
- [ ] high-resolution masters archived;
- [ ] captions and alt text complete;
- [ ] final layout placement approved;
- [ ] print/EPUB/PDF proofs verified.

**Issue #102 remains open until the final photographic assets are complete.**

# Reference 85 — Index

## Reference 85 — Index

## Status

**PRE-LAYOUT SEMANTIC INDEX — PAGE NUMBERS PENDING FINAL PAGINATION**

This index establishes the controlled vocabulary, synonyms, cross-references, and chapter/section locators for the handbook before typesetting. Page numbers must not be invented before layout. During final print production, chapter locators will be converted or supplemented with verified page locators.

Issue #103 remains open until final pagination and index proofing are complete.

Locator format used here: `Ch. N` or `Ref. N`.

---

## A

**absconding** — causes and emergency management, Ch. 38; distinction from swarming, Ch. 28

**acarapisosis** — see **tracheal mite**

**acaricides** — Varroa controls, Ch. 41; resistance, Ch. 41, Ch. 46; residues/authorisation, Ch. 66, Ch. 68

**active ingredient** — treatment choice and resistance, Ch. 41, Ch. 46; treatment records, Ch. 66, App. F

**adult-bee disease** — differential diagnosis, Ch. 39, Ch. 45; sudden mortality, Ch. 38

# Cross-Reference Vocabulary

Use the following preferred headings in the final index:

- **American foulbrood**, not separate independent headings `AFB disease` and `foulbrood, American`; create `AFB — see American foulbrood`.
- **European foulbrood**, with `EFB — see European foulbrood`.
- **Varroa destructor**, with `Varroa — see Varroa destructor` if the print index software requires a preferred form.
- **Nosema / Vairimorpha**, with both nomenclatures cross-referenced.
- **small hive beetle**, with scientific name *Aethina tumida* as a cross-reference.
- **tracheal mite**, with *Acarapis woodi* cross-reference.
- **Tropilaelaps**, including *T. mercedesae* under the main heading.
- **honey moisture** and **water activity** remain separate headings.
- **organic beekeeping** remains separate from **treatment-free beekeeping**.
- **pollinator conservation** remains separate from **honey bee management**.
- **hive** and **colony** remain separate where the physical/living distinction matters.

# Final Pagination Workflow

After the print manuscript is fully typeset:

1. freeze content sufficiently that page flow is stable;
2. export chapter/section-to-page mapping;
3. replace/add page locators for major index entries;
4. create subentries where one term appears across many pages;
5. verify `see` and `see also` references;
6. remove trivial mentions that do not teach anything;
7. verify scientific names and spelling;
8. proof index against PDF page numbers, not editor-view pages;
9. re-check after any late layout change;
10. keep the semantic chapter locators in the source file for EPUB/digital navigation if useful.

## Completion Criteria for Reference 85 / Issue #103

- [x] controlled vocabulary established;
- [x] major `see`/`see also` relationships established;
- [x] pre-layout chapter locators created;
- [ ] final page locators generated;
- [ ] subentries balanced for high-frequency topics;
- [ ] index proofed after final layout;
- [ ] print and digital navigation verified.

**Issue #103 remains open until final pagination is complete.**
